“I mustn’t say, ma’am,” Margaret replied, “but I know it well. And I know where he is too.”

“Ah, yes—surely at Norland, his family home,” Mrs. Jennings guessed. “Perhaps he’s the curate?”

“No,” Margaret said firmly. “He’s not in any profession.”

Marianne interrupted again, her voice rising. “Margaret, you know this is all nonsense. There is no such person.”

“Well then,” Margaret retorted, “he must be dead, because I’m certain there *was* such a man once—and his name begins with an F.”

Just then, Lady Middleton remarked, “It’s raining very hard.” Though Elinor suspected it was less out of concern for her and more due to Lady Middleton’s distaste for such idle talk, the comment was a welcome interruption. Colonel Brandon, ever thoughtful, seized the opportunity to join in, and soon the conversation turned entirely to the weather.

Willoughby opened the piano and invited Marianne to play. With music filling the air and several guests shifting focus, the earlier tension faded.

Yet Elinor remained unsettled. Her thoughts lingered on the name beginning with F—on the man who might never exist, yet whose memory carried such weight.

That evening, plans were made for a trip the next day to visit a magnificent estate twelve miles from Barton, belonging to Colonel Brandon’s brother-in-law. Without his influence, access was denied, as the owner—then abroad—had strictly forbidden visitors. The grounds were said to be breathtaking, with a large lake perfect for sailing. Cold food would be packed, carriages would be open, and everything arranged in the style of a grand pleasure party.

Sir John, who had led such excursions every summer for a decade, spoke passionately of the beauty. Still, some questioned the wisdom of the outing—after all, it had rained daily for two weeks, and the season was uncertain. Mrs. Dashwood, already unwell with a cold, agreed with Elinor to stay behind.

And so, as the group prepared to depart, Elinor stood apart, watching her sister’s light step and the quiet confidence in her face—knowing that beneath the surface, a future was unfolding, shaped not by reason, but by passion.

The planned outing to Whitwell turned out far differently than Elinor had anticipated. She had braced herself for damp clothes, exhaustion, and perhaps even a touch of fear—but what actually happened was worse: they didn’t go at all. By ten o’clock, the entire party had gathered at the park, ready to enjoy breakfast before setting off. Though rain had fallen through the night, the morning offered some relief—the clouds were breaking apart, and the sun peeked through now and then. Everyone was in high spirits, eager to make the most of the day, determined to endure any discomfort rather than miss this rare opportunity for joy.

As they sat down to eat, the letters arrived. Among them was one addressed to Colonel Brandon. He took it, glanced at the handwriting, paled slightly, and immediately excused himself from the table.

“What’s wrong with Brandon?” Sir John asked, puzzled.
No one could say.
“I hope he hasn’t received bad news,” Lady Middleton murmured.
“It must be something extraordinary,” Sir John added, “to make Colonel Brandon leave my breakfast table so abruptly.”

After a few minutes, Brandon returned.
“No bad news, Colonel?” Mrs. Jennings asked as soon as he stepped back into the room.
“None at all, ma'am, I thank you.”
“Was it from Avignon? I hope your sister isn’t worse?”
“No, ma'am. It came from town—it’s only business.”
“But why did you turn so pale? If it’s just business, why such a reaction?” she pressed. “Come now, Colonel—let us hear the truth.”

“Dear madam,” Lady Middleton interjected gently, “do remember your manners.”

But Mrs. Jennings paid no heed. “Perhaps it’s to tell you that your cousin Fanny is married?” she guessed.
“No indeed,” Brandon replied.
“Well, then, I know who it’s from,” she declared. “And I hope she’s well.”

“Who do you mean, ma'am?” he asked, his cheeks warming slightly.
“Oh! You know exactly who I mean.”

“I am truly sorry,” he said, turning to Lady Middleton, “that I should receive this letter today. It concerns matters that require my immediate presence in London.”

“In London!” Mrs. Jennings exclaimed. “What on earth could you possibly need there at this time of year?”

“My loss is great,” he continued, “in having to leave such a pleasant gathering. But I’m especially regretful, because I fear my absence may prevent you from gaining admittance to Whitwell.”

It was a devastating blow. The disappointment was palpable.

“But if you write a note to the housekeeper,” Marianne suggested eagerly, “wouldn’t that suffice?”

Brandon shook his head.
“We must go,” Sir John insisted. “We’re too close now to give up. You can’t possibly leave until tomorrow, Brandon—that’s all.”

“I wish it were so simple,” Brandon replied. “But I cannot delay my journey by even a single day.”

“If you’d only let us know what your business is,” Mrs. Jennings urged, “we might see if it could be postponed.”

“You wouldn’t be six hours later,” Willoughby said calmly, “if you waited until we returned.”

“I cannot afford to lose *one* hour,” Brandon repeated firmly.

At that moment, Elinor overheard Willoughby whisper to Marianne, “There are some people who simply can’t stand a good time. Brandon is one of them. He probably just wanted to avoid getting cold—and made up this excuse to get out of it. I’d bet fifty guineas that he wrote the letter himself.”

“I have no doubt of it,” Marianne replied without hesitation.

“Brandon,” Sir John said with a sigh, “you’ve always been stubborn when you set your mind to something. But still, think about it—here are the two Miss Careys come all the way from Newton, the three Miss Dashwoods walked up from the cottage, and Mr. Willoughby rose two hours earlier than usual just to join us at Whitwell.”

Brandon once again expressed his sorrow at causing such dismay, but stood firm in his decision.

“And when will you return?” Lady Middleton asked.
“I hope to see you at Barton soon,” she added, “as soon as you can conveniently leave town. We’ll have to postpone our visit to Whitwell until then.”

“You are very kind,” Brandon replied. “But I cannot promise when I might return—it’s uncertain.”

“Oh, he *must* and *shall* come back!” Sir John declared. “If he’s not here by the end of the week, I’ll go after him myself.”

“Do go, Sir John,” Mrs. Jennings chimed in. “Then maybe you’ll discover what his real business is.”

“I don’t want to pry into other men’s affairs,” Sir John said. “I suppose it’s something he’s ashamed of.”

Just then, the announcement came that Colonel Brandon’s horses were ready.
“You’re not riding all the way to town, are you?” Sir John asked.
“No. Only to Honiton. From there, I’ll take the post-chaise.”

“Well, since you’re resolved to go,” Sir John said, “I wish you a safe journey. But you really ought to reconsider.”

“I assure you,” Brandon said, “it’s beyond my control.”

He then bade farewell to everyone. Turning to Elinor, he said, “Is there any chance I might see you and your sisters in town this winter?”
“I’m afraid not,” she replied.
“Then I must bid you farewell for longer than I would like,” he said quietly.

To Marianne, he merely bowed and said nothing.

“Come, Colonel,” Mrs. Jennings called after him, “before you go—do tell us what this urgent matter is.”

He wished her a good morning and, escorted by Sir John, left the room.

Now, with no restraint of politeness, the full weight of their disappointment poured forth. Every person at the table lamented the ruined plan, repeating over and over how unjust it was to be denied such a day.

“I can guess what his business is,” Mrs. Jennings announced triumphantly.
“Can you, ma'am?” several voices asked.
“Yes,” she declared. “It’s about Miss Williams—I’m certain of it.”

“And who is Miss Williams?” Marianne asked.
“What! Don’t you know who Miss Williams is? You must have heard of her. She’s a relation of the Colonel’s—a very close one. We won’t say just how close, for fear of shocking the young ladies.”

She lowered her voice slightly and leaned toward Elinor. “She’s his natural daughter.”

“Indeed!” Elinor murmured.
“Oh, yes,” Mrs. Jennings confirmed. “And as like him as she can stare. I daresay the Colonel will leave her everything.”

When Sir John returned, he joined in the general regret but quickly shifted the mood. “Since we’re all together,” he said, “we must find some way to enjoy ourselves. Let’s not waste the day.” After brief discussion, they decided that while happiness couldn’t be found at Whitwell, they could at least seek comfort in a country drive.

The carriages were summoned. Willoughby’s curricle was first to depart, and Marianne looked more radiant than ever as she climbed inside. He drove swiftly through the park, and soon they vanished from sight. Not until much later did they return—after all the others had already come back.

Both seemed thoroughly delighted with their ride, though they spoke only in vague terms about keeping to the lanes while the others explored the downs.

That evening, a dance was arranged, and everyone vowed to be exceptionally merry. More of the Carey family arrived for dinner, swelling the company to nearly twenty—a number Sir John found immensely satisfying.

Willoughby took his usual seat between the two elder Miss Dashwoods. Mrs. Jennings sat beside Elinor, and not long after they were seated, she leaned behind them and addressed Marianne loudly enough for both sisters to hear:
“I’ve found you out, despite all your tricks. I know exactly where you spent the morning.”

Marianne flushed and replied sharply, “Where, pray?”

“Didn’t you know,” Willoughby said coolly, “that we went out in my curricle?”

“Yes, yes, Mr. Impudence,” Mrs. Jennings snapped. “I know that well enough. But I was determined to learn *where* you went. I hope you’re enjoying your house, Miss Marianne. It’s quite large, I understand. And when I visit you, I hope you’ll have furnished it properly—because it certainly needed it when I was there six years ago.”

Marianne turned away, deeply embarrassed. Mrs. Jennings laughed heartily.

Later, Elinor discovered that Mrs. Jennings had sent her maid to question Willoughby’s groom—and learned that they had gone to Allenham, spending a considerable time walking through the garden and touring the house.

Elinor found this hard to believe. It seemed unlikely that Willoughby would suggest entering the house while Mrs. Smith was still living there—someone Marianne barely knew.

As soon as they left the dining room, Elinor questioned her sister. Her surprise was immense when Marianne confirmed every detail.

“You doubted me?” Marianne said, annoyed.
“Why should I imagine we didn’t go there—or that we didn’t see the house? Isn’t that something you’ve often wanted to do yourself?”

“Yes,” Elinor admitted, “but I wouldn’t go while Mrs. Smith was there—especially with no one but Mr. Willoughby.”

“But Mr. Willoughby is the only one who has the right to show the house,” Marianne argued. “And since he traveled in an open carriage, we couldn perfection had another companion. I never spent a more delightful morning in my life.”

“I’m afraid,” Elinor replied gently, “that pleasure doesn’t always prove propriety.”

“On the contrary,” Marianne insisted, “nothing could better prove it. If I had done anything truly wrong, I would have felt it at the time—we always know when we’re acting improperly. And if I truly believed I was doing something wrong, I could not have enjoyed it.”

“But,” Elinor said, “since it’s already brought you such impertinent remarks, don’t you now question your own judgment?”

“If Mrs. Jennings’ opinions are to define what’s proper,” Marianne retorted, “then we’re all guilty every minute. I care not for her approval—or her disapproval. I feel no guilt for walking through Mrs. Smith’s grounds or seeing her house. One day, they’ll belong to Mr. Willoughby—and—”

“If they were to become yours someday, Marianne,” Elinor interrupted softly, “would you still consider your actions justified?”

Marianne blushed—but there was a flicker of satisfaction in her eyes. After a pause, she returned to her sister, smiling brightly.
“Perhaps,” she said, “it *was* rather unwise of me to go to Allenham. But Mr. Willoughby was eager to show me the place—and it is truly charming. There’s one remarkable sitting room upstairs—just the right size for daily use, and with modern furnishings, it would be perfect. It’s a corner room with windows on two sides. On one side, you look across the bowling green, behind the house, to a lovely hanging wood. On the other, you see the church and village, and beyond them, those magnificent hills we’ve admired so often. I didn’t see it at its best—the furniture was dreadful—but if it were refurnished—say, two hundred pounds, Willoughby says—it could be one of the most delightful summer rooms in England.”

If Elinor had been able to listen without interruption, Marianne would have described every room in the house with equal enthusiasm.

Colonel Brandon’s abrupt departure from the park, and his quiet refusal to explain why, stirred Mrs. Jennings into a state of constant speculation that lasted for days. She was naturally inclined to wonder—indeed, she thrived on it—especially when it came to the comings and goings of her acquaintances. Now, with Colonel Brandon gone so suddenly, her mind raced through every possible explanation. He had looked so grave, so deeply troubled, that she was certain something sorrowful must have happened.

“Something very melancholy must be the matter,” she declared more than once. “I could see it in his face. Poor man! I’m afraid he’s in financial trouble. Delaford isn’t worth much more than two thousand a year, and his brother left everything in debt. I do think he must have been summoned about money matters. What else could it be? I wonder if it’s true. I’d give anything to know what’s really going on.”

Her thoughts turned next to Miss Williams. “Perhaps it’s about her,” she mused. “He did look rather guilty when I mentioned her name. Maybe she’s ill in town—nothing would surprise me more, since I’ve always thought she’s rather delicate. I’d wager my last farthing it’s about her.” Then, reconsidering, she added, “But then again, it might not be. He’s such a sensible man—he must have cleared up the estate by now. So perhaps it’s not money after all. Could it be his sister in Avignon? She’s been unwell, hasn’t she? And he left so quickly—it looks just like a family emergency. Well, I truly hope he finds peace soon. And maybe, just maybe, he’ll come back with a wife too!”

Mrs. Jennings continued to shift her theories as often as the wind changed direction, each one seeming equally plausible at the moment it arose.

Elinor, though genuinely concerned for Colonel Brandon’s well-being, found herself less absorbed by his sudden departure than Mrs. Jennings did. Her thoughts were drawn elsewhere—by the silence of her sister Marianne and Willoughby. Their refusal to speak of their growing intimacy puzzled her deeply. It was clear, from their every glance and gesture, that something significant had occurred between them. Yet they said nothing—not even to their own family. Why this secrecy? Elinor couldn’t understand it.

She knew marriage might not be immediate—Willoughby, though independent, wasn’t wealthy. Sir John had estimated his income at only six or seven hundred pounds a year, yet he lived extravagantly. He himself had often lamented his lack of funds. But still, Elinor found it hard to believe that mere financial caution warranted such careful concealment. Their behavior was so open, so tender, that the silence seemed almost contradictory. Was there no engagement at all? That question sometimes crept into her mind, and it kept her from pressing Marianne for answers.

Yet Willoughby’s affection was impossible to miss. To Marianne, he showed every sign of deep, devoted love—gentle, passionate, and utterly sincere. To the rest of the Dashwood family, he was warm, attentive, and full of kindness. The cottage at Barton had become, in his eyes, a home. He spent more time there than at Allenham, and whenever he rode out in the morning, he invariably returned to it, spending the day beside Marianne, his favorite dog resting quietly at her feet.

One evening, about a week after Colonel Brandon had left, Willoughby seemed especially moved by the place. When Mrs. Dashwood casually mentioned plans to improve the cottage in spring, he reacted with genuine distress.

“What?” he exclaimed. “Improve this dear cottage? Never. Not while I live. No stone shall be added to its walls, no inch increased in size—if I am to have any say in it.”

“Don’t worry,” said Elin ornament, smiling gently. “No such thing will happen. Mother doesn’t have the money for it.”

“I’m truly glad to hear it,” he replied warmly. “May she stay poor forever, if she can’t use her wealth better than this.”

“Thank you, Willoughby,” she said, amused. “But I promise you, I wouldn’t sacrifice even one sentiment of your attachment—for anyone I love—to make changes like these. Whatever little sum remains after our accounts are settled in spring, I’d rather leave it untouched than spend it in a way that pains you. But are you *really* so fond of this cottage that you see no flaws?”

“I am,” he said firmly. “To me, it is perfect. In fact, I believe it’s the only kind of house where true happiness can exist. If I were rich enough, I’d tear down Combe and rebuild it exactly as this cottage stands.”

“And with dark stairs and a smoky kitchen, I suppose?” Elinor teased.

“Yes!” he answered, almost laughing. “Every detail—the good and the bad—must remain unchanged. Only then might I find the same joy at Combe that I’ve known here.”

“I daresay,” Elinor replied, “that even with larger rooms and wider stairs, you’ll one day find your own home as perfect as this one seems to you now.”

“There are circumstances,” Willoughby said softly, “that could make it dearer still. But this cottage will always hold a claim on my heart no other place can share.”

Mrs. Dashwood watched Marianne closely—her eyes fixed on Willoughby with an expression that spoke volumes. She smiled, knowing how deeply her daughter understood him.

“How often,” Willoughby continued, “did I wish, this time last year, when I was at Allenham, that someone lived in Barton cottage! I never passed it without admiring its beauty—and grieving that it stood empty. How little I imagined then that the first news I’d hear from Mrs. Smith when I returned to the country would be that the cottage was taken. And yet, I felt such joy—such interest—in that news. It must have been because I already sensed the happiness I’d find here. Mustn’t it have been so, Marianne?” he whispered, turning to her.

Then, returning to his usual tone, he added, “And yet you’d change it, Mrs. Dashwood? You’d ruin its charm with false improvements? This parlour—where our friendship began, where so many happy hours have passed—would you turn it into a mere hallway? Everyone would rush through it, unaware that it holds more comfort and warmth than any grand room in England.”

Mrs. Dashwood assured him again that no changes would be made.

“You’re a kind woman,” he said, sincerely. “Your word puts my mind at ease. But go a step further—promise me that not only will the house stay the same, but that you and yours will remain unchanged too. That you’ll always treat me with the kindness that has made everything about you so precious to me.”

The promise was given without hesitation.

That evening, Willoughby’s presence radiated contentment and affection. As he prepared to leave, Mrs. Dashwood asked, “Shall we see you tomorrow for dinner?”

“I don’t ask you to come in the morning,” she added, “since we must walk to the park to visit Lady Middleton.”

“I’ll be there by four o’clock,” he promised, and with a smile, he took his leave.

CHAPTER XV. The next day, Mrs. Dashwood paid a visit to Lady Middleton, accompanied by two of her daughters. Marianne, however, declined to join them, offering some light excuse about tasks that needed attending to. Her mother, who had suspected—perhaps rightly—that Willoughby had promised to call during their absence, was content to let her stay behind.

Upon returning from the park, they found Willoughby’s curricle and servant waiting at the cottage gate. Mrs. Dashwood smiled, certain her guess had been correct. Everything so far unfolded as she had expected. But when they stepped into the house, they encountered something entirely beyond her imagination.

No sooner had they entered the hall than Marianne rushed out of the parlour, clearly overwhelmed with grief, her handkerchief pressed tightly against her eyes. Without acknowledging them, she hurried upstairs. Alarmed and bewildered, the others followed into the room she had just left—only to find Willoughby standing alone, leaning against the mantelpiece, his back turned.

He turned at their entrance, and though he tried to appear composed, his face bore the unmistakable weight of sorrow that had consumed Marianne.

“Is anything wrong?” Mrs. Dashwood exclaimed, stepping forward. “Is she unwell?”

“I hope not,” he replied, forcing a smile. “But I am the one who may be ill—because I’m suffering a great disappointment.”

“Disappointment?”

“Yes. I must leave my engagement with you. This morning, Mrs. Smith summoned me on urgent business to London. I’ve just received my orders and bid farewell to Allenham. As a final gesture of cheerfulness, I came here to say goodbye.”

“To London? And this very morning?”

“Almost immediately.”

“This is most unfortunate,” Mrs. Dashwood said gently. “But Mrs. Smith must be grateful for your service—and I trust her affairs won’t keep you away long.”

Willoughby flushed slightly. “You are very kind,” he murmured, “but I don’t expect to return to Devonshire soon. My visits to Mrs. Smith are never repeated within a year.”

“And Mrs. Smith is your only friend? Allenham the only home where you’re welcome?” Mrs. Dashwood asked, her voice tinged with reproach. “For shame, Willoughby—can you truly wait for an invitation here?”

His colour deepened. He looked down, unable to meet her gaze. “You are too generous,” he whispered.

Mrs. Dashwood glanced at Elinor, surprised. Elinor felt equal shock. For a moment, silence hung heavy in the room.

Then Mrs. Dashwood spoke again. “I only wish to add, dear Willoughby, that Barton Cottage will always welcome you. I won no longer press you to return quickly—since you alone know what might please Mrs. Smith. On that matter, I shall not question your judgment or doubt your intentions.”

“My obligations,” Willoughby answered, flustered, “are such that—I dare not even hope—” He broke off.

Mrs. Dashwood, stunned, could not respond. Another silence fell—until Willoughby, with a faint, pained smile, said, “It’s foolish to linger like this. I’ll not torment myself any longer by staying among friends whose company I can no longer enjoy.” With that, he hastily took leave and left the room.

They watched him step into his carriage. Within minutes, it vanished from sight.

Mrs. Dashwood, overwhelmed, retreated to her chamber, needing solitude to process the suddenness of his departure. Elinor, too, was deeply troubled. She replayed every detail—the abruptness, the forced cheer, the reluctance to accept their hospitality—each trait unlike the man she knew. Was it possible he had never meant to marry Marianne? Or had they quarreled? The agony Marianne had shown upon leaving the room suggested a serious rift, though Elinor couldn’t imagine how such a thing could happen between two so deeply devoted.

Yet whatever the cause, Marianne’s anguish was real. Elinor pitied her sister, knowing she wasn’t merely grieving—but feeding her sorrow, perhaps believing it a duty, a mark of love.

About half an hour later, Mrs. Dashwood returned, her eyes red but her expression calm. “Our dear Willoughby is now miles from Barton,” she said softly, sitting down to sew. “And with what a heavy heart he travels!”

“It’s all so strange,” Elinor murmured. “Gone in the blink of an eye. Last night he was joyful, tender, full of affection. And now—just ten minutes’ notice, and gone forever? Without meaning to come back? There must be more to it than he told us. He didn’t speak like himself. _You_ noticed it too, didn’t you? What could have happened? Did they argue?”

“It wasn’t lack of desire,” Mrs. Dashwood insisted. “I saw plainly enough—he couldn’t accept. I’ve thought it through, and everything makes sense now.”

“Indeed?” Elinor asked, skeptical.

“Yes. I believe Mrs. Smith suspects Willoughby’s feelings for Marianne, disapproves of the match—perhaps because she has other plans for him—and wants him removed. The business trip is just an excuse. He knows she disapproves, so he dares not confess their engagement. And given his dependence on her, he must submit. That’s why he leaves for London, and won’t return soon.”

“You think so?” Elinor said quietly.

“I do. And though you’ll likely dismiss it, I won’t be swayed. I believe it fully. But tell me—what would you suggest instead?”

“Nothing,” Elinor admitted. “You’ve already guessed my answer.”

“So you’d say it might or might not have happened,” Mrs. Dashwood sighed. “Oh, Elinor, how difficult you are! You’d rather assume the worst than accept kindness. You’d prefer to see misery in Marianne and guilt in poor Willoughby than allow for a reasonable explanation. You insist on blaming him simply because he didn’t part with the usual warmth. Can’t you consider that he might be crushed by recent pain? That he might be acting under pressure? That there could be good reasons—reasons he cannot yet share?”

“I don’t blame him,” Elinor said slowly. “But I do wonder. It’s not that I doubt his character—far from it. I love him dearly. But still, I find it hard to believe he would leave without saying more. Secrecy may be necessary—but it doesn’t feel like *his* way.”

“Then you admit my reasoning holds some truth?” Mrs. Dashwood asked, relieved.

“I do. If they are engaged—and I believe they are—then secrecy from Mrs. Smith is wise. And if so, Willoughby should avoid Devonshire for now. But that doesn’t explain why they’ve kept it from *us*.”

“From us?” Mrs. Dashwood laughed softly. “Elinor, do you accuse them of hiding it? After all we’ve seen? After every glance, every gesture, every tender moment? Have you not seen how he looks at her? How he treats us all? Hasn’t his behaviour spoken louder than words? Hasn’t he asked for my consent every day in his manner, his care, his devotion? How can you doubt their engagement? How could Willoughby leave her—perhaps for months—without telling her he loves her? Without sharing their hopes?”

“I confess,” Elinor said, “that every sign points to an engagement—except one: their complete silence on the matter. To me, that outweighs everything else.”

“How strange!” Mrs. Dashwood exclaimed. “After all that has passed, you still suspect? Do you think he’s been pretending? That he doesn’t truly care for her?”

“No,” Elinor said firmly. “I believe he loves her. Deeply. But perhaps too much to show it now.”

“Then why does he seem so indifferent? So careless of the future?”

“Because,” Elinor said, “he may have just left her in agony. He may know he must go, and yet fear offending Mrs. Smith. And he may know that refusing our invitation—saying he won’t return—will seem cold, even cruel. So he’s torn. Embarrassed. Disturbed. A plain explanation would have been better—but I won’t judge him harshly for not giving one. His character is still noble. I only wish he’d been more open.”

“You speak wisely,” Mrs. Dashwood nodded. “Willoughby is not a man to be doubted. Though we’ve known him briefly, he’s respected by all. If he were free to marry at once, yes—it would be odd to leave without telling us. But he isn’t. Their marriage is uncertain. Secrecy, for now, may be wise.”

Their conversation was interrupted by Margaret’s entrance. Elinor sat quietly, reflecting on her mother’s words. She saw the logic in them, and began to hope they might be right.

They saw nothing of Marianne until dinner. When she finally entered the room, she sat silently, her eyes swollen and red. She avoided everyone’s gaze, ate nothing, and said nothing. Only when her mother gently squeezed her hand did her composure break. Tears flooded over, and she fled the room.

That evening, Marianne remained in a state of deep despair. She had no strength to control herself—nor any will to try. Even the slightest mention of Willoughby sent her into fresh waves of grief. Her family, watching helplessly, tried to comfort her—but found themselves drawn inevitably toward the subject that haunted her soul.

CHAPTER XVI. Marianne had not slept a single moment the night after Willoughby left, and she would have felt deeply ashamed if she had been able to rest. She dreaded facing her family the next morning—her eyes swollen, her voice hoarse, her spirit broken. Yet such feelings were too strong for sleep or silence. The entire night passed in wakefulness, and most of it in tears. When she finally rose, she did so with a pounding headache, unable to speak, unwilling to eat, her presence a constant source of sorrow to her mother and sister. No comfort was offered, nor could any be accepted. Her grief was too profound, too real.

After breakfast, she walked alone through the quiet lanes of Allenham, revisiting every spot where they had once laughed together. Each step stirred memories of joy now twisted into pain. She wandered aimlessly, her heart heavy, her thoughts consumed by what had been and what would never be. The evening brought no relief. At the piano, she played every tune they had shared—the songs he had taught her, the melodies their voices had once harmonized upon. She lingered over each note, tracing his handwriting on the music sheets, her breath catching as emotion overwhelmed her. There was no joy in this sorrow, only the deep, quiet ache of absence.

Day after day, she returned to these rituals: sitting at the instrument, singing until her voice cracked under the weight of her tears; reading books they had read together, lingering over pages that once held warmth but now only echoed loneliness. The intensity of her grief could not last forever. Within days, it softened into a quieter melancholy—but still, whenever she walked alone or sat in silence, the old sorrows resurfaced, as sharp and true as ever.

No letter came from Willoughby. And though her family wondered, Marianne showed no sign of expectation. Mrs. Dashwood found comfort in explanations that pleased her own tender heart. “Remember,” she told Elinor, “Sir John often collects our mail himself. It’s possible their letters pass through his hands, and secrecy must be preserved.” Elinor nodded, though she doubted whether such reasoning truly explained the silence. Still, she tried to believe it.

Yet one idea troubled her deeply. “Why don’t you simply ask Marianne,” she said gently, “whether she is engaged to Willoughby? You are her mother—kind, loving, understanding. Such a question would come naturally from affection, not suspicion. She has always been open with you.”

Mrs. Dashwood recoiled at the thought. “Never! Suppose they are not engaged—what anguish would such a question cause? It would be cruel, ungenerous. I would never earn her trust again if I forced her to confess something meant to remain private. I know my daughter. I know her heart. When the time comes, she will tell me herself. I will not press her. A child’s confidence should not be broken by a parent’s curiosity.”

Elinor sighed. She saw only weakness in such sentimentality, especially when her sister’s youth and suffering demanded clarity. But she could not sway her mother. Reason, care, prudence—all were lost beneath the glow of romantic tenderness.

It was several days before Willoughby’s name was spoken aloud in front of Marianne. Sir John and Mrs. Jennings, however, were less delicate. Their jokes and teasing only added to her pain. But one evening, while Mrs. Dashwood idly picked up a volume of Shakespeare, she said, “We never finished *Hamlet*, Marianne. Willoughby left before we could. Let’s put it aside—we’ll resume when he returns… though it may be months before he does.”

“Months!” Marianne exclaimed, startled. “No—weeks, at most.”

Her mother winced at the slip, but Elinor smiled. For once, Marianne had spoken with certainty—proof, she thought, that her sister still believed in love, in hope, in the return of the man who had promised everything.

A week after Willoughby’s departure, Marianne was persuaded—though reluctantly—to join her sisters on their usual walk. Until then, she had avoided them entirely during their rambles. If they planned to go to the downs, she slipped away toward the lanes; if they chose the valley, she climbed the hills. She disappeared whenever they set off, seeking solitude in the landscape that mirrored her inner world.

But Elinor, concerned for her sister’s isolation, insisted. Together, they walked along the road through the valley, speaking little. Marianne’s mind was elsewhere—too full of memory and longing to focus on anything else. Elinor, content merely to have her beside her, did not push further.

Beyond the valley entrance, the land opened into a broader stretch of countryside. Before them lay the long road they had first traveled when arriving at Barton. As they paused to take in the view—a scene they had never seen from this vantage point—they spotted a rider approaching.

In moments, they recognized him as a gentleman. Then, with sudden joy, Marianne gasped, “It’s him! It’s really him! I know it!”

She started forward, but Elinor called out, “Wait, Marianne—I think you’re mistaken. He’s not tall enough, and he doesn’t carry himself like Willoughby.”

“He does,” Marianne insisted, her eyes shining. “His coat, his horse—everything! I knew he’d come soon.”

She hurried ahead, eager, certain. Elinor quickened her pace to keep up, hoping to shield her sister from disappointment.

At thirty yards, they saw clearly. Marianne looked again—and her face fell. Her heart sank. She turned abruptly, ready to flee, when both sisters called out to stop her. Then another voice joined theirs—one familiar, warm, unmistakable.

She turned around, surprised, and there stood Edward Ferrars.

He was the only person in the world who could forgive himself for not being Willoughby. The only one whose presence could bring even a smile to her tear-streaked face. She wiped her eyes and welcomed him warmly, her own sorrow momentarily eclipsed by her sister’s happiness.

Edward dismounted, handed his horse to his servant, and walked back with them to Barton. He had come expressly to visit.

They greeted him with kindness—especially Marianne, whose warmth surpassed even Elinor’s. To her, however, the meeting between Edward and her sister seemed merely an extension of the distant coldness she had once noticed at Norland. Edward, in particular, gave no sign of affection. He appeared confused, barely showing pleasure at seeing them. He spoke little, answered only when questioned, and offered no special attention to Elinor.

Marianne watched, growing more puzzled. She began to feel a faint dislike for him—though, as always, her emotions quickly led her back to Willoughby, whose manner had been so alive, so passionate, so utterly unlike Edward’s reserve.

After a brief pause, Marianne asked, “Did you come straight from London?”

“No,” Edward replied. “I’ve been in Devonshire for two weeks.”

“Two weeks?” Marianne repeated, astonished that he had stayed so long without visiting Elinorth. “And you didn’t see her?”

He hesitated. “I’ve been staying near Plymouth with some friends.”

“Have you been in Sussex lately?” Elinor asked.

“I was at Norland about a month ago.”

“And how does dear, dear Norland look?” Marianne asked, her voice softening.

“Dear, dear Norland,” Elinor said, “probably looks much as it always does at this time of year—the woods thick with dead leaves.”

“Oh,” Marianne breathed, “how I used to love watching them fall! How the wind would scatter them around me, swirling in gusts! What beauty, what feeling they inspired! Now, no one notices them. They’re just swept away, treated as a nuisance.”

“It’s not everyone,” Elinor said gently, “who shares your passion for dead leaves.”

“No,” Marianne murmured, “my feelings aren’t often understood. But sometimes…” She trailed off, lost in thought.

Then she turned to Edward. “Look here—this is Barton Valley. See those hills? Do you think you’ve seen their equals? To the left, Barton Park, hidden among the trees. You can see the end of the house. And there, beneath that distant hill, rises our cottage.”

“It’s a beautiful place,” Edward said, “but these lowlands must be muddy in winter.”

“How can you think of dirt when such beauty surrounds you?” Elinor asked.

“Because,” he smiled, “among all those lovely sights, I see a very dirty lane.”

Marianne walked on, thinking to herself, *How strange.*

She turned to him. “Is your neighbourhood pleasant? Are the Middletons agreeable?”

“No,” Marianne replied, “we couldn’t be more poorly placed.”

“Marianne!” Elinor protested. “How can you say that? They’ve been kind to us. Don’t you remember all the happy days we’ve spent with them?”

“No,” Marianne whispered, “nor the painful ones.”

Elinor said nothing. Instead, she focused on keeping the conversation going, talking about their home, its comforts, trying to draw Edward into a natural exchange. But his silence and restraint wounded her. She was annoyed, almost angry—but resolved to treat him with fairness, guided by past kindness rather than present indifference.

Mrs. Dashwood’s surprise at seeing Edward was brief—so natural did his arrival seem to her that wonder gave way swiftly to joy. Her warmth and genuine affection overwhelmed any trace of reserve in him; the moment he stepped into the house, his usual shyness began to fade, and by the time he sat down with them, it had vanished entirely under the charm of her kindness. Indeed, a man who loved either of her daughters could scarcely remain indifferent to their mother. Elinor watched with quiet satisfaction as Edward gradually returned to himself—his manner softened, his interest in their lives rekindled. He was attentive, polite, and thoughtful, complimenting their home and its view, yet there lingered a certain heaviness about him. He was not truly at ease.

The family noticed it plainly. Mrs. Dashwood, quick to interpret his mood, attributed it to his mother’s rigid expectations and sighed with indignation at the selfishness of parents who valued status above their children’s happiness. “What are your mother’s plans for you now, Edward?” she asked after dinner, when they gathered around the fire. “Still determined to make you a great orator against your will?”

“No,” he replied, smiling faintly. “I hope she’s finally convinced I have neither the talent nor the desire for public life.”

“But how will your reputation be made?” Mrs. Dashwood pressed. “Your family expects greatness from you, and without fortune, passion for strangers, or confidence, it may prove difficult.”

“I don’t seek distinction,” Edward said firmly. “I have no wish to stand out—and thank Heaven, I can’t be forced into genius or eloquence.”

“You’ve never been ambitious,” Mrs. Dashwood observed. “Your desires are modest.”

“As modest as anyone else’s,” he answered. “I want nothing more than what every person wants—perfect happiness—but only on my own terms. Grandeur won’t bring it.”

“Strange that it wouldn’t!” Marianne exclaimed. “What does wealth or rank have to do with true contentment?”

“Grandeur has little,” Elinor said gently, “but wealth has much.”

“Elinor, for shame!” Marianne protested. “Money can only buy happiness where nothing else offers it. Beyond basic comfort, it brings no real joy—unless one is already lacking in spirit.”

“Perhaps we’re closer in thought than you think,” Elinor said with a smile. “Your ‘competence’ and my ‘wealth’ likely mean much the same. Without it, we’d both agree that comfort would be impossible. Only your ideals are nobler than mine. So tell me—what is your competence?”

“About eighteen hundred or two thousand a year,” Marianne replied. “No more than that.”

Elinor laughed. “Two thousand! One is my wealth—I knew this was coming.”

“And yet two thousand is quite modest,” Marianne insisted. “A family cannot live on less. I’m not extravagant—just reasonable. Proper servants, a carriage, perhaps two, and hunters—none of that can be managed on a smaller income.”

Elinor smiled again, recognizing in her sister’s words an uncanny vision of their future at Combe Magna.

“Hunters?” Edward repeated, puzzled. “But why must you have hunters? Not everyone hunts.”

Marianne flushed slightly. “But most people do.”

Margaret, struck by inspiration, suddenly declared, “I wish someone would give us each a large fortune!”

“Oh, that they would!” Marianne breathed, her eyes bright and her cheeks glowing with imagined delight.

“We’re all agreed on that,” Elinor said dryly, “despite our current lack of means.”

“Oh dear!” Margaret cried. “How happy I’d be! I wonder what I’d do with it?”

Marianne looked as if she already knew.

“I’d be hard-pressed to spend such a sum myself,” Mrs. Dashwood mused, “if my children were all wealthy without my help.”

“You should start by improving this house,” Elinor suggested. “Then your troubles would vanish.”

“What magnificent orders would go to London,” Edward joked, “in such a case! What a day for booksellers, music shops, and print dealers! You, Miss Dashwood, would commission every new print of merit—while Marianne…” He paused, grinning. “I know her soul—there wouldn’t be enough music in London to satisfy her. And books—Thomson, Cowper, Scott—she’d buy them over and over. She’d buy up every copy, I suspect, just to keep them from falling into unworthy hands. And every book that teaches her how to admire a twisted old tree. Would you not, Marianne? Forgive me if I’m too bold—but I wanted to show you I hadn’t forgotten our old arguments.”

“I love hearing of the past,” Marianne said softly. “Whether joyful or sad, I cherish memory. You’ll never offend me by speaking of former days. And yes—you’re right about how I’d spend my money—at least some of it. My loose cash would certainly go toward expanding my music and book collections.”

“And the rest,” Edward teased, “would be spent on annuities for authors and their heirs.”

“No,” Marianne said, “I’d have other uses for it.”

“Perhaps,” he ventured, “you’d award it as a prize to whoever wrote the finest defense of your favorite belief—that no one can fall in love more than once in their lifetime. Your opinion hasn’t changed, I suppose?”

“Undoubtedly,” she replied. “At my age, opinions are fixed. Nothing I see or hear now would alter them.”

“Marianne remains steadfast,” Elinor remarked. “She hasn’t changed.”

“She’s simply grown a little more serious,” Edward added.

“Nay, Edward,” Marianne said, “you needn’t reproach me. You’re not very cheerful yourself.”

“Why should you think so?” he replied, sighing. “But gaiety has never been part of my nature.”

“Nor, I think, of Marianne’s,” Elinor said. “I wouldn’t call her lively—she’s earnest, passionate in everything she does—sometimes speaks much, always with animation—but she’s rarely truly merry.”

“I believe you’re right,” Edward admitted. “Yet I’ve always seen her as lively.”

“I often misjudge people,” Elinor confessed. “I imagine them far more cheerful or solemn, cleverer or duller than they really are. Sometimes I’m misled by what they say about themselves—or what others say. I don’t take time to judge properly.”

“But surely,” Marianne said, “it’s right to trust others’ opinions? I thought our judgment was meant to serve those of our neighbors. That’s always been your teaching, I’m sure.”

“No, Marianne,” Elinor corrected gently. “I’ve never sought to subdue reason. I’ve only ever urged better behavior. I’ve wished you’d treat acquaintances with more attention—but never advised you to adopt their views in matters of consequence.”

“You haven’t succeeded in changing your sister’s ways,” Edward said to Elinor. “Are you making progress?”

“Quite the opposite,” she replied, glancing meaningfully at Marianne.

“My judgment,” Edward said, “is on your side—but my practice leans more toward your sister’s. I never wish to offend, but I’m so awkward, so shy, that I often seem careless when I’m merely hesitant. I’ve often wondered if nature intended me for humble company—I feel so ill at ease among genteel strangers.”

“Marianne doesn’t have shyness to excuse her inattention,” Elinor said.

“She knows her worth,” Edward replied. “Shyness comes from feeling inferior. If I believed my manners were easy and graceful, I wouldn’t be shy.”

“But you’d still be reserved,” Marianne said. “And that’s worse.”

Edward started. “Reserved? Am I reserved, Marianne?”

“Yes, very.”

“I don’t understand,” he said, coloring. “Reserved—how? In what way? What am I supposed to do? What could you possibly mean?”

Elinor looked surprised at his sudden emotion. But trying to lighten the mood, she said, “Don’t you know my sister well enough to understand? She calls anyone reserved who doesn’t talk as fast or admire things as wildly as she does.”

Edward said nothing. His expression grew serious, thoughtful. The weight of his feelings settled back upon him, and he sat silent and distant, lost in his own thoughts.

Elinor watched with quiet concern as her friend’s spirits remained low throughout his visit. His presence brought her only partial comfort, for it was clear that he was not truly at ease. Though he had come to Barton Cottage, his joy in being there seemed incomplete. She longed to believe that the affection he once clearly held for her still burned bright—but his manner was uncertain, shifting between warmth and reserve, leaving her unsure of where his heart truly lay.

The next morning, Edward joined Elinor and Marianne in the breakfast room before the rest of the household descended. Marianne, ever eager to foster closeness between them, soon excused herself and retreated upstairs—only to stop short when she heard the parlour door open. Turning around, she was startled to see Edward stepping out.

“I’m going into the village to check on my horses,” he said, “since you’re not quite ready for breakfast. I’ll be back shortly.”

When he returned, he spoke with renewed appreciation for the surrounding countryside. His walk had taken him through various parts of the valley, and from the higher ground of the village, he’d been treated to a sweeping view that delighted him. This sparked Marianne’s interest instantly. She began to describe her own deep admiration for the scenery, eager to know what details had caught Edward’s eye.

But he cut her off gently. “Don’t ask too closely, Marianne,” he said. “I confess—I have no real understanding of the picturesque. I’ll offend you if we go into specifics. I’ll call hills steep when they should be bold, surfaces odd when they ought to be rugged, and distant views invisible when they’re merely softened by mist. You must settle for the honesty of my opinion. To me, this is a fine country—the hills are steep, the woods thick with good timber, and the valley looks warm and inviting, dotted with rich meadows and tidy farmhouses. It fits perfectly with my idea of beauty: nature combined with usefulness. And since you find it beautiful, I can easily believe it’s picturesque too—even if I don’t see the rocks or moss or tangled brushwood that make it so. I simply don’t understand those things.”

“It’s true enough,” Marianne replied, “but why boast of it?”

Elinor smiled faintly. “I think,” she said, “Edward avoids one kind of pretension by falling into another. He dislikes people who feign deep emotion over landscapes, so he chooses instead to pretend indifference. He’s fastidious—he must have an affectation of his own.”

“Quite right,” Marianne agreed. “Admiration of scenery has become nothing more than empty talk. Everyone pretends to feel deeply, quoting the same old phrases about ‘picturesque beauty’ as if it were a language of its own. I hate such jargon. Sometimes I’ve kept my real feelings silent because I couldn’t express them without sounding stale and worn-out.”

“I’m certain,” Edward said, “that you feel every bit of delight you claim to feel. But in return, your sister must allow me to feel only what I say. I enjoy a fine view—but not for the reasons others do. I don’t admire twisted, dying trees. I prefer tall, straight ones that thrive. I don’t care for crumbling cottages or wild thistles. A neat farmhouse pleases me far more than a ruined tower—and a group of cheerful villagers brings me more joy than any band of romantic outlaws.”

Marianne stared at him, astonished. Then she glanced at Elinor with pity. Elinor only laughed softly. The conversation ended there, and Marianne fell into thoughtful silence—until something unexpected drew her attention.

She was seated beside Edward when Mrs. Dashwood handed him his tea. As he reached for the cup, his hand passed directly in front of her, revealing a ring on one finger—a ring with a plait of hair set in the center.

“I never saw you wear a ring before, Edward,” she exclaimed. “Is that Fanny’s hair? I remember her saying she’d give you some. But I would have thought hers darker.”

Her words slipped out without thought, but the moment she saw the pain in Edward’s face, she regretted them fiercely. His cheeks flushed deeply, and he glanced briefly at Elinor before replying, “Yes—it’s my sister’s hair. The setting changes how it looks, you know.”

Elinor met his gaze—and felt a sudden certainty. That hair was hers. Not a gift, but something taken, hidden, perhaps even stolen from her own possession. Yet she did not feel offended. Instead, she chose to ignore the moment, turning immediately to another subject, while silently resolving to examine the ring closely whenever she could, to confirm beyond doubt that the hair matched her own shade.

Edward remained visibly unsettled for hours, his thoughts drifting inward. He was unusually quiet all morning. Marianne reproached herself bitterly for her careless words—but she didn’t realize how little hurt she had caused Elinor.

By midday, Sir John and Mrs. Jennings arrived, drawn by news of a gentleman guest at the cottage. With Margaret’s earlier hints, they quickly deduced that the name Ferrars began with an F—and thus, their future teasing was already planned. For now, though, they contented themselves with meaningful glances and knowing smiles directed at Elinor.

Sir John, as always, came bearing invitations. “You *must* come drink tea with us tonight,” he declared. “We’ll be alone—perfect for a quiet evening. And tomorrow, you absolutely must dine with us—we’ll have a large gathering.”

Mrs. Jennings added her encouragement. “And who knows? You might even raise a dance!” she teased. “That would tempt *you*, Miss Marianne.”

“A dance?” Marianne gasped. “Impossible! Who would dance?”

“Why, yourselves, the Careys, the Whitakers—naturally!” Sir John replied. “What, you thought no one could dance now that *certain someone* has gone?”

“I wish with all my heart,” he continued, “that Willoughby were here again.”

His words, and Marianne’s sudden flush, struck Edward sharply. He leaned toward Elinor, whispering, “Who is Willoughby?”

She gave a brief answer. But Marianne’s expression said far more. Edward read her face now—understanding not just the meaning of others’ remarks, but also the mystery behind her earlier emotions.

As their visitors left, he moved quietly beside her and whispered, “I’ve been guessing. May I tell you what I think?”

“What do you mean?”

“Shall I?”

“Of course.”

“Well then—I guess Mr. Willoughby hunts.”

Marianne was both surprised and flustered, yet she couldn’t help smiling at his quiet wit. After a pause, she murmured, “Oh, Edward! How can you? But someday—perhaps you’ll like him.”

He looked at her, puzzled by her earnest tone. Had he not assumed it was a joke, a light-hearted tease meant to amuse their circle, born of mere acquaintance? He would never have dared speak of it otherwise.

CHAPTER XIX. Edward stayed at the cottage for a week, and though Mrs. Dashwood warmly urged him to remain longer, he seemed determined to leave just as his time among them was most joyful. His spirits, though still uneven, had brightened in the final days—he grew increasingly fond of the house and its surroundings, sighed whenever he mentioned departure, declared himself free of obligations, and even hesitated over where to go next. Yet go he must. The week passed with such speed that he could scarcely believe it was over. He repeated this often, and said other things too—words that revealed the true depth of his feelings, contradicting his actions. He had no pleasure at Norland, detested London, yet both were destinations he would be forced to choose. He valued their kindness above all else, and found his greatest joy in being with them. Still, he left at the end of the week, despite their wishes and his own, with no ties binding him to stay.

Elinor attributed his strange behavior to his mother’s influence. It was fortunate for her that Mrs. Ferrars remained largely unknown to her, so she could blame every inconsistency on the elder woman’s rigid expectations. Though disappointed and sometimes irritated by Edward’s indecisiveness—especially toward herself—Elinor approached his conduct with patience and generosity, far more than she had ever shown Willoughby. She saw his lack of energy, honesty, and consistency not as flaws of character, but as consequences of his financial dependence and his awareness of his mother’s cold ambitions. His brief visit, his firm resolve to depart, all stemmed from the same inner conflict—the struggle between desire and duty, child and parent. The familiar sorrow of obligation overriding affection lay at the root of everything.

She longed to know when this torment would end, when Mrs. Ferrars might soften, and Edward be free to live as he wished. But since such hopes were vain, she turned instead to comfort in what she could trust: the sincerity of Edward’s affection, the warmth of his glances, the words he spoke, and especially the ring he wore—a constant, quiet proof of his devotion.

At breakfast on the last morning, Mrs. Dashwood remarked, “Edward, I think you’d be happier if you had a profession to occupy your mind and give direction to your life. Of course, it might mean less time for us—but then (with a smile), you’d at least know where you were going when you left.”

“I assure you,” he replied, “I’ve thought about this for years. It’s been—and always will be—a great misfortune for me to have no real purpose, no trade or calling to claim my time. But it’s not entirely my fault. My own inclinations and those of my family have shaped me into an idle, helpless man. We never agreed on a profession. I favored the church—still do. But that wasn’t fashionable enough for them. They suggested the army. That was too flashy for me. Law was considered genteel; many young men in the Temple looked well-dressed and impressive, driving smart gigs through town. But I had no interest in law, even in this polished form they approved. As for the navy—it had style, yes, but I was too old to begin. In the end, since there was no need for a profession at all—since one could be just as grand without a red coat as with one—idleness was deemed the most advantageous, the most honorable path. And so, at eighteen, I gave in to my friends’ advice and entered Oxford. Since then, I’ve been properly idle.”

“And the result?” said Mrs. Dashwood. “Will your sons grow up with as many pursuits as Columella’s?”

“They’ll be raised,” he said gravely, “to be as unlike me as possible—in feeling, action, condition, everything.”

“Ah, Edward,” she smiled, “this is only the melancholy of parting. You’re feeling low, and imagine anyone different from yourself must be happy. But remember—everyone feels sorrow at separation, no matter their upbringing. Know your own happiness. You lack only patience—or call it hope. Your mother will grant you independence soon. It is her duty, and in time, it will become her joy to prevent your youth from slipping away in discontent. What can’t a few months accomplish?”

“I think,” Edward replied quietly, “that many months will bring me no relief.”

Though he couldn’t share this despair with Mrs. Dashwood, it deepened the pain of his departure, leaving a lingering unease—especially in Elinor. Yet she resolved to master her sorrow, refusing to show more grief than her family felt. Unlike Marianne, who retreated into silence and solitude to deepen her sadness, Elinor returned to her drawing-table as soon as he was gone. She worked steadily throughout the day, avoided dwelling on his name, and maintained her usual interest in household matters. If her sorrow didn’t lessen, at least it didnnot grow unnecessarily, and her family was spared worry.

Marianne saw such restraint as lacking merit. To her, self-control was impossible with strong emotions, and pointless with weak ones. She admitted, though reluctantly, that Elinor’s affections were calm—yet still loved and respected her sister, despite this painful truth.

Without isolating herself or lying awake in thought, Elinor found ample time each day to reflect on Edward—on his actions, his moods, his promises. When her family was occupied or absent, conversation ceased, and solitude took hold. Her thoughts drifted naturally to the past and future, consumed by memory, imagination, and emotion. She pondered him with tenderness, pity, approval, criticism, and doubt—each mood shaped by her shifting heart.

One morning, shortly after Edward’s departure, she was drawn from such reverie by the sound of footsteps outside. She glanced out the window and saw a group approaching the door—Sir John and Lady Middleton, Mrs. Jennings, and two strangers: a gentleman and a lady she did not recognize. Sir John spotted her immediately and crossed the lawn, insisting she open the casement to speak to him, though the distance between window and door made privacy nearly impossible.

“Well,” he called, “we’ve brought you some visitors. How do you like them?”

“Hush!” she whispered. “They’ll hear you.”

“Never mind,” he laughed. “It’s only the Palmers. Charlotte’s very pretty—look this way!”

But Elinor declined, knowing she’d see them soon enough.

“Where’s Marianne? Has she fled because we’ve come? I see her piano is open.”

“She’s walking, I believe.”

Mrs. Jennings arrived breathless, unable to wait for the door to open. “How are you, my dear? How’s Mrs. Dashwood? Where are your sisters? What! All alone? You’ll be glad of company. I’ve brought my son and daughter to see you. Only think—they came so suddenly! I heard a carriage last night while we were drinking tea, but I thought it might be Colonel Brandon back again. So I said to Sir John, ‘Do you think I hear a carriage? Perhaps it’s Colonel Brandon!’”

Elinor had to turn away mid-sentence to greet the others. Lady Middleton introduced the newcomers. Mrs. Dashwood and Margaret descended the stairs, and all sat down to exchange glances while Mrs. Jennings continued her story, pacing through the hall with Sir John at her heels.

Mrs. Palmer was younger than Lady Middleton and utterly different. Short and plump, with a lovely face and a radiant expression of cheerfulness, she moved with warmth and ease. Her manners lacked Lady Middleton’s refinement, but they were far more engaging. She entered smiling, smiled constantly—except when laughing—and smiled again as she left. Her husband, Mr. Palmer, was a serious-looking young man of twenty-five or six, with a fashionable air and a quiet demeanor. He entered with a haughty glance, bowed stiffly without speaking, scanned the room briefly, then picked up a newspaper and read silently until departure.

Mrs. Palmer, meanwhile, was full of admiration. “Oh! What a delightful room! I’ve never seen anything so charming! Mama, look how much better it is than last time! I always thought it sweet, ma’am—but you’ve made it perfect! Look, sister, isn’t everything lovely? I’d love a house like this! Wouldn’t you, Mr. Palmer?”

Mr. Palmer gave no reply, nor lifted his eyes from the paper.

“Oh, Mr. Palmer doesn’t hear me,” she giggled. “He never does sometimes. It’s so ridiculous!”

This struck Mrs. Dashwood as odd—she had never before found humor in someone’s indifference. She stared at the couple in bewilderment.

Mrs. Jennings kept talking loudly, recounting their surprise the night before at seeing the Palmers arrive. Mrs. Palmer laughed heartily at the memory, and everyone echoed their agreement that it had been a pleasant surprise.

“You may believe how delighted we were,” Mrs. Jennings added, leaning toward Elinor and speaking softly, though they sat apart. “But I can’t help wishing they hadn’t traveled so fast, or made such a long journey. They went all the way around by London for some business—you know (nodding significantly toward her daughter) it wasn’t safe for her in her condition. I wanted her to rest today, but she insisted on coming. She was so eager to see you all!”

Mrs. Palmer laughed, saying it wouldn’t hurt her.

“She expects to deliver in February,” Mrs. Jennings continued.

Lady Middleton could endure no more and asked Mr. Palmer if there was any news in the paper.

“No,” he replied flatly, and resumed reading.

“Here comes Marianne!” Sir John announced. “Now, Palmer, you’ll see a truly beautiful girl.” He hurried into the hall, opened the front door, and ushered her in.

Mrs. Jennings greeted her at once. “Have you been to Allenham?”

Mrs. Palmer burst into laughter, clearly understanding the reference.

Mr. Palmer looked up, studied Marianne for several moments, then returned to his paper.

Mrs. Palmer’s attention shifted to the drawings on the walls. She rose to examine them closely.

“Oh, dear! How beautiful these are! Just look, mama—how sweet! I declare they’re absolutely enchanting—I could gaze at them forever!”

Then she sat down again, forgetting them entirely.

When Lady Middleton rose to leave, Mr. Palmer followed, folded the paper, stretched, and surveyed the room once more.

“My love,” his wife teased, “have you been asleep?”

He said nothing. After a moment, he observed, “The ceiling is crooked,” and bowed his way out.

Sir John had begged them all to spend the next day at the park. Mrs. Dashwood refused—she didn’t wish to dine with them more often than they visited the cottage. Her daughters might decide for themselves. But neither Elinor nor Marianne felt any curiosity about Mr. and Mrs. Palmer’s dining habits, nor expected pleasure from their company. They tried to decline—citing uncertain weather—but Sir John insisted. The carriage would come for them. Lady Middleton, Mrs. Jennings, and Mrs. Palmer all joined in urging them, seemingly united in avoiding a quiet evening at home. Reluctantly, the sisters agreed.

“Why should they invite us?” Marianne asked as the guests departed.

“The rent here is said to be low,” Elinor replied, “but it’s a hard bargain if we must dine at the park every time someone stays with either side.”

“They mean to be kind,” Elinor said gently, “just as they were weeks ago. Their hospitality hasn’t changed. If their gatherings seem dull now, the fault lies elsewhere—not with them.”

The next day, as the Miss Dashwoods entered the drawing-room of the park through one door, Mrs. Palmer burst in at the other, her usual cheerfulness undimmed by the weather or any other circumstance. She greeted them all with warm affection, clasping their hands and exclaiming with genuine delight, “I am so glad to see you again! I was afraid you might not come—such a dreadful day! But it would have been a shame if you hadn’t, since we’re leaving tomorrow. The Westons are coming to us next week, you know.”

She paused only briefly before adding, “It was quite sudden that we came at all—I didn’t even know until the carriage was at the door. Then Mr. Palmer asked me if I’d go with him to Barton. He’s so droll—he never tells me anything!” She laughed, then added with regret, “I’m so sorry we can’t stay longer. But we’ll meet again in town soon, I hope.”

Elinor and Marianne exchanged glances. They were compelled to gently disappoint her. “Not go to town?” Mrs. Palmer exclaimed, laughing. “Oh, I should be terribly disappointed if you didn’t. I could find the most lovely house in Hanover Square—right next door to ours! You must come. And I’d be delighted to chaperone you, if Mrs. Dashwood doesn’t feel like attending public events. I’ll be happy to do it until I’m confined.”

They thanked her warmly but politely declined, explaining their plans. Mrs. Palmer turned to her husband, who had just entered the room. “Oh, my love,” she said, “you must help me persuade the Miss Dashwoods to come to town this winter!”

Mr. Palmer offered no reply. After a brief bow to the ladies, he began grumbling about the weather. “How horrid all this is!” he declared. “Rain makes everything and everyone unbearable. Dullness creeps in just as much indoors as out. It makes one loathe every acquaintance. What on earth does Sir John mean by not having a billiard room? How few people truly understand comfort! Sir John is as dull as the weather itself.”

As the rest of the company arrived, Sir John remarked with mild dismay, “I’m afraid, Miss Marianne, you’ve missed your usual walk to Allenham today.”

Marianne looked grave and said nothing.

“Oh, don’t be so coy,” Mrs. Palmer teased. “We know all about it, I assure you. And I admire your taste—he’s extremely handsome, isn’t he? We don’t live far from him in the country—only ten miles, I’d say.”

“More like thirty,” Mr. Palmer corrected dryly.

“Oh, well—there’s not much difference,” she replied, unfazed. “I’ve never been to his house, but they say it’s a sweet little place.”

“Vile,” Mr. Palmer snapped. “The worst spot I’ve ever seen.”

Marianne remained silent, though her expression betrayed her interest.

“Is it really so ugly?” Mrs. Palmer pressed. “Then perhaps it’s some other place they’re referring to.”

At dinner, Sir John observed with regret that only eight were present. “My dear,” he said to his wife, “it’s very frustrating that we’re so few. Why didn’t you invite the Gilberts?”

“I did tell you earlier,” she replied, “that it wouldn’t work—they dined with us last time.”

“Ah, but you and I, Sir John,” Mrs. Jennings interjected, “shouldn’t be so formal.”

“That would make you very ill-bred,” Mr. Palmer retorted sharply.

“My love, you contradict everyone,” Mrs. Palmer said, laughing as always. “Do you know you’re being quite rude?”

“I didn’t realize I was calling your mother ill-bred,” he muttered.

“Ay, you may abuse me all you like,” Mrs. Jennings chuckled, “but you took Charlotte off my hands and can’t give her back. So there—now I have the upper hand.”

Charlotte laughed heartily, clearly pleased that her husband couldn’t rid himself of her. “I don’t care how cross he gets,” she declared, “we’re stuck together.”

Mrs. Palmer’s good humor was unshakable. Her husband’s studied indifference, insolence, and constant complaints seemed to amuse her rather than offend. When he scolded or mocked her, she found it endlessly entertaining.

“Mr. Palmer is so droll!” she whispered to Elinor. “He’s always in such a bad mood.”

Elinor, after watching him closely, did not believe he was genuinely ill-tempered or uncivil as he pretended to be. Perhaps his sourness stemmed from the common male frustration of marrying a woman whose charm outweighed her sense—but she knew such misjudgments were too frequent for any sensible man to remain wounded by them. Instead, Elinor suspected his behavior arose from a desire to appear superior—a wish to stand apart from others by belittling everything around him. It was a familiar motive, though the method—by cultivating an air of rudeness—was unlikely to win him many friends beyond his wife.

Soon, Mrs. Palmer leaned toward Elinor and said, “Oh, my dear Miss Dashwood, I have a favor to ask—will you and your sister come spend Christmas with us at Cleveland? Please do—come while the Westons are here. I cannot imagine how happy I’d be! It will be delightful.”

She turned to her husband. “My love, don’t you long to have the Miss Dashwoods come to Cleveland?”

“Certainly,” he replied, sneering. “I came into Devonshire with no other purpose.”

“There now!” Mrs. Palmer triumphantly declared. “You see, Mr. Palmer wants you—so you can’t refuse.”

But both sisters firmly declined.

“But you *must* come,” Mrs. Palmer insisted. “I’m sure you’ll love it. The Westons will be here, and it will be charming. Cleveland is such a lovely place—and we’re so lively now, because Mr. Palmer is constantly traveling around the countryside canvassing for the election. So many new people have come to dine with us—I’ve never seen such a crowd! But poor fellow—it’s exhausting for him. He has to make everyone like him.”

Elinor struggled to keep a straight face as she sympathized with the burden.

“How wonderful it will be when he’s in Parliament!” Charlotte exclaimed. “Won’t it be ridiculous? I shall laugh so hard to see all his letters addressed with ‘M.P.’ But do you know—he says he won’t frank for me. He refuses! Don’t you, Mr. Palmer?”

Mr. Palmer ignored her.

“He hates writing,” Charlotte continued. “He says it’s simply shocking.”

“No,” he replied coldly, “I never said anything so absurd. Don’t attribute your own misuse of language to me.”

“There now!” Mrs. Palmer giggled. “See how droll he is. That’s always the way with him. Sometimes he won’t speak to me for hours, and then suddenly blurts out something hilarious—about anything at all.”

Later, as they returned to the drawing-room, Mrs. Palmer surprised Elinor by asking, “Do you like Mr. Palmer very much?”

“Certainly,” Elinor replied. “He seems quite agreeable.”

“Well, I’m so glad you do. I knew you would—he’s so pleasant. And Mr. Palmer adores you and your sister, I can tell you. He’ll be terribly disappointed if you don’t come to Cleveland. I can’t understand why you’d object.”

Elinor once again declined, changing the subject to end the conversation.

Yet she had hoped that, living in the same county, Mrs. Palmer might offer more insight into Mr. Willoughby’s character than the Middletons’ limited acquaintance could provide. Any confirmation of his worth would ease Marianne’s fears. So Elinor cautiously asked, “Do you see much of Mr. Willoughby at Cleveland? Are you well acquainted with him?”

“Oh, dear yes,” Mrs. Palmer answered brightly. “I know him extremely well—though I’ve never actually spoken to him. I’ve seen him countless times in town. Somehow, I never stayed at Barton while he was at Allenham. Mama saw him once here, but I was with my uncle in Weymouth. Still, if we’d been in Somersetshire together, we’d have seen a great deal of him. He’s rarely at Combe, I believe—but even if he were, Mr. Palmer wouldn’t visit him. He’s in the opposition, you know, and Combe is so far away.”

She paused, then added, “I know exactly why you’re asking. Your sister is going to marry him, isn’t she? I’m absolutely thrilled—for then she’ll be my neighbor.”

Elinor blinked. “Upon my word, Mrs. Palmer, you seem to know more about it than I do. Do you really expect such a match?”

“Don’t pretend to deny it,” Mrs. Palmer insisted. “Everyone’s talking about it. I heard it myself on the way through town.”

“My dear Mrs. Palmer,” Elinor said, astonished.

“Upon my honor, I did,” she continued. “I met Colonel Brandon Monday morning in Bond Street, just before we left London, and he told me right away.”

Elinor was stunned. “Colonel Brandon told you? Surely you’re mistaken. To reveal such news to someone unconnected—especially if it’s true—is not what I’d expect from him.”

“But I swear it’s true,” Mrs. Palmer insisted. “Let me explain. When we met, he turned back and walked with us. We talked about my brother and sister, and various things. Then I said, ‘So, Colonel, there’s a new family at Barton cottage, I hear—and mama says they’re very pretty. One of them is supposed to be marrying Mr. Willoughby of Combe Magna. Is it true? You must know, since you’ve been in Devonshire recently.’”

“And what did he say?” Elinor asked.

“Oh, he didn’t say much. But he looked—well, as if he knew it was true. From that moment, I decided it was certain. It will be quite delightful, I declare! When is it to happen?”

“Was Mr. Brandon well?” Elinor asked.

“Oh, perfectly. And he spoke so highly of you—kept praising you nonstop.”

Elinor smiled. “I’m flattered by his regard. He seems an excellent man—uncommonly pleasing.”

“So do I,” Mrs. Palmer agreed. “He’s such a charming gentleman—it’s a pity he’s so serious and dull. Mama says *he* was in love with your sister too. Imagine—what a compliment! He hardly ever falls in love with anyone.”

Elinor hesitated. “Is Mr. Willoughby well known in your part of Somersetshire?”

“Oh, yes—extremely,” Mrs. Palmer replied. “Though not many people actually know him, since Combe Magna is so remote. But everyone thinks him extremely agreeable. Nobody dislikes him wherever he goes. Tell your sister that—she’s a lucky girl to get him, truly. Though he’s luckier still to have her, because she’s so beautiful and delightful—nothing could be good enough for her. Of course, I don’t think she’s any handsomer than you. I think you’re both exquisite—and so does Mr. Palmer, though he wouldn’t admit it last night.”

Mrs. Palmer’s words offered little substance, but any praise of Willoughby was welcome to Elinor.

“I’m so glad we’re finally acquainted,” Charlotte said, turning to Elinor. “Now I hope we’ll be great friends. I’ve longed to meet you! It’s such a joy that you’re living at the cottage—nothing could be better! And I’m so happy your sister is marrying well. I hope you’ll visit Combe Magna often. It’s said to be a lovely place.”

“You’ve known Colonel Brandon for a long time, haven’t you?” Elinor asked.

“Yes, a long while,” Charlotte replied. “Ever since my sister married. He was a close friend of Sir John’s. In fact,” she added softly, “I believe he would have liked to marry me—if he could. Sir John and Lady Middleton wanted it very much. But mama thought the match wasn’t good enough for me. Otherwise, Sir John would have mentioned it to the Colonel, and we’d have been married immediately.”

“Did Colonel Brandon know about Sir John’s proposal before it was made? Had he ever confessed his feelings to you?”

“Oh, no,” Charlotte said. “But if mama hadn’t objected, I’m sure he would have been overjoyed. He hadn’t seen me more than twice—before I left school. Still, I’m much happier as I am. Mr. Palmer is the kind of man I like.”

CHAPTER XXI. The Palmers returned to Cleveland the following day, leaving the families at Barton once more to their own company. But this quiet arrangement did not last long. Elinor had scarcely finished reflecting on her recent visitors—wondering how Charlotte could be so content without apparent cause, or why Mr. Palmer, though plainly capable and decently behaved, seemed so ill-matched to his wife—when Sir John and Mrs. Jennings, ever eager for social activity, introduced a new circle of acquaintances.

On a morning trip to Exeter, they had encountered two young ladies whom Mrs. Jennings proudly claimed as distant relatives. That was all the excuse Sir John needed: he promptly invited them to Barton Park as soon as their engagements in Exeter allowed. The invitation was accepted without hesitation, and Lady Middleton found herself thrown into mild distress upon hearing that she would soon host two strangers—women whose elegance and refinement she could not verify, despite her husband’s and mother’s assurances. Their being related only heightened her anxiety; and when Mrs. Jennings tried to comfort her by suggesting that cousins must endure one another’s foibles, it only made matters worse.

Yet, since the visit could not be avoided, Lady Middleton resigned herself with the grace of a well-bred woman, merely reminding her husband several times a day of his impulsive generosity.

The young ladies arrived—and to everyone’s relief, they were far from unrefined. Dressed neatly, courteous in manner, and clearly delighted by the grandeur of Barton Park, they quickly won over Lady Middleton. Their affection for children was especially pronounced, and before an hour had passed, her opinion of them had turned entirely favorable. She declared them “very agreeable girls indeed”—a compliment of such high praise in her vocabulary that it nearly amounted to rapture.

Sir John, buoyed by this approval, rushed off to the cottage to inform the Dashwood sisters of their arrival, declaring the Miss Steeles to be “the sweetest girls in the world.” But Elinor, who knew better than to trust such sweeping declarations, saw through the exaggeration. To her, the phrase “sweetest girls in the world” meant nothing—it could describe anyone, anywhere, under any circumstances.

Still, Sir John insisted the entire family come immediately to meet them. “Do come now,” he urged, “pray come—you must come—I declare you shall come! You can’t imagine how much you’ll like them. Lucy is monstrously pretty and so good-natured! The children are already clinging to her like old friends. And they’ve been dying to see you—they’ve heard you’re the most beautiful creatures in England, and I’ve told them it’s all true and then some.”

But Elinor and Marianne refused to go right away, offering only to call within a few days. Sir John left, baffled by their reluctance, and returned to boast again to the Miss Steeles about the charm of the Dashwoods—just as he had earlier boasted about them to the Dashwoods.

When the promised visit finally occurred, the sisters’ first impression of the eldest Miss Steele was unimpressive: she was nearly thirty, plain-faced, and lacked intelligence in her expression. But the younger sister, no more than twenty-three, struck them as genuinely attractive. Her features were delicate, her eyes sharp and lively, and her bearing quick and animated—though not elegant in the classical sense, she carried herself with a certain distinction.

Their manners were polished, and Elinor soon recognized a degree of tact in the way they flattered Lady Middleton. They lavished attention on her children, praising their beauty, indulging their whims, and adoring every little gesture. When not occupied with the children, they praised Lady Middleton’s latest dress, copied patterns from it, and expressed awe at her every movement.

Such sycophancy, however, met no resistance from Lady Middleton. A devoted mother, she was both demanding and gullible when it came to her children’s admiration. The Miss Steeles’ exaggerated devotion pleased her completely. She watched with pride as her youngest son pulled at one of the ladies’ ribbons, tugged at her hair, and stole her scissors—yet she saw only mutual affection. “John is in such spirits today!” she exclaimed as he threw her handkerchief out the window. “He’s full of monkey tricks!” Later, when William pinched Miss Steele’s finger, she added fondly, “How playful William is!”

Then came Annamaria, a three-year-old girl who had been unusually quiet. “Here is my sweet little Annamaria,” Lady Middleton said, cradling her. “She’s always so gentle and quiet—never was there such a quiet little thing!”
