But as she leaned in to kiss her daughter, a pin from her headband scratched the child’s neck. The result was immediate and violent—screams erupted louder than any noise the room had known in weeks.

Lady Middleton was horrified. But the Miss Steeles reacted with even greater alarm. Together, they sprang into action: one knelt beside the child, bathing the wound with lavender water, while the other stuffed her mouth with sugar plums. Still, the child shrieked on, kicking at her brothers and refusing any comfort—until Lady Middleton remembered a similar incident last week, when apricot marmalade had calmed a bruised temple. This time, the remedy was eagerly proposed. At the mention of it, the child paused briefly—giving hope that it might work.

She was carried out in her mother’s arms, followed by her two brothers, who insisted on joining despite her pleas. Left alone, the four women sat in sudden silence.

“Poor little creatures!” said Miss Steele. “It might have been a very sad accident.”

“But I hardly know how,” Marianne replied, “unless it had happened under different circumstances. This is just the usual way of inflating fear where there’s nothing real to fear.”

“What a sweet woman Lady Middleton is!” said Lucy Steele.

Marianne remained silent. She could not feign sentiment when none existed. The burden of polite falsehoods fell naturally to Elinor.

“She is pleasant enough,” Elinor said carefully, “though perhaps not quite as warm as Miss Lucy.”

“And Sir John too,” said the elder sister, “what a charming man he is!”

Elinor agreed simply: “He is very good-natured and friendly.”

“And what a delightful family they have!” Lucy added. “I never saw such fine children in my life. I quite doat upon them already. I’m always fond of children.”

“I should guess so,” Elinor replied with a smile, “from what I’ve seen this morning.”

Lucy continued, “I think children should be lively and spirited. I can’t stand those who are tame and quiet.”

Elinor answered gently, “While I’m here at Barton Park, I never find quiet children abhorrent.”

A brief silence followed. Then Miss Steele, eager to continue, asked abruptly, “And how do you like Devonshire, Miss Dashwood? Were you sorry to leave Sussex?”

Elinor was taken aback by the directness. “Yes,” she said. “Norland was a beautiful place, wasn’t it?”

“We’ve heard Sir John speak of it highly,” Lucy said, seemingly embarrassed by her sister’s boldness.

“I think anyone who has seen it must admire it,” Elinor replied. “Though no one can value its beauty as we do.”

“Did you have many smart beaux at Norland?” Miss Steele pressed. “I suppose there aren’t as many here. For my part, I think they’re always a great addition.”

Lucy looked ashamed. “Why should you think there aren’t as many genteel young men in Devonshire as in Sussex?”

“Oh, dear,” said the elder sister, “I don’t mean to say there aren’t. There are plenty in Exeter. But how could I know what kind of beaux there were near Norland? I only feared you might find Barton dull.”

“Young ladies may not care about beaux,” Lucy said, changing the subject. “But I think they’re very agreeable—if they dress well and behave properly. Though I can’t bear them dirty and untidy. Like Mr. Rose in Exeter—he’s a proper beau, clerk to Mr. Simpson—but if you meet him in the morning, he’s not fit to be seen.”

“And your brother,” she added, turning to Elinor, “was he a beau before he married? He was so rich, after all.”

Elinor smiled. “I don’t fully understand what a ‘beau’ means. But if he ever was one, he still is—there’s not a trace of change in him.”

“Oh, dear,” said Anne, “one never thinks of married men as beaux—they have other duties.”

“Lord!” cried her sister. “You talk of nothing but beaux—Miss Dashwood will think you think of nothing else.”

She then shifted to admiring the house and furnishings.

This glimpse was enough. Elinor found the eldest Miss Steele vulgar and intrusive, and though the younger sister had beauty and wit, she lacked genuine elegance and sincerity. Elinor left without desire to know them further.

Not so the Miss Steeles. They arrived from Exeter armed with admiration for Sir John, his family, and all his relations. Now, they lavished praise on the Dashwood sisters—calling them the most beautiful, elegant, accomplished, and delightful girls they’d ever met—and expressed eagerness to grow closer.

And grow closer they would. With Sir John firmly on their side, opposition was futile. An inevitable intimacy began—one marked by daily visits, shared rooms, and forced conversation. To Sir John, mere presence equaled friendship. As long as they were together, he believed they were intimate. He also took pleasure in sharing private details about his guests, hoping to break down barriers.

Within two meetings, the elder Miss Steele congratulated Elinor on her sister’s success in capturing “a very smart beau” since arriving at Barton.

“It’ll be a fine thing to marry so young,” she said. “And I hear he’s quite a beau—prodigiously handsome. I hope you’ll have as much luck soon—perhaps you already have someone in the corner.”

Elinor suspected Sir John would not hesitate to reveal his suspicions about her feelings for Edward—especially since it had become his favorite joke. Since Edward’s visit, he had toasted her affections at every dinner, winking and nodding until the whole table noticed. The letter “F” had become a running gag, endlessly joked about until it was hailed as the wittiest letter in the alphabet.

Now, the Miss Steeles were privy to these jokes. The elder sister grew curious about the gentleman’s name—though Elinor expected no subtlety from her.

But Sir John did not keep it long. “His name is Ferrars,” he whispered loudly. “But don’t tell anyone—it’s a great secret.”

“Ferrars!” repeated Miss Steele. “Mr. Ferrars? Your sister-in-law’s brother, Miss Dashwood? A very agreeable young man, I know him well.”

“How can you say that, Anne?” Lucy interjected. “We’ve seen him once or twice at my uncle’s. It’s rather too much to pretend we know him well.”

Elinor listened closely. Who was this uncle? Where did he live? How had they met?

She wished the conversation would continue—but it didn’t. For the first time, she felt Mrs. Jennings strangely indifferent to gossip.

And yet, Miss Steele’s casual familiarity with Edward unsettled her. It sounded less like admiration and more like concealed disapproval. Was she aware of something troubling about him?

But no further mention was made. The name Ferrars faded from the conversation, leaving Elinor with unanswered questions—and growing unease.

Marianne, who had never been known for her patience with anything resembling impertinence, vulgarity, or a lack of refinement, was now especially unkindly disposed—her spirits too low, her heart too full—to welcome the Miss Steeles or to encourage their attempts at friendship. Her constant coolness toward them discouraged any effort on their part to grow closer, and Elinor, observing this, soon noticed how the sisters began to turn their attention more toward her. Lucy, in particular, seemed determined to win her favor, seeking every opportunity to draw her into conversation, sharing her thoughts with an ease and openness that made their acquaintance feel less like a formal exchange and more like a genuine connection.

Lucy was naturally quick-witted; her observations were often sharp and amusing, and for brief moments, Elinor found her pleasant company. Yet beneath this charm lay a troubling lack of education. She was ignorant of the world’s most basic knowledge, unrefined in speech and manner, and though she tried earnestly to appear refined, her deficiencies could not be hidden. Elinor pitied her—the waste of natural talent left untended by learning—but she also saw something far deeper: a lack of moral sincerity. The way Lucy flattered, fawned, and sought approval at the Park revealed a mind without delicacy, without integrity. To be near someone so clever yet so dishonest, so eager to impress while lacking honesty, filled Elinor with unease. How could she truly enjoy the company of one whose ignorance kept them from equal conversation, and whose conduct rendered every kind word or gesture meaningless?

One day, as they walked together from the park to the cottage, Lucy broke the silence with a question that startled Elinor.

“You’ll think my question strange, I’m sure,” Lucy said, “but do you know Mrs. Ferrars personally? Edward’s mother?”

Elinor did think it odd—and her expression betrayed her surprise—as she replied, “I’ve never seen her.”

“Indeed?” Lucy said, her eyes narrowing slightly. “I thought you might have met her at Norland. Then perhaps you don’t know what sort of woman she is?”

“I know nothing of her,” Elinor answered carefully, unwilling to share her true feelings about Edward’s family, and not inclined to satisfy what seemed like idle curiosity.

“I must seem very strange to you,” Lucy continued, watching her closely. “But there may be reasons—I wish I could tell you. Still, I hope you’ll believe I mean no harm.”

Elinor gave a polite reply, and they walked in silence for a few minutes. Then Lucy spoke again, hesitantly but with growing urgency.

“I can’t bear the idea of you thinking me prying. I’d rather suffer anything than lose your good opinion. And I wouldn *’t* trust anyone else—no, I’d want your advice if I were in such a difficult situation. But I won’t trouble you. I’m sorry you don’t happen to know Mrs. Ferrars.”

“I am sorry I don’t,” Elinor said, stunned. “If it mattered to you, I suppose. But I didn’t realize you had any connection to the family at all. I’m surprised you’d ask so seriously about her.”

“I know you are,” Lucy said gently. “But if I could tell you everything, you wouldn’t be so shocked. Mrs. Ferrars means nothing to me now—but someday, perhaps soon, we might be very close indeed. It depends entirely on her.”

She looked down, blushing, but cast a fleeting glance at Elinor to gauge her reaction.

“Good heavens!” Elinor exclaimed. “What do you mean? Are you acquainted with Mr. Robert Ferrars? Could you possibly be?”

She felt no warmth at the thought of such a sister-in-law.

“No,” Lucy replied, “not Robert Ferrars. I’ve never even seen him. But,” she said, fixing her gaze on Elinor, “his eldest brother.”

At that moment, Elinor felt a surge of disbelief so strong it nearly overwhelmed her. She turned to Lucy, silent and amazed, unable to grasp why such a claim would be made—or what purpose it served. Her face flushed, but her mind remained firm in denial. She felt no fainting sickness, no wave of hysteria—only a cold, hard certainty that this could not be true.

“You may well be surprised,” Lucy went on. “You couldn’t have guessed. He never mentioned it to you or anyone—because it’s always been meant to be secret. And I’ve kept it faithfully, only telling Anne. I wouldn’t have told you at all, if I hadn’t trusted you completely. And I knew your questions about Mrs. Ferrars would seem odd, so I thought I should explain. I’m sure Mr. Ferrars won’t mind—he holds your family in the highest regard, and sees you all as his own.”

She paused, letting the words sink in.

Elinor said nothing for a moment. Her astonishment was too great. Then, forcing herself to speak calmly, she asked, “May I ask—how long has this engagement been going on?”

“Four years,” Lucy said quietly.

“Four years!” Elinor whispered, her voice barely steady.

“Yes,” Lucy confirmed.

Though deeply shaken, Elinor still refused to believe it. “I didn’t even know you were acquainted until recently.”

“Our acquaintance goes back many years,” Lucy said. “He was under my uncle’s care—Mr. Pratt. You’ve heard of him, haven’t you?”

“I think I have,” Elinor replied, summoning strength as her emotions surged. “He was with your uncle for four years—near Plymouth, at Longstaple. That’s where we first met. My sister and I often stayed with our uncle, and that’s where we became engaged—though not until a year after he left as a pupil. But he came back often after that.”

She sighed. “I didn’t want to enter into it without knowing his mother’s approval. But I was young—and I loved him too much to be wise.”

“You must have seen enough of him,” Lucy said, “to know he’s capable of making a woman truly devoted.”

“Certainly,” Elinor murmured, though her mind was racing. Then, with renewed confidence in Edward’s honor and her own judgment, she added, “Engaged to Mr. Edward Ferrars? I confess I’m utterly astonished. There must be some mistake—perhaps of name or person. We cannot mean the same man.”

“We mean no other,” Lucy said, smiling. “Edward Ferrars, eldest son of Mrs. Ferrars of Park Street, brother to your sister-in-law, Mrs. John Dashwood—that is the man I mean. I wouldn’t be mistaken about the man on whom all my happiness depends.”

“It’s strange,” Elinor said, her voice trembling, “that I never heard him mention your name.”

“That’s not surprising,” Lucy replied. “We’ve kept it secret. You didn’t know me or my family, so there was no reason to mention me. And he feared his sister might suspect—so he never spoke of it.”

They walked in silence for a moment.

Then Lucy pulled out a small miniature from her pocket. “To avoid any confusion, please look at this face. It doesn’t do him justice, but I’m sure you’ll recognize him. I’ve had it over three years.”

Elinor took it. As she looked at the portrait, every doubt vanished. It was undeniably Edward’s face. She returned it quickly, acknowledging the likeness.

“I’ve never been able to give him my picture in return,” Lucy said, “which troubles me greatly. He’s always wanted it. But I’ll have it painted the very first chance I get.”

“You’re right to wait,” Elinor said, her voice calm but her heart breaking.

They walked a few steps in silence before Lucy spoke again.

“I know you’ll keep this secret,” she said. “You must understand how important it is—not to let his mother find out. She’d never approve. I have no fortune, and I believe she’s extremely proud.”

“I didn’t seek your confidence,” Elinor said, “but I assure you, it is safe with me. Still, I must admit—this revelation seems unnecessary. Your knowing me shouldn’t make the secret safer.”

As she spoke, she studied Lucy’s face, hoping to catch a flicker of falsehood. But Lucy’s expression remained unchanged.

“I feared you’d think I was overstepping,” she said. “I’ve known you by reputation for a long time, and when I saw you, I felt like an old friend. Besides, after asking so many questions about Mrs. Ferrars, I thought I owed you an explanation. And I have no one else to confide in. Anne knows—but she has no sense. She’d ruin everything. I was terrified when Sir John mentioned Edward’s name—I thought she’d blurt it out.”

She dabbed her eyes with her handkerchief.

“I wonder sometimes,” she said, after wiping her tears, “if we should end it altogether.”

She looked directly at Elinor.

“But then I can’t bring myself to do it. I couldn’t bear to see him so miserable. And for myself—how could I live without him? What would you do, Miss Dashwood? What would you advise?”

“I cannot advise you,” Elinor said, startled. “This is not a matter for advice. Only your own judgment can guide you.”

After a pause, Lucy said, “Of course, his mother will have to provide for him eventually. But poor Edward—how low-spirited he is! Did you notice how depressed he seemed when he visited Barton?”

“He was miserable,” Elinor admitted. “Especially when he left us.”

“Ah, yes,” Lucy said. “He’d just spent two weeks with us at Longstaple. I begged him to cheer up—for fear you’d guess what was wrong. But it broke his heart to leave so soon. And seeing me so upset—poor fellow!”

She pulled out a letter and casually showed it to Elinor.

“He writes in terrible spirits,” she said. “I received this just before leaving Exeter. You know his handwriting, I’m sure—it’s lovely. But this one isn’t quite as neat. He must have been tired—he filled the page completely.”

Elinor looked at the writing. It was unmistakably Edward’s. This was not a painting or a rumor—this was proof. A correspondence between them could only exist under a formal engagement. For a moment, she felt her world collapse—her heart sank, her breath caught. But she forced herself to stand firm. She could not fall apart. Not now.

“Writing to each other,” Lucy said, tucking the letter away, “is all we have during these long separations. I have his picture. Poor Edward has nothing—except a lock of my hair set in a ring. I gave it to him last time he was at Longstaple. He said it helped, but not as much as a portrait. Perhaps you noticed the ring when you saw him?”

“I did,” Elinor said, her voice steady, though her soul was in torment.

She remembered the ring—remembered Edward’s quiet sadness, his evasiveness about his time at Plymouth. Now, everything made sense. And everything was ruined.

They reached the cottage. The Miss Steeles lingered briefly, then returned to the Park. Elinor remained alone.

And then, at last, she allowed herself to grieve.

CHAPTER XXIII. Though Elinor had never placed much trust in Lucy’s truthfulness, she could no longer doubt her now—especially when the weight of evidence was so overwhelming and the motive for lying so utterly absent. Lucy’s claim, supported by undeniable facts—their early acquaintance at Mr. Pratt’s house, Edward’s melancholy visit near Plymouth, his restless uncertainty, his inconsistent behavior toward her, the intimate knowledge the Miss Steeles had of Norland and their family, not to mention the portrait, the letter, and the ring—formed a chain of proof too strong to ignore. It was not mere suspicion; it was certainty. Edward had deceived her. And though the sting of betrayal burned fiercely at first, Elinor soon found herself caring more for him than for herself.

Had he meant to mislead her? Had he pretended affection he did not feel? Was his promise to Lucy one born of love? She doubted it. Whatever it had once been, it was no longer real. His heart belonged to her—she knew that with absolute conviction. Their shared history, the quiet glances, the unspoken understanding at Norland, the way even Fanny Dashwood had noticed his regard—none of it was illusion. He loved her. That belief softened the wound, making forgiveness almost possible.

Yet how deeply he had wronged himself! While she might heal, what future awaited him? Could he truly be happy with Lucy Steele? A woman whose mind was narrow, whose charm was superficial, whose character lacked depth? The boyish infatuation of nineteen might have blinded him to her flaws, but four years of maturity should have revealed them. Meanwhile, Lucy, removed from better company and immersed in trivial pursuits, may have lost the gentle simplicity that once made her appealing. Her beauty remained, but without grace or substance, it would soon fade into emptiness.

And now, instead of marrying a woman of equal standing and spirit, Edward faced an engagement to someone far beneath him in fortune and connection. If before, his mother’s opposition had seemed daunting, it was nothing compared to the resistance such a match would provoke. Yet if Edward’s heart was already turned away from Lucy, perhaps even the thought of familial disapproval brought him some comfort—a strange relief in his sorrow.

As these thoughts followed one another like shadows across her mind, Elinor wept—not for herself, but for him. She clung to the certainty that she had done nothing to deserve this pain, and that Edward, despite his fault, had not forfeited her respect. With that inner strength, she managed to conceal her anguish from her family. When she joined them at dinner just two hours after learning the truth, no trace of her suffering showed. To all eyes, she appeared calm, composed—while within, her soul was breaking.

She had chosen to keep the secret. Not because she feared their judgment, but because she knew they would grieve too deeply, and worse, condemn Edward out of love for her. Their sorrow would only deepen her own. She needed no counsel, no praise, no pity. Alone, she was stronger. Her reason held firm, her composure unwavering—though the wound was fresh and deep.

Still, she longed to speak with Lucy again. Not out of bitterness, but to understand. She wanted to hear the full story once more, to gauge the sincerity behind Lucy’s claims of devotion. And above all, she wished to show Lucy—through her calmness, her restraint—that she was not wounded, not jealous, not consumed by passion. She feared that her earlier agitation had made Lucy think otherwise. And indeed, Lucy’s jealousy was likely. Edward had always spoken of Elinor with admiration—not just in words, but in actions: trusting her with a secret so intimate, revealing it so soon. Even Sir John’s teasing remark carried weight.

But Elinor understood Lucy’s motives. Her confession was not kindness—it was strategy. To warn Elinor off, to assert her own claim. And Elinor saw through it. Yet she resolved to act honorably. She would avoid Edward, suppress her feelings, and remain true to her principles. But she would also prove to Lucy that her heart was not broken.

She was ready to face the conversation again. There was no new pain left to endure. So when the opportunity arose, she seized it.

The weather kept them indoors, and meetings at the park or cottage offered little privacy. They gathered for meals, laughter, games—no room for serious talk. Then came Sir John’s invitation: Lady Middleton, lonely without him, begged them to dine. Elinor saw a chance. Without her husband’s boisterous energy, the gathering might be quieter, more suited to quiet conversation. She accepted. Margaret followed, Marianne reluctantly agreed under maternal pressure, and they all went.

The evening was as dull as expected. No spark of thought or feeling stirred. The children joined them in the drawing room, and Elinor realized any attempt to speak privately would fail. Only when tea was cleared and cards were laid out did hope return.

Lady Middleton, noticing Lucy’s embroidery, said, “I’m glad you’re not finishing Annamaria’s basket tonight. Candlelight must strain your eyes. We’ll make up for it tomorrow.”

Lucy replied quickly, “You’re mistaken, Lady Middleton. I’ve been waiting to see if you need me at the table. I’d never disappoint the dear child. If you do want me now, I’ll finish it after supper.”

“Ah, how kind,” said Lady Middleton. “Would you ring for working candles? Poor Annamaria would be heartbroken if it weren’t done.”

Lucy moved her worktable at once, smiling brightly. The others drew cards for Casino. Marianne, ever impervious to social norms, declared, “Your Ladyship will excuse me—I detest cards. I shall go to the piano-forte. I haven’t played since it was tuned.” And she walked away without a glance back.

Lady Middleton looked relieved. “Marianne can never stay away from that instrument,” Elinor said gently. “And I don’t blame her—it’s the finest piano I’ve ever heard.”

Then, as the game began, Elinor added, “If I cut out, perhaps I could help Miss Steele with her papers. There’s so much still to do on the basket—surely she can’t finish alone. I’d enjoy helping.”

“Oh, I’d be most grateful!” Lucy exclaimed. “There’s more work than I thought. It would be dreadful to let poor Annamaria down.”

“Oh, indeed!” said Miss Steele. “How I adore that little angel!”

Elinor smiled. “Then I’ll wait until the next rubber,” she said, choosing to delay her turn.

Lady Middleton approved. “Very kind. You may take your chance later.”

Thus, Elinor gained her seat beside Lucy, both focused on the delicate filigree work. The piano nearby played its soft, distracting melody. And as Marianne lost herself in music and memory, Elinor judged the moment right. She could speak—without fear of being overheard—and begin the conversation she had waited for.

Elinor began with a steady, though measured tone. “I would be unworthy of the trust you’ve placed in me if I didn’t wish for it to continue—or if I weren’t still curious about what lies behind it. So I won’t apologize for bringing it up again.”

“Thank you,” Lucy replied warmly, her voice brightening. “You’ve eased my mind—because somehow, I’d begun to fear I’d offended you when I told you that Monday.”

“Offended me? How could you think such a thing?” Elinor said sincerely. “Nothing could be farther from my intention than to make you feel that way. Could you possibly have any reason to confide in me that wouldn’t be both an honor and a compliment?”

“And yet,” Lucy answered, her sharp eyes gleaming with quiet meaning, “I felt there was a coldness in your manner—something displeased—that made me uneasy. I was certain you were angry with me. Ever since, I’ve been blaming myself for daring to share such personal matters. But now I’m relieved to know it was only my own imagination, and that you truly don’t hold it against me. If you knew how much comfort it gave me to speak openly about something I carry in my heart every moment of every day, I’m sure your kindness would overlook everything else.”

“I can well believe it brought you relief,” Elinor said gently. “And I assure you, you’ll never regret having trusted me. Your situation is indeed unfortunate—you seem surrounded by obstacles, and you’ll need all the strength your love can give you to endure them. Mr. Ferrars, I understand, is entirely dependent on his mother.”

“He has only two thousand pounds of his own,” Lucy admitted. “To marry on that alone would be madness—though I wouldn’t hesitate to give up every other hope without a sigh. I’ve always lived with very little, and I could bear any hardship for Edward’s sake. But I love him too deeply to become the selfish cause of robbing him of what his mother might offer if he married someone she approves of. We must wait—perhaps many years. With almost any other man, that would be unbearable. But Edward’s love and faithfulness—I know nothing could ever take those away from me.”

“That certainty must mean everything to you,” Elinor said softly. “And he, no doubt, finds the same comfort in knowing yours remains unshaken. If your mutual devotion had weakened, as it so often does after long engagements, your plight would truly be pitiable.”

Lucy glanced up at her, but Elinor kept her expression carefully neutral, guarding against any hint of suspicion.

“Edward’s love,” Lucy continued, “has been tested thoroughly by our long separation—years, really—and it has held firm. I’d be unjust to question it now. I can honestly say he has never given me even a moment’s doubt.”

Elinor hesitated—was she meant to smile or sigh? She chose silence.

Lucy pressed on. “I confess I’m naturally jealous, especially given our different lives—his being so much more involved in society than mine, and us rarely seeing each other. That made me particularly alert. If he’d changed in the least when we met—any hesitation, any sadness I couldn’t explain, any talk of another woman, or less joy at Longstaple than before—I would have known instantly. I may not be especially observant in general, but in this case, I’d be impossible to fool.”

*All this,* Elinor thought, *is charming—but it means nothing to either of us.*

After a brief pause, she asked, “But what are your plans? Or do you have none beyond waiting for Mrs. Ferrars’s death—a prospect that feels grim and terrible? Is Edward truly willing to endure years of uncertainty rather than risk his mother’s anger by revealing the truth?”

“If we could be sure it would only be temporary!” Lucy sighed. “But Mrs. Ferrars is proud and stubborn. In her first rage, she might secure everything for Robert. The thought of that—especially for Edward’s sake—chills any desire I might have for hasty action.”

“And for your own sake, too,” Elinor added quietly. “Your selflessness seems to go beyond reason.”

Lucy looked at her, then fell silent.

“Do you know Mr. Robert Ferrars?” Elinor asked.

“No,” Lucy replied. “Never seen him. But I imagine he’s nothing like Edward—foolish and vain.”

“A great coxcomb!” Miss Steele exclaimed, catching the words during a sudden break in Marianne’s music. “Oh, they’re talking about their favorite gentlemen, I suppose.”

“No, sister,” Lucy said quickly, “you’re mistaken. Our favorite beaux are not coxcombs.”

“I can vouch for Miss Dashwood’s,” Mrs. Jennings laughed. “Her gentleman is one of the most modest, well-mannered young men I’ve ever met. But Lucy—well, she’s such a secretive little thing, who knows whom she likes?”

“Oh,” Miss Steele said, glancing around with a knowing look, “I daresay Lucy’s beau is just as modest and well-behaved as Miss Dashwood’s.”

Elinor flushed involuntarily. Lucy bit her lip and shot her sister a glare. A quiet tension settled over them.

At last, Lucy broke the silence, lowering her voice despite Marianne’s dramatic concerto filling the room. “I’ll tell you honestly about a plan I’ve recently considered—one that might help move things forward. In fact, I feel I must share it with you, since you’re directly involved. You’ve seen enough of Edward to know he’d prefer the church above all other professions. My idea is this: he should enter the clergy as soon as possible. Then, through your influence—your kindness toward him, and your regard for me—perhaps your brother could be persuaded to give him the Norland living. I understand it’s a good position, and the current holder isn’t expected to live long. That income would be enough for us to marry on, and we could leave the rest to time and fortune.”

“I’d always be glad,” Elinor replied, “to show my respect and friendship for Mr. Ferrars. But don’t you see that my influence would be entirely unnecessary? He is the brother of Mrs. John Dashwood—surely that alone would be enough recommendation to her husband.”

“But Mrs. John Dashwood wouldn’t approve of Edward taking holy orders,” Lucy said.

“Then I suspect my influence would be of little use,” Elinor replied calmly.

They sat in silence for several minutes. Then Lucy sighed deeply. “Perhaps the wisest course would be to end the engagement altogether. We’re trapped on every side. Though it would hurt us now, we might find greater happiness in the long run. But you won’t give me your advice, Miss Dashwood?”

“No,” Elinor answered, smiling faintly, though her heart stirred beneath the surface. “On such a matter, I certainly will not. You know well enough that my opinion would mean nothing to you unless it aligned with your own desires.”

“You misunderstand me,” Lucy said solemnly. “I value your judgment above all others. And I truly believe—if you were to say plainly, ‘I advise you to end your engagement with Edward Ferrars; it would bring greater happiness to both of you’—I would act on it immediately.”

Elinor blushed—not at the compliment, but at the insincerity of the woman who would soon be Edward’s wife. “That kind of praise terrifies me,” she said. “It places me in too powerful a position. To have the power to separate two people so deeply attached—it’s far too much for someone who is merely an outsider.”

“It’s precisely because you’re an outsider,” Lucy replied, a hint of irritation in her tone, “that your judgment would carry real weight. If you were biased by your own feelings, your opinion wouldn’t matter at all.”

Elinor decided not to respond—lest their conversation grow too intimate, too unrestrained. She resolved, silently, never to bring up the subject again.

Another long silence followed, until Lucy spoke once more. “Will you be in town this winter, Miss Dashwood?” she asked, regaining her usual composure.

“Certainly not,” Elinor replied.

“I’m sorry to hear that,” Lucy said, her eyes lighting up. “It would have been such a pleasure to meet you there! But I’m sure you’ll come anyway—your brother and sister will surely invite you.”

“It won’t be in my power to accept,” Elinor said.

“How unfortunate!” Lucy exclaimed. “I’d counted on seeing you. Anne and I are going to visit relatives in January—invited for years now. But I’m only going for Edward. He’ll be in London in February—otherwise, London holds no charm for me. I lack the spirit for it.”

Elinor was summoned to the card table as the first rubber ended, and thus the private conversation came to a close—neither woman reluctant, for nothing had been said to deepen their regard for one another. Elinor took her seat with a heavy heart, convinced that Edward was not only indifferent to the woman he was to marry, but also doomed to a marriage lacking even the chance of contentment. For only self-interest, she believed, could compel a woman to cling to an engagement she clearly saw as burdensome and unwanted.

From that point on, Elinor never brought up the subject again. When Lucy did—often, and with careful attention to detail, especially when sharing letters from Edward—Elinor responded with calm reserve, ending the conversation as politely as possible. Such exchanges, she felt, were indulgences neither deserved nor safe.

The Miss Steeles’ visit to Barton Park stretched far longer than originally intended. Their presence became increasingly welcome; Sir John refused to let them leave. Despite their commitments in Exeter and the urgent need to return each week, they stayed nearly two months—long enough to fully participate in the festive season, which demanded extra balls and grand dinners to mark its significance.

CHAPTER XXV. Though Mrs. Jennings usually spent much of the year with her children and friends, she had a home of her own—a modest house near Portman Square where she lived each winter since her husband’s passing. As January drew near, she began to think of returning there, and one day, without warning, invited the elder Misses Dashwood to join her in London. Elinor, not noticing the flush of excitement on Marianne’s face or the eager light in her eyes, quickly refused on behalf of both sisters, believing it reflected their shared desire. Her reason was simple: they could not leave their mother at such a time.

Mrs. Jennings blinked in surprise and repeated her invitation at once. “Oh, Lord! Your mother can spare you perfectly well,” she insisted, her voice warm with conviction. “And I do beg you’ll come—my heart is set on it. Don’t think I’ll make any trouble. Just sending Betty by coach will do, and I’m sure I can afford that. We three could fit nicely in my chaise. Once we’re in town, if you don’t care to go wherever I do, no matter—you can always go with one of my daughters. I’m certain your mother won’t mind. In fact, she’ll be pleased—after all, I’ve managed to get my own children off my hands, so she’ll trust me to look after you. And if I don’t see at least one of you safely married before I’m done with you, it won’t be for lack of trying. I’ll speak well of you to every young man I meet.”

Sir John chimed in, half-jokingly: “I suspect Miss Marianne wouldn’t mind at all—if only her sister would agree. It’s cruel to deny her pleasure just because Miss Dashwood isn’t keen. So I say, when you’re tired of Barton, just set off for town—no need to tell Miss Dashwood a thing.”

“Nonsense!” Mrs. Jennings laughed. “I’d love Miss Marianne’s company whether Miss Dashwood comes or not. The more, the merrier, I always say. And it’ll be better for them to go together—so if they tire of me, they can talk among themselves and laugh at my odd ways behind my back. But one or both—I must have at least one. Good heavens, how can I bear being alone? I’ve always had Charlotte with me this winter. Come now, Miss Marianne—let’s shake on it. If Miss Dashwood changes her mind later, so much the better.”

Marianne turned to her with sudden warmth. “I thank you sincerely, ma’am,” she said, her voice trembling slightly. “Your kindness moves me deeply. To accept your invitation would bring me almost the greatest joy I know. But my mother—my dear, dear mother—how can I leave her? Elinor is right. If my absence would cause her even a moment’s sorrow, I would not go. Not for anything in the world.”

Mrs. Jennings assured her again that Mrs. Dashwood could manage perfectly well without them. Elinor, now seeing clearly how completely Marianne’s heart was fixed on Willoughby, made no further resistance. She simply deferred the decision to their mother, though she doubted she would find support there. She knew her mother would favor anything that brought happiness to Marianne—and especially something that promised the chance to see Willoughby again. Elinor dared not explain her own reasons for wanting to stay. It was painful enough to witness how Marianne, who had always been so sensitive and fastidious, could overlook Mrs. Jennings’ coarse manners, her loud laughter, her vulgar ways—all of which had once caused her such irritation. This blind devotion revealed just how deeply she was attached to one man, and it shook Elinor more than she cared to admit.

When Mrs. Dashwood heard of the invitation, she was delighted. She saw only the joy it would bring her daughters. “It’s exactly what I wanted,” she declared. “You’ll both benefit from it. Margaret and I will be so happy together—just books and music, quiet and peaceful. When you return, you’ll see how much Margaret has improved! I’ve even planned some small changes to your rooms—now’s the perfect time. You should go to London. Every young woman in your position should experience its society, its life. You’ll be under the care of a kind, motherly woman—I have no doubt about her goodness. And perhaps you’ll see your brother. Whatever his faults—or those of his wife—I cannot bear to have you estranged from him. He’s still Edward’s son, after all.”

Elinor hesitated. “Though you’re always so thoughtful for our happiness,” she said gently, “there is still one concern I cannot ignore.” Marianne’s face fell. “What is it?” asked Mrs. Dashwood, smiling. “Don’t tell me it’s about cost. That’s nonsense.”

“It’s not about money,” Elinor replied. “It’s that while I respect Mrs. Jennings’ intentions, I do not believe her company will add to our comfort, nor her presence give us any real standing.”

Her mother nodded thoughtfully. “True,” she said. “But you won’t be spending much time alone with her. You’ll often appear with Lady Middleton. That matters far more.”

“If Elinor fears Mrs. Jennings,” Marianne said lightly, “it need not stop me. I have no such worries. I can endure whatever discomforts come my way—easily.”

Elinor smiled faintly. She remembered how hard it had been to get Marianne to behave politely toward Mrs. Jennings before. Yet now, her sister seemed willing to overlook everything for the sake of a single hope. Still, Elinor decided: if Marianne went, she would go too. She could not leave her sister entirely to her own judgment, nor abandon Mrs. Jennings to Marianne’s unpredictable moods. Besides, Edward Ferrars, according to Lucy Steele, would not arrive in London until February. Their visit could end before he came—and that gave her some peace.

“I want you both to go,” Mrs. Dashwood insisted. “These objections are foolish. You’ll enjoy yourselves. Especially together. And Elinor—when you stop thinking so much, you might actually find pleasure in meeting your sister-in-law’s family.”

Elinor had long wished to lessen her mother’s faith in Edward’s affection for her. Now, though she knew it was hopeless, she tried once more. “I like Edward very much,” she said calmly, “and I’ll always be glad to see him. But the rest of the family—it makes no difference to me whether I ever meet them.”

Mrs. Dashwood smiled and said nothing. Marianne looked up in surprise. Elinor sighed inwardly—she might as well have kept silent.

After little more discussion, the plan was settled. The invitation was accepted. Mrs. Jennings rejoiced, promising endless kindness and attention. It wasn’t just her joy—Sir John was thrilled, for the idea of two new people in London eased his dread of loneliness. Even Lady Middleton feigned delight, though it was an effort. And the Miss Steeles—especially Lucy—were beside themselves with happiness.

Elinor submitted to the decision with less resistance than she expected. For herself, it mattered little whether she went or stayed. But seeing her mother so pleased, and Marianne radiant with anticipation—her spirits lifted, her voice bright, her whole manner transformed—she found it hard to regret the choice. Marianne’s joy was almost overwhelming, a wild ecstasy that left her breathless with impatience. Only her deep love for her mother held her back, and at parting, her grief was profound. Her mother wept nearly as much. Elinor alone seemed to understand this separation might not be forever.

They left in the first week of January. The Middletons were to follow shortly. The Miss Steeles remained at the park, waiting to depart with the rest of the family.

CHAPTER XXVI. Elinor sat beside Mrs. Jennings in the carriage, journeying to London as her guest, and could not help marveling at how swiftly their lives had changed. Just days before, she had been skeptical of such an arrangement—so brief their acquaintance, so different their temperaments, so contrary her own inclinations—but now, swept along by Marianne’s fervent hope and her mother’s eager consent, she found herself en route to a city that promised both uncertainty and possibility. Though she still harbored quiet doubts about Willoughby’s sincerity, she could not deny the radiant joy that lit Marianne’s face, the way her eyes sparkled with anticipation, the very breath she took seemed charged with longing. In contrast, Elinor felt only a hollow stillness within her own heart, a loneliness that deepened each time she saw her sister’s delight. If only she too could have such a reason to hope, such a passion to chase—she would gladly bear every worry, every fear, just to feel that kind of fire.

They traveled for three days, and during the journey, Marianne remained mostly silent, lost in thought, speaking only when some beautiful landscape stirred her soul into sudden exclamation—always directed at Elinor, never at Mrs. Jennings. To balance this reserved behavior, Elinor made it her duty to be warm and attentive to their hostess. She chatted, laughed, and listened whenever Mrs. Jennings spoke, showing kindness in every gesture. In return, Mrs. Jennings doted on them both, fussing over their comfort, insisting on their preferences at every inn, though frustrated that they wouldn’t reveal whether they favored salmon or cod, fowl or cutlets.

On the third day, they arrived in London by three o’clock, relieved to step from the carriage and into the warmth of a real fire. Their lodgings were pleasant—a well-appointed room once occupied by Charlotte, whose artistic touch lingered in the silk-embroidered landscape above the mantel, a reminder of years spent in a grand school in town. Dinner was not ready for two hours, so Elinor decided to write home to her mother. As she began, she noticed Marianne doing the same.

“I’m writing to Mama,” Elinor said gently. “Wouldn’t you like to wait a day?”

“No,” Marianne replied quickly, almost sharply, as if wanting to end the conversation. Her tone told Elinor everything: she was not writing to their mother. She was writing to Willoughby.

The realization struck Elinor like a quiet certainty: they must be engaged. The secrecy, the haste, the urgency—it all pointed to a secret bond. Though the thought brought no peace, it stirred something deeper—an ache of longing, yes, but also a flicker of hope for her sister’s happiness. With renewed purpose, Elinor continued her letter.

Marianne finished hers in minutes—short, folded, sealed, and addressed with trembling hands. Elinor caught sight of a large "W" on the envelope. No sooner was it done than Marianne rang for the footman and sent it off by the two-penny post. There was no doubt now.

Her spirits soared, yet beneath them lay a nervous flutter that unsettled Elinor. As evening fell, the tension grew. Marianne barely touched her dinner. Back in the drawing room, she leaned forward, ears straining at every sound from the street. Each carriage passing outside sent her heart racing.

Elinor was grateful that Mrs. Jennings was occupied in her own room, unaware of the storm brewing in her daughter’s mind. Tea was served, and again, Marianne jumped at the sound of a knock—only to find it came from a neighbor’s door. But then, a louder, unmistakable rap echoed through the hall. This one could belong to no other.

Elinor knew instantly it was him. Marianne sprang up, rushing toward the door. Silence followed—then she returned, breathless, eyes wide with emotion. “Oh, Elinor,” she breathed, “it’s Willoughby! It really is!”

She turned, ready to throw her arms around him—only to see Colonel Brandon standing there instead.

The shock was too much. Marianne fled from the room without a word.

Elinor, too, felt a pang of disappointment. Yet she could not help but respect Colonel Brandon. His presence brought no joy to her sister, and she regretted that he might sense her grief. He looked after Marianne with concern, his expression softening with surprise and sorrow. When he turned back to Elinor, he asked quietly, “Is your sister unwell?”

Elinor hesitated, then offered excuses—headache, fatigue, low spirits. He listened intently, then composed himself, shifting the conversation to pleasantries about their journey and their friends. They spoke politely, but neither truly heard the other. Elinor longed to ask if Willoughby were in town, but feared it would wound him. Instead, she ventured, “Have you been here since I last saw you?”

“Yes,” he replied, slightly uneasy. “Almost constantly. I’ve visited Delaford a few times, but never returned to Barton.”

His words stirred memories—the abruptness of his departure, the unease it caused Mrs. Jennings. Elinor winced, realizing her question had carried more weight than she’d intended.

Mrs. Jennings soon joined them, bursting in with her usual cheer. “Colonel! How lovely to see you! I’ve been so busy settling things—Cartwright, the house, the servants—you know how it is!” She chattered on, asking about his life, his business, his travels. He answered with quiet dignity, but gave little away.

Elinor began making tea, and Marianne reappeared, pale but composed. Colonel Brandon became quieter, more distant. Mrs. Jennings pressed him to stay longer, but he excused himself shortly after.

No one else came that evening. The girls agreed unanimously to go to bed early.

The next morning, Marianne woke refreshed, her face bright with expectation. The night’s disappointment seemed forgotten, replaced by the thrill of what might come today.

Breakfast was barely over when Mrs. Palmer’s barouche pulled up at the door. Moments later, she burst into the room, laughing, full of joy and contradictions—delighted to see them, annoyed they hadn’t accepted her invitation first, furious they’d taken her mother’s place, yet unable to forgive them if they hadn’t come at all.

“Mr. Palmer will be so thrilled!” she declared. “I can’t remember what he said when he heard you were coming—but it was hilarious!”

After an hour of lively chatter—mostly inquiries from Mrs. Jennings and giggles from Mrs. Palmer—Mrs. Palmer suggested they go shopping. Both Mrs. Jennings and Elinor agreed readily; they had errands of their own. Marianne hesitated, then relented.

Everywhere they went, Marianne’s gaze darted around, searching. In Bond Street especially, her eyes scanned the crowds, her thoughts far from the silks and trinkets displayed before her. She offered no opinion on purchases, showed no interest in anything, only grew more restless, impatient to return home. Mrs. Palmer, captivated by every new thing, dawdled endlessly, leaving Marianne seething with frustration.

By late morning, they returned. No sooner had they stepped inside than Marianne dashed upstairs. When Elinor followed, she found her sister turning from the table, her face heavy with sorrow.

“Has any letter been left for me?” Marianne asked the footman, who entered with the parcels.

He shook his head. “None, ma'am.”

“Are you certain? No servant? No porter?”

“Quite certain.”

“How odd,” she murmured, voice low and broken, as she walked to the window.

“How odd indeed,” Elinor whispered to herself, watching her sister with growing anxiety. *If she believed Willoughby was in town, why write to him? Why not send a note to Combe Magna? And if he is here… why hasn’t he come? Why hasn’t he written?*

Her heart ached. She thought of her mother’s indulgence, the secrecy, the reckless trust placed in a man so unknown. *This cannot continue.* She resolved, after careful thought, that if this silence persisted, she would speak plainly to her mother—insist on knowing the truth, demand answers. For Marianne’s sake, she would do whatever was necessary.

That evening, Mrs. Palmer dined with them, along with two elderly acquaintances of Mrs. Jennings. After tea, Mrs. Palmer left for her engagements, and Elinor helped set up a whist table. Marianne refused to play—she had never learned—and though she had free time, the evening offered no relief. She tried reading, but soon tossed the book aside. Instead, she paced the room, pausing at the window every few moments, listening, hoping, waiting—for a knock that never came.

“Should this pleasant weather continue much longer,” Mrs. Jennings remarked at breakfast the next morning, “Sir John will be loath to leave Barton next week. It’s such a pity for sportsmen to miss a day of sport. Poor things! I always feel for them when they do—they take it so deeply to heart.”

“That’s true,” Marianne replied cheerfully, stepping toward the window to gaze out at the sky. “I hadn’t thought of that. This kind of weather will keep many hunters in the country.”

It was a fortunate thought—her spirits instantly lifted. “It’s lovely weather for them, indeed,” she continued, settling into her seat with a bright smile. “How they must enjoy it! But”—a flicker of unease crossed her face—“it won’t last long. At this time of year, after all that rain, we can’t expect much more of it. Frost will soon come, and likely with force. Perhaps in another day or two; this warmth can’t endure much longer—nay, it might even freeze tonight!”

“At any rate,” Elinor said quickly, hoping to divert Mrs. Jennings from reading too closely into her sister’s thoughts, “I’m sure Sir John and Lady Middleton will be in town by the end of next week.”

“Oh, yes, my dear,” Mrs. Jennings assured her. “I’ll wager they will. Mary always gets her own way.”

And now, Elinor silently mused, “She’ll write to Combe today, no doubt.”

But if she did, the letter was sent off so quietly that Elinor could not catch sight of it. Whatever the truth, though she remained far from content, seeing Marianne in high spirits eased her own unease. And Marianne *was* in high spirits—delighted by the mildness of the air, and even more thrilled by the prospect of frost.

The morning passed mostly in calling on Mrs. Jennings’s acquaintances to announce their arrival in town. Marianne spent every moment watching the wind, studying the clouds, sensing the subtle shifts in the atmosphere. “Don’t you feel it’s colder than earlier?” she asked Elinor. “There’s a definite change. I can barely warm my hands in my muff. It wasn’t like this yesterday. Look—the clouds are parting. The sun will break through soon. We’ll have a clear afternoon!”

Elinor found herself alternately amused and pained by her sister’s obsession. But Marianne persisted, finding signs of approaching frost in every crackling fire and every morning breeze.

The Dashwood sisters had little reason to complain about Mrs. Jennings’s lifestyle or circle of friends. Her hospitality was generous, her manners kind, and she carefully avoided introducing them to anyone who might unsettle their sensibilities. Elinrna, grateful for this comfort, accepted the limitations of their evenings—most of which revolved around cards—with quiet resignation.

Colonel Brandon, who had been invited regularly, visited nearly every day. He came to see Marianne and talk with Elinor, whose conversations with him were often the most meaningful part of her day. Yet she could not help noticing how deeply he seemed to care for her sister—how his eyes lingered on Marianne, how his mood grew heavier since leaving Barton. She feared his feelings were only growing stronger.

About a week after their arrival, it became clear that Willoughby had also reached London. His card lay on the table when they returned from their morning drive.

“Good heavens!” Marianne exclaimed. “He’s been here while we were out!”

Elinor, relieved to know he was in town, ventured, “I’m certain he’ll call again tomorrow.”

But Marianne barely heard her. As Mrs. Jennings entered, she snatched up the card and retreated with it.

This news stirred Elinor’s hopes—and reignited Marianne’s restless longing. From that moment, her mind was never still. She waited for him every hour, unable to focus on anything else. The next morning, she insisted on staying behind while the others went out.

Elinor’s thoughts raced—what was happening in Berkeley Street? But one glance at her sister upon their return told her: Willoughby had not returned.

Just then, a note arrived and was placed on the table.

“For me!” Marianne cried, rushing forward.

“No, ma'am,” the servant corrected. “For my mistress.”

Marianne, unconvinced, seized it anyway. “It *is* for Mrs. Jennings—how annoying!”

“You’re expecting a letter?” Elinor asked, unable to stay silent.

“Yes—just a little—not much.”

After a pause, Elinor softly said, “You have no confidence in me, Marianne.”

“Nay, Elinor,” Marianne replied sharply, “this accusation from *you*—you who trust no one?”

“Me?” Elinor stammered, embarrassed. “Indeed, Marianne, I have nothing to conceal.”

“Nor I,” Marianne declared with conviction. “Our situations are alike. We have neither of us anything to tell—you because you don’t share, and I because I hide nothing.”

Elinor, wounded by the charge of secrecy, felt helpless to press further. Mrs. Jennings soon appeared, and the note was handed over. She read aloud:

> *Dear Mother,*
> *We arrived in Conduit Street last night. We hope you and your dear cousins will join us this evening. Sir John has business, and I am suffering from a violent cold, so we could not call on you.*
> *With love,*
> *Lady Middleton*

The invitation was accepted. But as the appointed hour approached, Elinor struggled to persuade her sister to go. Marianne still hadn’t seen Willoughby, and though she wasn’t eager for company, she dreaded missing him should he come again while she was away.

That evening, Elinor discovered that changing homes doesn’t change human nature. Despite being newly settled, Sir John had already gathered nearly twenty young people for a ball—a gathering Lady Middleton disapproved of. In the country, such an impromptu dance was acceptable, but in London, where elegance mattered and reputations were fragile, hosting a small affair with just two violins and a sideboard supper risked ridicule.

Mr. and Mrs. Palmer were among the guests. Mr. Palmer, who had taken care to avoid appearing attentive to his mother-in-law, gave them only a brief nod from across the room. Marianne glanced once around the hall—then sat down, clearly uninterested in pleasure or conversation.

After about an hour, Mr. Palmer drifted over. “I didn’t expect to see you both in town,” he said. “Colonel Brandon told me first, and I made a joke when I heard you were coming.”

“I thought you were both in Devonshire,” he added.

“Did you?” Elinor replied. “When do you return?”

“I don’t know.”

Their exchange ended there.

Never before had Marianne been so reluctant to dance—or so exhausted by it. On the way back, she complained bitterly.

“Aye, aye,” Mrs. Jennings chuckled. “We know why. If a certain person—let’s say no names—had been there, you wouldn’t have tired at all. And honestly, it wasn’t very polite of him not to meet you, even though he was invited.”

“Invited?” Marianne gasped.

“So my daughter Middleton told me,” Mrs. Jennings explained. “Sir John met him this morning in the street.”

Marianne said nothing—but her face flushed with hurt.

Elinor, desperate to ease her sister’s torment, decided the next morning to write to their mother. She hoped to stir her fears for Marianne’s health and finally prompt the inquiries that had long been delayed. Her resolve deepened when she saw Marianne writing—surely to Willoughby.

Later that day, Mrs. Jennings left for errands, and Elinor began her letter immediately. Marianne, restless and anxious, paced between windows or sat by the fire lost in thought.

Elinor wrote with urgency, detailing everything she suspected—Willoughby’s shifting affections—and pleaded with her mother to ask Marianne directly about her relationship with him. Duty and love demanded it.

She had scarcely finished when a knock announced a visitor. Colonel Brandon.

Marianne, having seen him from the window, fled the room before he entered.

He looked unusually grave. Though he expressed relief at finding Elinor alone—as if he had something important to say—he sat in silence for several minutes.

Elinor, convinced he had something urgent to tell her about her sister, waited anxiously.

At last, he spoke, his voice trembling slightly. “When may I congratulate you on gaining a brother?”

Elinor was stunned. “I don’t understand,” she said simply.

He tried to smile. “Your sister’s engagement to Mr. Willoughby is widely known.”

“It cannot be,” Elinor protested. “Not even our family knows.”

He looked surprised. “I beg your pardon—I fear I’ve intruded. But I assumed no secrets were meant, since they correspond openly, and their marriage is the talk of everyone.”

“Who has spoken of it?” Elinor asked.

“Many—some you don’t know, others close to you: Mrs. Jennings, Mrs. Palmer, the Middletons. Still, I might have doubted… until today, when the servant let me in and I saw a letter in his hand—addressed to Mr. Willoughby, in your sister’s handwriting. I came to ask—but I knew already. Is it final? Is there no chance—?” He stopped himself. “I have no right. No hope. Excuse me, Miss Dashwood. I believe I’ve said too much. But I don’t know what to do. I rely on your wisdom. Tell me—it’s all settled? That concealment, if possible, is all that remains?”

These words revealed his love plainly. Elinor was deeply moved. She could not speak at first. When she recovered, she wrestled with how best to respond. She knew so little of the true state between Marianne and Willoughby—she might say too much or too little.

Yet she understood that Marianne’s devotion left no hope for Colonel Brandon. Still, to protect her sister from blame, she chose kindness over truth.

She said, “Though I’ve never been told directly, I have no doubt of their mutual affection. And their correspondence—well, I’m not surprised to hear of it.”

He listened in silence. Then rose, voice thick with emotion. “To your sister, I wish every happiness. To Mr. Willoughby, I pray he deserves her.”

And with that, he left.

Elinor was left not with comfort, but with a heavy sorrow—for Colonel Brandon’s pain, and for the certainty that nothing could heal it.

The days that followed brought no word or sign from Willoughby—no letter, no visit, no token of his presence. To Elinor, this silence was not a cause for regret, but rather a quiet confirmation of what she had already feared: that the affection once so freely given had faded, perhaps vanished entirely. She watched her sister closely, sensing the weight of unspoken sorrow pressing down upon Marianne like a shadow.

At last, an invitation arrived for a gathering hosted by Lady Middleton—a social event to which Mrs. Jennings could not attend due to her youngest daughter’s illness. Marianne prepared for the evening with little care, her movements slow and listless, as if she were merely going through the motions of dressing, not expecting joy or comfort from the occasion. Her appearance reflected her inner state: pale, withdrawn, untouched by the usual glow of anticipation. After tea, she sat motionless by the drawing-room fire, lost in thought, oblivious even to Elinor’s presence beside her. It wasn’t until Lady Middleton’s carriage was announced at the door that Marianne stirred—startled, as though she had forgotten the world beyond her own mind.

They arrived at the party on time, stepping out of their carriage into a throng of carriages and guests. The house was brilliantly lit, crowded with people, and stiflingly warm. After offering their polite curtsies to Lady Middleton, they were left to navigate the crush of conversation and heat. Eventually, Lady Middleton seated herself at a game of Cassino, and since Marianne showed no desire to mingle, Elinor managed to secure two chairs nearby. They settled quietly, observing the scene around them.

It was then that Elinor saw him—Willoughby—standing just a few yards away, engaged in earnest conversation with a young woman whose fashionable attire and poised manner marked her as someone of high society. He glanced up and caught Elinor’s eye. He bowed briefly, but made no attempt to approach or speak. His attention remained fixed on the woman beside him, though he could not have failed to notice Marianne sitting only a few feet away.

Elinor turned to her sister, hoping to gauge whether she had seen him. At that moment, Marianne looked up—and froze. A sudden flush swept across her face, her eyes brightening with hope. She rose instinctively, reaching out her hand as if to call him to her. But Elinor seized her arm gently yet firmly.

“Good heavens!” Marianne whispered, trembling. “He is here—he is here! Why doesn’t he look at me? Why won’t he speak?”

“Be still,” Elinor urged, her voice low and urgent. “Do not betray your feelings. Perhaps he has not seen you yet.”

But Elinor knew better. She saw the way his gaze flickered toward them, then quickly away. And when he finally turned again, it was too late. Marianne stood, breathless, calling out his name in a tone thick with emotion, her hand extended.

He stepped forward—not to her, but to Elinor. Addressing her with forced calm, he asked after Mrs. Dashwood, inquired how long they had been in town—his words clipped, hurried, avoiding any direct contact with Marianne. He spoke more to Elinor than to her sister, as though deliberately shielding himself from her gaze.

Elinor was stunned. She could not respond. But Marianne, unable to contain herself, burst forth. “Good God, Willoughby! What does this mean? Have you not received my letters? Will you not shake hands with me?”

He took her hand, but only for a second—his grip fleeting, almost painful. His face betrayed a struggle; his composure wavered. Then, after a pause, he regained control.

“I called at Berkeley Street last Tuesday,” he said steadily. “I was disappointed not to find you at home. I trust my card reached you?”

Marianne gasped. “Have you not received my notes? There must be some terrible mistake! Tell me—what is wrong? For heaven’s sake, tell me!”

He did not answer. His color deepened. He looked flustered, uneasy. But then, catching sight of the young woman he had been speaking with earlier, he straightened himself, composed his features, and replied coolly, “Yes, I received word of your arrival. Thank you for sending it.” With that, he bowed slightly and walked away.

Marianne sank into her chair, her face ashen, her body trembling. Elinor rushed to her side, fetching lavender water to revive her. As soon as she could speak, Marianne clutched at Elinor’s sleeve. “Go to him,” she pleaded. “Force him to come back. I must see him—must speak to him now. I cannot bear this silence. Something dreadful has happened—some terrible misunderstanding. Oh, go to him this instant!”

“How can I?” Elinor whispered. “No, dearest Marianne, you must wait. This is not the place. We will speak tomorrow—when we can be alone.”

But Marianne would not be calmed. She muttered broken phrases under her breath—words of grief, despair, disbelief—each one cutting deeper than the last. Soon, Elinor saw Willoughby leave the room, heading toward the staircase. She told Marianne he was gone, urging her to remain still, to endure until the next day.

But Marianne could not. “Take us home,” she begged. “I cannot stay another minute. I am too miserable.”

Lady Middleton, mid-game, paused without hesitation. Upon hearing Marianne was unwell, she immediately handed off her cards and arranged for the carriage. Within moments, they were on their way back to Berkeley Street.

Not a word passed between them during the journey. Marianne sat silent, her face drawn, her spirit crushed. Only when they reached their rooms and Mrs. Jennings had not yet returned did Elinor dare to ease her sister into bed. Hartshorn brought a slight return of color, and slowly, Marianne drifted into sleep, asking only to be left alone.

Alone in the quiet room, Elinor sat and reflected. She no longer doubted that a serious connection had once existed between Willoughby and Marianne. And now, the coldness of his behavior left no room for denial—he had moved on. Not out of malice, perhaps, but certainly out of indifference. That he had once cared deeply, she could still believe. But that he no longer did—that was undeniable.

And yet, there had been something in his expression—something troubled, ashamed—even as he avoided her sister’s eyes. It suggested guilt, not cruelty. He had not played with her affections lightly; he had changed, perhaps, under pressure or convenience, but not without regret.

For Marianne, the pain was unbearable. The cruel meeting, the unanswered questions, the silence—it all pointed toward a final, irrevocable end. Elinor grieved for her sister, knowing the depth of her suffering. Yet her own heart, though touched by Edward’s absence, held a steadier resolve. She could still honor him, still cherish the bond they shared, even if fate kept them apart. But for Marianne—whose love had been so pure, so passionate—this rejection felt like a wound that might never heal.

The morning light struggled through the heavy clouds of a January day, cold and dull, as Marianne sat half-dressed on a window seat, her face turned toward the weak glow filtering through the glass. Tears fell steadily onto the page before her, blurring her writing, yet she pressed on, driven by an urgency that left no room for hesitation. Elinor, awakened by the sound of her sister’s quiet sobs, found her there—hunched over the letter, trembling, her breath shallow and uneven. For a moment, Elinor watched in silence, her heart clenched with helpless sorrow. Then, softly, she asked, “Marianne, may I ask—?”

“No,” Marianne whispered, her voice flat and brittle, “ask nothing. You will soon know.”

That brief calm shattered instantly. A fresh wave of grief surged through her, and she buried her face in her hands, shoulders shaking. It took minutes to gather herself enough to write again. Each pause was marked by a sob, each line stained with tears. Elinor stood nearby, offering only quiet presence—no words, no comfort that might be mistaken for judgment. She knew this letter was likely the last Marianne would ever send to Willoughby.

When Marianne finally rose, still unsteady, she wandered the house like a ghost—avoiding eyes, seeking solitude, restless and broken. At breakfast, she refused food entirely. Elinor, aware of how much attention Mrs. Jennings might give to her sister’s distress, quietly drew the older woman into conversation, keeping her occupied with talk of wool and knitting patterns. The meal dragged on, and just as they settled around the work table, a servant delivered a letter to Marianne.

She snatched it from his hand, her face draining of color as if struck. Without a word, she fled the room.

Elinor saw it all—and in that instant, she knew. The letter must be from Willoughby. Her chest tightened. Her breath caught. She sat frozen, trembling, afraid to move, afraid to breathe. But Mrs. Jennings, oblivious, laughed. “Ah! What a lovely surprise! I do hope he’s written something sweet. Poor dear Miss Marianne—she’s been quite the love-struck bird since she came to town!”

Elinor forced a smile. “You’re mistaken, Ma’am. There is no engagement. I assure you, I’d be more surprised than anyone to hear otherwise.”

“Nonsense!” Mrs. Jennings clucked, measuring yarn. “Don’t pretend to be so coy. Everyone knows it! They were head over heels from the first moment. I saw them together every day in Devonshire—always walking, always talking. And don’t tell me your sister didn’t come to London to buy wedding clothes? Come now, it’s common knowledge. Even Charlotte says so.”

“I beg you not to spread such falsehoods,” Elinor said, her voice low but firm. “It’s cruel to imagine what you do not know.”

Mrs. Jennings chuckled, unconvinced. But Elinor had no strength left for argument. She hurried upstairs, heart pounding, desperate to see the letter.

In the bedroom, she found Marianne sprawled across the bed, gasping, clutching a single letter in one hand, others scattered beside her. Elinor approached slowly, silently. She sat beside her sister, took her hand, kissed her cheek—then broke down, weeping as fiercely as Marianne had done. In that shared sorrow, words were unnecessary. After a while, Marianne handed over the letters. Then, covering her face, she let out a cry so raw it tore through the silence.

Elinor waited until the storm passed, then read the letter:

*I have just received your letter and thank you for it. I regret any offense I may have given you last night, though I cannot understand where it might have arisen. I assure you, it was never my intention to displease. I value my acquaintance with your family deeply and shall always recall our time in Devonshire with gratitude. Should any misunderstanding arise, I hope it will not sever our connection. My respect for your household is sincere—but if I’ve led you to believe more than I felt, I take full responsibility for my carelessness. I can only say that my affections have long been pledged elsewhere. I expect this engagement to be fulfilled within weeks. It is with great regret that I return the letters and lock of hair you so kindly gave me.*

Elinor read it twice. Then three times. The cruelty of it—the coldness, the pretense of politeness masking utter betrayal—left her reeling. This was not merely a rejection. It was a declaration of indifference, a dismissal of everything Marianne had believed. No remorse. No explanation. Only a polite lie wrapped in formality.

She looked up at her sister, whose face was contorted with pain. Yet even in her agony, Marianne seemed to sense the depth of Elinor’s own anguish.

“Poor Elinor,” she whispered. “How unhappy I make you.”

“If only there were something I could do,” Elinor murmured.

“You *can* do nothing,” Marianne choked out. “Leave me. Hate me. Forget me. But don’t stand there and watch me suffer. How can you speak of strength when you feel nothing but joy? You are happy—happy because Edward loves you.”

“Happy?” Elinor repeated, her voice breaking. “Do you think I am? I see you in this state, and I am miserable.”

“But I am worse,” Marianne cried. “No one can understand. Nothing can ease this.”

“You must not say that,” Elinor insisted. “Think of Mama. Think of how her heart breaks for you. For her sake, you must endure.”

“I cannot,” Marianne whispered. “I cannot bear it. I am not strong enough.”

“Then consider what might have been,” Elinor said gently. “Had you been engaged for months, had you planned a future—wouldn’t the blow have been worse? At least now, you know the truth before it’s too late.”

“Engagement?” Marianne snapped. “There was no engagement.”

“No engagement?” Elinor echoed.

“No,” Marianne said firmly. “He never promised. Never said he loved me—only implied it. I thought he did. But he never truly professed it.”

“And yet you wrote to him?”

“Yes,” Marianne admitted. “But how could I not? Everything between us… it felt real. I trusted him.”

Elinor remained silent, then picked up the other letters. She read them quickly—the first, written upon arriving in London; the second, after missing him at the Middleton ball; the third, demanding answers after their final meeting. Each was filled with affection, expectation, longing. They spoke of trust, of intimacy, of a bond that felt irrevocable.

And yet Willoughby had responded with this—this icy refusal.

Elinor sighed. “I would not have believed such a letter could come from him.”

“It wasn’t him,” Marianne said, her voice rising. “It couldn’t be. He was kind once. He loved me. I saw it. That look he gave me when I gave him the lock of hair—do you remember? His voice—his eyes—he was torn. He said he might not see me for weeks. He was distressed. He cared.”

For a moment, she was lost in memory. Then she returned, shaken. “He has been used. Not by himself. By someone else. Someone who lied about me. Who poisoned his mind. I would rather suspect every person in the world than believe Willoughby capable of such cruelty.”

Elinor hesitated. “Whoever it was, let them not triumph. Let your integrity be your strength.”

“No,” Marianne said, her voice cracking. “I have no pride left. I do not care who sees me broken. Let them enjoy it. I cannot pretend to be strong. I am wretched—and I will not hide it.”

They sat in silence. Elinor paced, lost in thought. Marianne stared at the letter, reading each sentence again, trembling.

“It is too much,” she breathed. “Oh, Willoughby—could this be yours? To call the lock of my hair ‘so obligingly bestowed’—as if it were a favor, not a gift from my heart! Where was your heart when you wrote that? Barbarous. Insolent. Unforgivable.”

“No,” Elinor said softly. “Nothing excuses him.”

“And this woman—this unknown woman—who is she? How long has this been planned? Did he ever speak of her? Of anyone? No—never. He only spoke of me.”

Another pause. Then: “I must go home. I must go to Mama. Can we leave tomorrow?”

“Tomorrow?” Elinor asked, startled.

“Yes. Why stay? I came here for him. Now—now no one cares for me. No one sees me.”

“We cannot leave tomorrow,” Elinor said gently. “We owe Mrs. Jennings kindness. We cannot vanish so abruptly.”

“Then in two days,” Marianne insisted. “I cannot stay longer. I cannot face the pity of the Middletons, the Palmers—especially Lady Middleton. Oh, what would *he* say to see me pitied by her?”

Elinor helped her back onto the bed. Marianne tried to rest, but her body would not still. She shifted, writhed, gasped. Her breathing grew erratic. Elinor held her, whispering reassurances, pressing lavender drops to her lips until the spasms eased.

At last, she lay still—motionless, pale, exhausted. Elinor stayed by her side, watching, waiting, holding the weight of her sister’s sorrow in her arms.

Mrs. Jennings burst into the room the moment she returned, her usual briskness replaced by a look of genuine distress. Without waiting for permission, she pushed open the door and stepped inside, her voice thick with sympathy. “How are you, my dear?” she asked, turning to Marianne, who turned her face away, unable even to speak.

“How is she, Miss Dashwood?” Mrs. Jennings continued, her eyes fixed on the pale figure lying still on the bed. “Poor thing! She looks dreadful. No wonder—poor girl. It’s true, I’m afraid. He’s marrying very soon—a shameless fellow, if ever there was one! I can’t bear it. Mrs. Taylor told me just half an hour ago, and she heard it from a close friend of Miss Grey herself. Otherwise, I wouldn’t have believed it. I nearly fainted when I heard. Well,” she said, shaking her head, “all I can say is, if this is true, he’s behaved abominably to a young lady I know well—and I hope with all my heart his wife gives him no peace. That’s what I’ll always say, my dear—you may count on it. I’ve no patience for men who act like this. If I ever see him again, I’ll give him such a piece of my mind as he hasn’t had in years. But there’s one comfort, Miss Marianne: he’s not the only man in the world worth having. And with your beauty, you’ll never lack admirers. Poor soul! I won’t stay longer—I’ll let her cry it out now and be done with it. The Parrys and Sandersons are coming tonight, you know. That’ll cheer her up.”

She left quietly, tiptoeing out as though every sound might deepen Marianne’s sorrow.

To Elinor’s surprise, Marianne insisted on dining with them. Though Elinor urged her not to, saying it might be too much, Marianne replied firmly, “No, I’ll go down. I can manage. The noise will distract me more than hurt me.”

Elinor, touched that her sister could summon such strength—even if fleeting—said nothing further. She helped Marianne dress, adjusting her gown as gently as she could while Marianne remained seated on the bed. When they were summoned, Elinor supported her sister into the dining room.

There, despite her drawn features and hollow eyes, Marianne ate more than expected and seemed calmer than Elinor had dared hope. Had she been aware of Mrs. Jennings’s constant, well-meaning but clumsy efforts to soothe her—offering delicacies, telling news, coaxing her to eat—she might have broken down. But Marianne was lost in thought, untouched by the world around her. Her silence was absolute.

Elinor, understanding Mrs. Jennings’s intentions, though often overbearing, offered polite thanks and returned courteous nods where her sister could not. Their kind-hearted hostess saw Marianne’s pain and felt bound to ease it however she could. She treated her like a child on the last day of summer holidays—giving her the best seat by the fire, tempting her with every treat in the house, and regaling her with stories of the day’s events.

Had Elinor not seen the sorrow in her sister’s face, she might have found amusement in Mrs. Jennings’s attempts to cure heartbreak with olives, sweets, and warmth. But when the repetition of these gestures finally registered with Marianne, she could endure no more. With a sharp cry—“Misery!”—and a gesture for Elinor not to follow, she rose and fled from the table.

“Oh, poor thing!” Mrs. Jennings exclaimed once she was gone. “It breaks my heart to see her! And she didn’t even finish her wine! Or the dried cherries! Lord, nothing seems to help. If I knew what she’d enjoy, I’d send across the whole town for it. I just don’t understand how a man can treat such a lovely girl so cruelly! But when one side has plenty of money and the other next to none—well, bless you, they don’t care at all!”

“The woman—Miss Grey, I think you said—is very wealthy?”

“Fifty thousand pounds, my dear. Did you ever see her? They say she’s stylish, smart, but not particularly handsome. I remember her aunt well—Biddy Henshawe, married a rich man. The whole family’s well-off. Fifty thousand pounds! And from what I hear, it won’t come until it’s needed; because he’s practically ruined. No wonder—racing about with his curricle and hunters! Still, it doesn’t matter what we say. When a young man comes to court a pretty girl and promises marriage, he shouldn’t run off just because he’s poor and a richer girl is ready to take him. Why doesn’t he sell his horses, rent out his house, dismiss his servants, and start fresh? I’m sure Miss Marianne would have waited. But that’s not how things are anymore. Young men today won’t give up any pleasure.”

“What sort of girl is Miss Grey? Is she kind?”
