“I’ve never heard a bad word about her. In fact, I barely know her—except that Mrs. Taylor mentioned earlier that Miss Walker hinted Mr. and Mrs. Ellison wouldn’t mind seeing Miss Grey married, since she and Mrs. Ellison never got along.”

“And who are the Ellisons?”

“Her guardians, my dear. But now she’s of age and free to choose. And what a choice she’s made!—Well, now,” she paused, “your poor sister’s gone back to her room, I suppose, to grieve alone. Is there anything we could bring her? Poor dear—it’s cruel to leave her alone. Soon we’ll have friends over. That’ll help. What shall we play? She hates whist, I know—but is there any round game she likes?”

“Dear ma’am,” Elinor replied softly, “your kindness is truly unnecessary. Marianne likely won’t come out again tonight. I’ll do my best to persuade her to go to bed early. She needs rest.”

“Aye, that’s probably best,” Mrs. Jennings agreed. “Let her name her supper and go straight to bed. Lord, no wonder she’s looked so low these past days. This must have weighed on her for weeks. And the letter today—well, that finished it. Poor soul! If I’d known, I’d never have joked her about it. But how could I guess? I thought it was just another love letter—everyone laughs about those. Oh, Sir John and my girls will be so upset when they hear. I should have stopped by Conduit Street on my way home. But I’ll tell them tomorrow.”

“It wouldn’t be necessary,” Elinor said gently, “for you to warn Mrs. Palmer or Sir John against mentioning Mr. Willoughby or alluding to what’s happened before my sister. Their good nature will naturally prevent them from speaking of it. And the less said about it, the better—for myself, I assure you, it would spare me much pain.”

“Oh, indeed I understand,” Mrs. Jennings responded. “It must be terrible to hear it discussed. And your sister—well, I wouldn’t mention a word to her for the world. You saw I didn’t at dinner. Nor would Sir John or my girls—they’re all thoughtful. Especially if I remind them. Honestly, I believe the less we talk about such things, the sooner they’re forgotten. What good does talking ever do?”

“In this case,” Elinor said, “it can only harm. More so than in many similar situations, because of certain circumstances that make it unfit for public discussion. I must do this justice to Mr. Willoughby—he has not broken a formal engagement with my sister.”

“Law, my dear!” Mrs. Jennings exclaimed. “Don’t try to defend him! No formal engagement? After showing her every room in Allenham House, picking out their future bedroom? That’s more than enough!”

Elinor, mindful of her sister’s feelings, did not press further. She hoped it wasn’t required of her for Willoughby’s sake—though Marianne might lose much, he stood to gain little from truth being spoken.

After a quiet pause, Mrs. Jennings brightened again. “Well, my dear, it’s true what they say—an ill wind blows someone good. This will be excellent news for Colonel Brandon. He’ll get her at last. Mark my words—he’ll marry her by midsummer. Lord, how he’ll rejoice! I hope he comes tonight. It’ll be a far better match for your sister. Two thousand a year—no debts, no worries—except that little love-child, of course. Ah, I forgot her. But she can be apprenticed cheaply, and then what does it matter? Delaford is a lovely place—just the sort I like: old-fashioned, comfortable, full of conveniences. Surrounded by high garden walls covered in the finest fruit trees. And that mulberry tree in the corner! Charlotte and I stuffed ourselves the last time we were there. Then there’s a dove-cote, beautiful stew-ponds, a little canal—everything one could want. And it’s near the church, only a quarter-mile from the turnpike road, so it’s never dull. Sit in the yew arbour behind the house, and you can watch every carriage pass by. Oh, it’s a charming place! A butcher nearby, the parsonage just a stone’s throw away. To me, a thousand times prettier than Barton Park, where they have to send three miles for meat and no neighbor closer than your mother. I’ll rally the Colonel as soon as I can. One shoulder of mutton drives another down. If we can just get Willoughby out of her thoughts!”

“If we can do that,” Elinor said quietly, “we’ll be doing very well—whether Colonel Brandon comes or not.”

Then rising, she went to find Marianne, whom she discovered in her own room, leaning over the dying embers of a small fire—the only light in the dim chamber.

“You’d better leave me,” Marianne murmured without looking up.

“I will,” Elinor replied, “if you’ll go to bed.”

At first, Marianne refused, caught in the stubborn pride of grief. But Elinor’s gentle persistence softened her, and soon she lay down, resting her aching head on the pillow. Elinor watched, hoping she might sleep peacefully before she left.

In the drawing-room, Mrs. Jennings soon joined her, holding a glass of wine. “My dear,” she said, “I just remembered—I have the finest old Constantia wine in the house. I brought a glass for your sister. My poor husband—how he loved it! Whenever his old colicky gout flared up, he said nothing helped like this. Take it to her.”

“Dear ma’am,” Elinor smiled, “you’re so kind. But I’ve just left Marianne in bed—almost asleep. And I believe nothing would help her more than rest. If you’ll allow it, I’ll drink it myself.”

Mrs. Jennings, disappointed she hadn’t arrived five minutes sooner, accepted the compromise. As Elinor sipped the wine, she reflected that while its healing powers for colicky gout were irrelevant now, its soothing effect on a broken heart might be just as real—for herself as for her sister.

Colonel Brandon arrived while tea was being served. From the way he glanced around the room, Elinor sensed he neither expected nor wished to see Marianne. He already knew why she was absent.

Mrs. Jennings, however, missed the sign. Shortly after he entered, she walked over to Elinor and whispered, “The Colonel looks as grave as ever. He knows nothing of it—do tell him, my dear.”

A few moments later, he pulled a chair close to hers and, with a glance that confirmed his knowledge, asked, “How is your sister?”

“Marianne is unwell,” Elinor said. “She’s been indisposed all day. We’ve persuaded her to go to bed.”

“Then perhaps,” he hesitated, “what I heard this morning… may be true. More true than I could have believed at first.”

“What did you hear?”

“That a gentleman—someone I had reason to believe was engaged—someone I _knew_ was engaged—but how shall I say it? If you already know, I needn’t tell you.”

“You mean,” Elinor said, forcing calmness, “Mr. Willoughby’s marriage to Miss Grey. Yes, we know everything. Today has been a day of revelations. This morning was when it all came to light. Mr. Willoughby is inscrutable. Where did you hear it?”

“In a stationer’s shop in Pall Mall. I had business there. Two ladies were waiting for their carriage, and one was telling the other about the wedding plans—in a voice so loud, I couldn’t help hearing. The name ‘Willoughby’—John Willoughby—kept repeating itself. Then came a clear statement: everything was settled. It wouldn’t be a secret anymore. It would happen within weeks. Details of preparations, travel plans—all laid out. One thing I remember especially—it identified him: after the wedding, they were going to Combe Magna, his estate in Somersetshire. My astonishment—I can’t describe it. The woman who spoke, I learned later, was Mrs. Ellison. And as I’ve since been told, that’s the name of Miss Grey’s guardian.”

“Yes. But have you heard that Miss Grey has fifty thousand pounds? That might explain it.”

“It may. But Willoughby is capable—well, I think—” he paused, then added, almost to himself, “And your sister—how did she take it?”

“Her suffering has been severe. I can only hope it’s brief. It’s been a cruel blow. Until yesterday, I believe she never doubted his affection. Even now, perhaps—but I’m almost convinced he never truly loved her. He’s been deceitful. And in some ways, there’s a hardness in him.”

“Ah,” Colonel Brandon said softly, “there is indeed. But your sister—does she see it as you do?”

“You know her nature. You can imagine how eager she is to justify him, if she can.”

He said nothing. Moments later, the tea things were cleared, and card games began—ending the conversation naturally.

Mrs. Jennings, who had watched them closely, expecting to see joy spark in Colonel Brandon’s eyes upon hearing the news, was stunned. Instead, he remained more serious and thoughtful than ever throughout the evening.

Marianne awoke the next morning with no greater peace than she had known the night before. Though sleep had come more easily than expected, her heart remained heavy, her sorrow unrelenting. The same ache that had carried her into dreams still clung to her when she opened her eyes. Elinor, ever gentle and attentive, urged her to speak—truly speak—of what she felt. Before breakfast was served, they had walked through the pain again and again, each time with the same familiar pattern: Elinor’s calm conviction and tender advice, Marianne’s passionate shifts of belief, her emotions rising and falling like tides.

At times, Marianne could believe Willoughby had been as misled, as wronged, as she was—his actions born of misfortune rather than malice. Then, in the next breath, she would be consumed by doubt, convinced he had betrayed her utterly, beyond redemption. One moment she cared nothing for the world’s judgment; the next, she longed to vanish from sight forever. Another moment brought fierce defiance, a refusal to let anyone see her broken. But one thing never changed: her resolve to avoid Mrs. Jennings whenever possible, and to remain silent when she could not escape her presence.

Her heart was closed to the idea that Mrs. Jennings could truly understand her grief. “No, no, no,” she insisted, voice trembling, “she cannot feel. Her kindness is not sympathy. Her warmth is not tenderness. She only wants talk, gossip—and now she likes me because I give it to her.”

Elinor listened quietly, knowing well how Marianne’s sensitivity often led her to misjudge others. With such keen feelings and a mind so finely tuned to emotion, Marianne expected others to mirror her depth of feeling, and measured their intentions by how much they hurt or comforted her. It was a flaw, though born of good nature and intelligence. And it was this flaw that now deepened her disdain for Mrs. Jennings.

Later, after breakfast, while the sisters sat together in their room, the door opened with a cheerful flourish. Mrs. Jennings entered, letter in hand, face glowing with hope. “Now, my dear,” she said brightly, “I bring you something I’m certain will cheer you!”

The words were enough. In an instant, Marianne’s imagination painted a scene: a letter from Willoughby, full of regret and love, explaining everything—followed swiftly by him himself, rushing in, pleading at her feet, his eyes speaking more than words ever could.

But the joy was short-lived. The handwriting was not Willoughby’s—it was her mother’s. A wave of disappointment crashed over her, so sharp it felt like a new wound. The agony of false hope was worse than any sorrow she’d known before. To her, Mrs. Jennings’s act was cruel—though the older woman meant only kindness.

Tears spilled down Marianne’s cheeks, silent but furious. She could not speak, could only weep, a reproach too deep for words. Mrs. Jennings, unaware of the storm she had stirred, offered piteous condolences and pressed the letter into her hands. “Read it, dear,” she urged. “It’s just what you need.”

But the letter brought little comfort. Her mother wrote with unwavering faith in Willoughby’s constancy, still believing their engagement was real, urging Marianne to open up to them both. The tone was loving, hopeful, trusting—so full of affection for Willoughby that Marianne read it through tears of anguish. Her longing to return home surged anew. Her mother was dearer than ever—not because she was wise, but because she loved so blindly, so beautifully. Marianne wanted to rush back to her arms, to hide in her embrace.

Elinor, uncertain whether London or Barton would be better for her sister, offered no firm advice—only patience until they knew their mother’s wishes. Eventually, Marianne consented to wait.

Mrs. Jennings left earlier than usual, eager to share the news with the Middletons and Palmers. She refused Elinor’s offer to accompany her, insisting on going alone.

Left alone, Elinor sat down to write to their mother, her heart heavy. She knew the weight of what she must say, and feared her own words had failed to prepare her sister. As she wrote, Marianne stood nearby, watching the pen move across the page, grieving for Elinor’s burden and for the pain her mother would feel upon reading it.

They sat in silence for about fifteen minutes, when a sudden knock startled Marianne. “Who can that be?” Elinor asked, alarmed. “So early! I thought we’d finally be safe.”

Marianne rushed to the window. “It’s Colonel Brandon!” she said, irritated. “We’re never safe from _him_.”

“He won’t come in,” Elinor said. “Mrs. Jennings isn’t here.”

“I won’t trust that,” Marianne replied, retreating to her room. “A man who has nothing to do with his own time has no right to invade others’.”

Yet Colonel Brandon did come in. Elinor saw it at once—the concern in his face, the quiet sadness in his eyes. He looked as if he bore a secret too painful to carry alone. And though Marianne scorned him, Elinor felt a pang of pity—for him, and for her sister’s blindness.

“I met Mrs. Jennings in Bond Street,” he began, after the first greeting. “She encouraged me to come, and I was glad to accept—especially since I hoped to find you alone. I came with one purpose only: to help your sister. Not to ease her pain immediately—but to give her lasting truth. My regard for her, for you, for your mother… I hope to prove it by telling you something I’ve long held close. I believe it may help her. But I fear I am overstepping—perhaps I am wrong to speak. After all, how many hours have I spent convincing myself I should?”

He paused, breathless.

“I understand,” Elinor said gently. “You know something about Mr. Willoughby—something that reveals his true character. If you can tell me, it will be the greatest kindness you could show Marianne. I thank you already. And hers—her gratitude—will follow in time.”

He nodded slowly. “Then I shall begin. When I left Barton last October—I must go further back. You’ll think me awkward, Miss Dashwood. I don’t know where to start. Perhaps I must tell you a little of myself. It will be brief. On such a subject,” he sighed deeply, “there is little temptation to linger.”

He paused, then continued. “You may have forgotten a conversation we had at Barton Park—one evening after the dance. I mentioned a lady I once knew, who reminded me of your sister.”

“No,” Elinor said softly, “I remember.”

His expression softened. “If memory serves, there is a strong resemblance—both in looks and spirit. The same fire, the same passion. This woman was my cousin—my father’s niece—raised by us from childhood. We were nearly the same age, and grew up together. I loved her from the beginning—deeply. And she, I believe, returned my affection as fiercely as your sister loves Mr. Willoughby. But her fate was no less tragic—though for different reasons.

“At seventeen, she was married—against her will—to my brother. Her fortune was large, our estate encumbered. My father, her guardian, made the decision. My brother did not love her. He treated her poorly. I had hoped her love for me would sustain her—but it broke under the strain. She promised me she would never falter—but she could not endure it. We had almost fled to Scotland. But a servant betrayed us. I was sent away. She was imprisoned in her home, cut off from friends and light.

“She suffered terribly. My father died soon after. I was abroad. Without support, without guidance—how could she resist? I had believed she would hold fast. But she fell.

“Two years later, I heard she had been divorced. That was the moment I lost all hope. I felt as if the world had ended.

“I returned to England three years after. I searched for her—desperately. But I found only rumors. Her allowance had been transferred. I feared the worst.

“And then, six months after my return, I found her. I went to visit a former servant in a debtor’s prison—and there she was, in the same house, sick and pale, barely recognizable. She was dying—consumption, they said. I took her to lodgings, tended her daily. I was with her when she died.

“She left behind a daughter—just three years old—born of her first affair. I raised her as best I could. I placed her in school, visited her often. After my brother died, she came to live with me at Delaford. I called her a distant relation—but everyone suspected otherwise.

“Three years ago, I moved her to a respectable household in Dorsetshire. For two years, all was well. But last February, she vanished.

“She had gone to Bath with a friend—whose father I knew. I trusted them. But she told me nothing. The father claimed ignorance—confined to bed, he said, while the girls wandered freely.

“For eight months, I knew nothing. Only last October did I receive a letter—from her herself. She wrote from Delaford. I received it the morning of our planned trip to Whitwell—that’s why I left so suddenly. No one understood. But I had to go. I had to help.

“The letter told me she was near delivery. I found her in hiding, ill, terrified. I took her and her child to the country.

“But the name of her lover—she confessed it to me, though unwillingly. It was Mr. Willoughby.”

Elinor gasped. “Could it be—could it be *he*?”

“Yes,” Colonel Brandon said quietly. “He had seduced her, left her in poverty and shame. Promised to return—never did. Never wrote. Left her alone, with no home, no family, no way to survive. And he knew it.”

Elinor shuddered. “This changes everything. His character is revealed—reckless, selfish, cruel. And yet, you found me here—still hoping, still loving him. Can you imagine what I felt? When I came to you last week, I meant to learn the truth. I didn’t know what to do—but I couldn’t stand by and watch you be deceived.”

He paused. “I met him once since then. We met by appointment. I came to confront him. He came to defend himself. We fought—no blood drawn. But the meeting was kept secret.”

Elinor sighed. She could not judge a soldier’s honor.

“Thus,” he said, “the fate of mother and daughter mirrors each other. And I failed them both.”

“Is she still in town?” Elinor asked.

“No. I removed her to the country as soon as she recovered. She remains there, hidden, safe.”

After a moment, he rose. “I have taken too much of your time. I must go.”

Elinor thanked him sincerely, her heart full of respect and compassion. “You have done a great service,” she said. “I believe this knowledge will help Marianne. At first, she will suffer—more even than before. But soon, she will see the truth. And when she does, she will be grateful.”

Colonel Brandon bowed slightly, then left, leaving Elinor alone—moved, humbled, and filled with a deeper understanding of love, loss, and the cost of silence.

CHAPTER XXXII. When Elinor shared the details of her conversation with Marianne, she expected a deeper reaction—perhaps even a spark of renewed hope or anger. But Marianne’s response was quiet, almost unnervingly so. She listened without protest, without defense for Willoughby, and without a single word to justify him. Her eyes filled with tears, not of rage, but of sorrow too deep for words. It was clear she accepted the truth—not just that Willoughby had deceived her, but that he had betrayed others before her.

Elinor saw the change in her sister: the way she now greeted Colonel Brandon with a softness she had once refused, how she spoke to him with a gentle respect tinged with pity. Her agitation had lessened, but it was replaced by something worse—a quiet despair. The loss of Willoughby’s affection had been painful enough, but now the full weight of his character came crashing down upon her. His betrayal of Miss Williams, the suffering of that young woman, and the chilling thought that he might have once meant to deceive her too—all of it settled into her heart like a stone. She could not speak of it, even to Elinor. Instead, she withdrew into silence, letting her grief fester in private, which only made Elinor ache more for her.

Mrs. Dashwood’s reaction, when she read Elinor’s letter, mirrored the daughters’ pain—though perhaps even more deeply. Her disappointment was profound, her indignation fierce. She wrote back at once, pouring out her sorrow and urging Marianne to endure with strength. To hear her mother speak of "fortitude" was heartbreaking—proof that this wound was far beyond anything mere time could heal. Mrs. Dashwood knew the source of the pain was not just lost love, but shame and humiliation, and she did not want her daughter to dwell on either.

She insisted the girls stay in London longer than planned, not because she wished them to linger in sorrow, but because she believed Barton would only make things worse. Every corner of the cottage reminded Marianne of Willoughby—the garden where they walked, the room where they talked, the very air that carried memories. By staying in town, surrounded by new faces and distractions, Marianne might find moments of respite, even if only brief ones. And besides, her son-in-law and his wife were coming to London soon. It was important, Mrs. Dashwood felt, that they see their brother.

Marianne agreed to follow her mother’s advice, though silently she resisted. She longed to be near her mother, to feel her comfort, to cry in her arms. But instead, she was left in a city where every person seemed to know the story, where gossip hummed through drawing rooms like a bitter wind. Still, she found solace in knowing that her sacrifice would bring some good—her sister would not have to face Edward so soon.

Elinor, too, had her own burden. Though she hoped to avoid Edward entirely, she feared it was impossible. Yet she took comfort in thinking that staying longer might help Marianne more than returning home would. And she guarded her sister carefully—ensuring no one mentioned Willoughby’s name. Her efforts were not wasted. Neither Mrs. Jennings nor Sir John dared speak of him in front of Marianne. Even Mrs. Palmer, though furious, kept her tongue in check.

But Elinorum could not escape the storm of outrage around her. Sir John raged about the man he once admired. “A good-natured fellow! A bold rider! And now—such a scoundrel!” he thundered. “I’d rather see him dead than speak to him again. Not even if we were both trapped in the same covert for two hours.” He vowed never to acknowledge him again, calling him a “deceitful dog” who once offered him a puppy from Folly’s litter—only to betray everyone.

Mrs. Palmer was equally incensed. “I’ll drop him immediately,” she declared. “I’m thankful I never really knew him. If Combe Magna weren’t so close to Cleveland, I’d never have minded—but now I hate him so much I won’t even say his name again. I’ll tell everyone what a useless creature he is.”

Yet her anger was matched by curiosity. She eagerly gathered news of the wedding—where the carriage was being built, who painted Willoughby’s portrait, where Miss Grey’s clothes could be seen. She passed it all along to Elinor, as if it were gossip worth sharing.

Lady Middleton, however, offered a different kind of relief. Calm, polite, and utterly unconcerned, she barely acknowledged the affair. Once or twice a day, she would murmur, “It is very shocking, indeed,” and then move on. No questions, no prying, no emotional demands. For Elinor, whose spirits were often crushed under the weight of others’ sympathy, this quiet dignity was a balm. Sometimes, she found herself valuing good manners above kindness, simply because they allowed her peace.

Colonel Brandon, meanwhile, remained a quiet presence. His inquiries were gentle, never intrusive. He had earned the right to speak openly with Elinor, having shown such care and compassion during the crisis. Their conversations were always respectful, sometimes tender. And though he never said much, the look Marianne gave him at times—pitying, softened—was enough to reassure him that his efforts had not been in vain. Elinor saw it too and dared to hope that one day, perhaps, there might be something more between them.

But Mrs. Jennings, ignorant of these subtleties, began to lose faith. After two days, she sighed, “Well, if it’s not Midsummer, it’ll be Michaelmas.” By the end of the week, she decided it wouldn’t happen at all. The Colonel’s silence, his continued seriousness, made her think the match was over. And with that, she began to imagine other possibilities—perhaps Miss Dashwood would claim the mulberry-tree and yew arbour after all. Mrs. Ferrars faded from her mind.

Early in February, less than two weeks after receiving Willoughby’s letter, Elinor delivered the final blow: he was married. She had taken care to learn the news first, so Marianne would not discover it in the papers, which she checked each morning with trembling hands.

Marianne heard it with quiet dignity. She said nothing at first, shed no tears—then, suddenly, they came, flooding out uncontrollably. She sat in silence for the rest of the day, wrapped in sorrow so thick it seemed to suffocate her.

The Willoughbys left London quickly, and Elinor hoped this might finally allow her sister to begin healing. Perhaps, slowly, she could coax Marianne out of the house again.

At the same time, the two Miss Steeles returned to London, newly arrived at their cousin’s home in Holburn. They appeared at Conduit Street and Berkeley Street, welcomed with open arms by their relatives. All except Elinor, who felt a cold knot tighten in her chest at their sight.

Lucy Steele, especially, seemed delighted. “I should have been *so* disappointed if I hadn’t found you here still,” she said, emphasizing the word with a smile. “But I always knew you’d stay. You told me you’d leave after a month, but I knew better. It would have been such a pity to go before your brother and sister came. And now—well, you’ll be in no hurry to leave, will you? I’m so glad you didn’t keep to your word.”

Elinor forced a smile. She understood Lucy’s meaning perfectly—she wanted to believe Elinor would stay long enough to marry Edward. But she held her tongue.

Mrs. Jennings, ever eager for drama, asked, “And how did you travel?”

“Not by stage,” Miss Steele replied proudly. “We came post all the way, and had a very smart beau with us. Dr. Davies was going to town, so we joined him in a chaise. He behaved most genteelly and paid ten or twelve shillings extra.”

“Oh, oh!” Mrs. Jennings clapped her hands. “Very pretty indeed! And the doctor is unmarried, I’ll wager.”

“Now, everybody laughs at me about the Doctor,” Miss Steele simpered. “My cousins say I’ve made a conquest. But I assure you, I don’t think of him at all. ‘Lord! Here comes your beau, Nancy,’ my cousin said the other day. My beau? Why, I don’t know who you mean. The Doctor isn’t my beau.”

“Aye, aye,” Mrs. Jennings chuckled. “That’s fine talk—but I see the man.”

“No, indeed!” Miss Steele protested, with mock seriousness. “And I beg you, if you ever hear it said, please contradict it.”

Mrs. Jennings promised she would—and Miss Steele beamed with satisfaction.

Then Lucy turned to Elinor. “You’ll go and stay with your brother and sister when they come to town, I suppose?”

“No,” Elinor replied gently. “I don’t think we shall.”

“Oh, yes, I dare say you will.”

Elinor let it pass. “How delightful it must be,” she added, “that Mrs. Dashwood can spare you both for so long.”

“Long a time, indeed!” Mrs. Jennings interjected. “Their visit has only just begun!”

Lucy fell silent.

“I’m sorry we can’t see your sister,” Miss Steele said, turning to Elinor. “I’m sorry she’s not well—” for Marianne had already left the room.

“You’re very kind,” Elinor replied. “She would be sorry to miss you, but she’s been plagued lately with nervous headaches. She’s not fit for company.”

“Oh, dear—that’s a great pity! But such old friends as Lucy and me—we ought to be able to see her. I’m sure we wouldn’t say a word.”

Elinor declined politely. “She may be resting—or dressed in her gown. She’s not ready to receive visitors.”

“Oh, if that’s all,” Miss Steele exclaimed, “we can just go and see her.”

Elinor braced herself—but before she could respond, Lucy sharply rebuked her sister. “Nancy, you mustn’t be so forward.” And just as before, Lucy’s sharp tone silenced her cousin, preserving the fragile peace.

CHAPTER XXXIII. After some hesitation, Marianne finally agreed to accompany her sister and Mrs. Jennings on a short outing one morning, though she insisted they would not call upon anyone—only go to Gray’s in Sackville Street, where Elinor was negotiating the sale of a few old-fashioned jewels left by their mother. As they reached the shop, Mrs. Jennings remembered she had a friend to visit at the other end of the street and, having no business there herself, decided to go alone while the sisters attended to theirs. She would return for them shortly.

Inside, the shop was crowded, and so many customers were ahead of them that the Miss Dashwoods had no choice but to wait. They took seats at the far end of the counter, where only one gentleman stood, seemingly indifferent to the delay. Elinor, perhaps hoping his attention might be drawn to their presence, watched him closely. But his focus was entirely absorbed by a peculiar task: selecting a toothpick-case. He examined every option with meticulous care, debating size, shape, and ornamentation as if it were a matter of great consequence. For nearly fifteen minutes, he scrutinized each piece, rejecting them all until he finally settled on a design of his own invention—one combining ivory, gold, and pearls. Only then did he turn his gaze toward the two sisters, offering them a glance so broad and unfeeling it seemed less like admiration than an idle curiosity. The man was impeccably dressed, yet his manner revealed a soul devoid of warmth or charm. Elinor noted him well—a figure of fashion without grace, polish without character.

Marianne, meanwhile, remained untouched by the scene. Lost in her thoughts, she sat quietly, unaware of the gentleman’s rudeness or the absurdity of his choices. Her mind was elsewhere, drifting through memories and emotions too deep for such trivial surroundings to disturb.

At last, the transaction was complete. The gentleman carefully slipped on his gloves, cast another glance at the sisters—this time more suggestive of superiority than interest—and departed with an air of self-satisfaction. Elinor quickly resumed her own business, nearly finishing when another man stepped forward beside her. She turned—and with surprise recognized her brother, John Dashwood.

Their meeting brought a moment of genuine affection, which made a pleasant contrast to the otherwise dull atmosphere of the shop. John was clearly glad to see them again, and his inquiries about their mother were courteous and thoughtful. He told them he and Fanny had arrived in town just two days before. “I truly meant to call yesterday,” he said, “but we had to take Harry to see the wild beasts at Exeter Exchange, and afterward spent the day with Mrs. Ferrars. Harry was delighted. This morning I intended to come, if I could find half an hour, but one always has so much to do upon first arriving.” He added that he was now there to order a seal for Fanny, but hoped to visit them the next day and meet Mrs. Jennings. “I understand she is quite well-off,” he remarked. “And the Middletons too—you must introduce me to them. As my mother-in-law’s relations, I should be pleased to show them every respect. I’ve heard they’re excellent neighbors.”

“Indeed they are,” Elinor replied. “Their kindness and thoughtfulness have been beyond words.”

“I’m very glad to hear it,” he said warmly. “It should be so—they’re wealthy, related to you, and naturally ought to offer every comfort. And you’re happily settled in your little cottage, wanting for nothing? Edward gave us a wonderful description—said it was the most perfect dwelling of its kind, and you all seemed utterly content. That pleased us greatly.”

Elinor felt a quiet embarrassment at his tone, but was spared further conversation by the arrival of Mrs. Jennings’s servant, who announced that her mistress waited at the door. John Dashwood accompanied them downstairs, was introduced to Mrs. Jennings, and promised to call again soon. His manners were calm yet polite, especially toward Mrs. Jennings, whom he treated with particular deference. When Colonel Brandon entered shortly after, John studied him with a curious eye—his expression suggesting that he found the colonel worth knowing precisely because he appeared to be wealthy.

After staying half an hour, John asked Elinor if she would walk with him to Conduit Street to meet Sir John and Lady Middleton. The weather was bright and inviting, and she consented readily.

Once outside, he began with questions. “Who is Colonel Brandon? Is he well-off?”

“Yes,” Elinor answered. “He has good property in Dorsetshire.”

“That’s good,” John said. “He seems a gentlemanly sort. And I think, Elinor, I may congratulate you on the prospect of a respectable future.”

“Me?” she asked, startled. “What do you mean?”

“He likes you,” John declared. “I observed him closely and am certain of it. What is the extent of his fortune?”

“About two thousand a year,” she replied.

“Two thousand!” John exclaimed, then paused, gathering himself into a burst of generous enthusiasm. “Elinor, I wish with all my heart it were twice that—for your sake.”

“I believe you,” she said, though she doubted his sincerity. “But I assure you, Colonel Brandon has no intention of marrying me.”

“You’re mistaken,” he insisted. “A little encouragement from you would secure him. Perhaps he hesitates now—your modest fortune may give pause, or his friends may advise against it. But a few gentle attentions, a word here and there—those small things women can so easily offer—would settle him, even against his will. There’s no reason why you shouldn’t try. You know, attachments of that nature are out of the question—objections are insurmountable. You have too much sense not to see that. So Colonel Brandon must be the one. And I’ll do everything in my power to make him pleased with you and your family. It’s a match that will bring universal satisfaction. In fact”—he lowered his voice conspiratorially—“it will be exceedingly welcome to all parties.”

Then, as if remembering himself, he added, “That is to say—your friends are deeply concerned for your welfare. Fanny especially; she cares for you very much. And Mrs. Ferrars too—she’s a kind woman, and I’m sure she’d be delighted. She mentioned it the other day.”

Elinor offered no reply.

“It would be rather amusing,” John continued, “if Fanny married a brother and I a sister at the same time. Not unlikely, really.”

“Is Mr. Edward Ferrars going to marry?” Elinor asked firmly.

“It’s not settled,” he said, “but it’s being discussed. He has an excellent mother. Mrs. Ferrars, with great generosity, will settle a thousand pounds a year on him if the match goes through. The lady is the Honorable Miss Morton, daughter of the late Lord Morton, with thirty thousand pounds. A splendid connection on both sides—I have no doubt it will happen. A thousand a year is a great deal for a mother to give up, but Mrs. Ferrars has a noble spirit. Let me tell you another example of her generosity: as soon as we came to town, realizing our finances might be tight, she handed Fanny two hundred pounds in banknotes. Very welcome indeed—we must live extravagantly while we’re here.”

He paused, expecting sympathy. Elinor forced a response: “Your expenses in town and country must certainly be high, but your income is substantial.”

“Not as large as people think,” he replied. “I don’t complain—it’s comfortable enough, and I hope it will improve. The enclosure of Norland Common is draining us heavily. And I’ve made a recent purchase—East Kingham Farm, you remember it? Old Gibson used to live there. The land was ideal—adjacent to my own property, so I felt compelled to buy it. I couldn’t let it fall into someone else’s hands. A man must pay for convenience. It cost me a fortune.”

“More than it’s truly worth,” Elinor murmured.

“Perhaps not,” he said. “I could have sold it back the next day for more. But I might have been ruined—the stocks were so low that if I hadn’t already had the money in my banker’s hands, I’d have had to sell out at a terrible loss.”

Elinor smiled faintly.

“There are other unavoidable costs too,” he went on. “Our dear father left all the Stanhill effects at Norland to your mother—very valuable, of course. I wouldn’t dream of complaining—he had the right to dispose of his estate as he wished. But because of that, we’ve had to replace everything—linens, china, and so on. You can imagine how much that’s cost. After all this, we’re far from rich. Mrs. Ferrars’s kindness is invaluable.”

“Certainly,” Elinor said. “With her help, I hope you’ll soon be comfortably established.”

“Another year or two may do much,” he replied gravely. “But still, there’s much to be done. Fanny’s greenhouse hasn’t begun, and the flower garden is only sketched out.”

“Where will it be?” she asked.

“On the knoll behind the house. We’ve felled all the old walnut trees to clear space. It will be visible from many parts of the park, and the garden will slope down before it—very pleasing. We’ve cleared away the old thorns too.”

Elinor kept her disapproval silent, grateful that Marianne wasn’t present to witness the extravagance.

Having laid bare his financial constraints, John shifted to lighter matters. He praised Mrs. Jennings, calling her “a most valuable woman,” noting her fine home and elegant lifestyle. “Her invitation to you to come to town is a great advantage,” he said. “And her kindness suggests such regard that when she dies, you may not be forgotten. She must have considerable wealth to leave.”

“She likely won’t leave anything,” Elinor countered. “She only has her jointure, which passes to her children.”

“But surely she doesn’t spend everything,” John insisted. “Few sensible people do. Whatever she saves, she can dispose of freely.”

“And do you suppose she’d leave it to us instead of her daughters?” Elinor asked.

“Her daughters are well married,” he replied. “So there’s no need to provide further for them. But by treating you so kindly, you’ve earned a claim on her consideration—one a conscientious woman wouldn’t ignore. Her behavior is remarkably kind. She can’t possibly do all this without creating expectations.”

“But she raises none in those most involved,” Elinor said gently. “Brother, your concern for our welfare is touching—but it goes too far.”

“Oh,” he said, as if recollecting himself, “people have little control over such things. But tell me—what’s wrong with Marianne? She looks poorly, pale, thin. Is she ill?”

“She’s not well,” Elinor admitted. “She’s had a nervous complaint for weeks.”

“I’m sorry to hear it,” John said. “At her age, illness can ruin a woman’s bloom forever! Hers was so brief—last September she was the most beautiful girl I ever saw, and clearly destined to attract a fine match. There was something about her beauty that men particularly admired. Fanny once said she’d marry sooner and better than you. Not that she doesn’t love you—she does—but that was her impression. She’ll be mistaken, though. I doubt Marianne will marry anyone worth more than five or six hundred a year. But you—well, I have no doubt you’ll do better. Dorsetshire! I know little of it, but I’d be happy to learn more. And I promise you, Fanny and I will be among your earliest visitors.”

Elinor tried earnestly to dissuade him from believing Colonel Brandon would propose, but the idea brought him such pleasure he refused to abandon it. He was determined to cultivate a friendship with the colonel and encourage the match with every gesture. He carried enough guilt over neglecting his sisters to feel compelled to ensure others did much for them—an inheritance from Mrs. Jennings or a proposal from Colonel Brandon would serve as ample atonement.

They found Lady Middleton at home, and Sir John joined them before the visit ended. Warm greetings passed between them all. Sir John was eager to like everyone, and though John Dashwood knew little about horses, he quickly judged him a pleasant fellow. Lady Middleton admired his fashionable appearance and welcomed the acquaintance. John returned home delighted.

“I shall have a wonderful story for Fanny,” he said as they walked back. “Lady Middleton is truly elegant—exactly the sort Fanny would enjoy knowing. And Mrs. Jennings—most respectable, though not quite so refined as her daughter. Your sister needn’t hesitate to visit her, which is fortunate, since we’ve had reservations. We knew only that Mrs. Jennings was the widow of a man who made his fortune in trade, and Fanny and Mrs. Ferrars both assumed she and her daughters weren’t suitable company. But now I can report back with full confidence—both are admirable.”

Mrs. John Dashwood, trusting fully in her husband’s discernment, called upon Mrs. Jennings and her daughter the very next day. Her confidence was soon rewarded: she found Mrs. Jennings, though not particularly refined, quite tolerable—and Lady Middleton, she declared, was one of the most delightful women she had ever met. There was a quiet, unfeeling sort of self-absorption that bound them together, a shared preference for polished manners without depth, and a mutual appreciation for the absence of real intellect. Their mutual admiration was sincere, though born more of similarity than warmth.

But while Lady Middleton welcomed Mrs. Dashwood with open arms, Mrs. Jennings saw only a stiff, aloof woman—proud in bearing, cold in greeting—whose visit to Berkeley Street had been marked by nearly eight minutes of silence. She gave no sign of affection toward her sisters-in-law, and seemed entirely at ease in her own reserve.

Elinor longed to know whether Edward was in town, though she hesitated to ask. Fanny Dashwood, however, would never have spoken his name freely—she waited until she could announce his engagement to Miss Morton or until her husband’s hopes regarding Colonel Brandon were fulfilled. She feared their lingering attachment, and so kept them apart, word and deed alike, with careful discretion.

Yet the news came from another source. Lucy Steele arrived shortly afterward, pleading Elinor’s sympathy: Edward had reached London, but dared not come to Bartlett’s Buildings for fear of discovery. Though their longing to see each other was intense, they could do little more than write. And within days, Edward himself confirmed his presence—twice he called on the Dashwoods, leaving his card behind when they returned from their morning walks. Elinor was glad he had come; yet even gladder that she had missed him.

The Dashwoods, enchanted by the Middletons, decided to host a dinner party—a rare gesture for such reserved people. They invited the Middletons to dine at their new home in Harley Street, a comfortable house rented for three months. Mrs. Jennings and the Miss Dashwoods were included, and John Dashwood took care to invite Colonel Brandon as well. The colonel, always drawn to the Miss Dashwoods, accepted with quiet pleasure, though surprised by the warmth of his welcome.

They were to meet Mrs. Ferrars—the mother of Edward’s betrothed. Elinor did not know if her sons would attend, but the mere thought of seeing Mrs. Ferrars stirred her curiosity. Once, she might have trembled at such an encounter, fearing judgment, disapproval, or rejection. But now, she faced it with calm resolve. Her feelings for Edward were unchanged, yet her anxiety about others’ opinions had faded. Still, she longed to see the woman who had shaped his life—to understand her character, her expectations.

That longing grew sharper when she learned the Miss Steeles would also be there. Lucy, especially, had made herself agreeable to Lady Middleton, whose favor she now enjoyed. Though neither sister was elegant—Lucy lacking grace, her sister barely genteel—they had won a place at the table through their tireless attentions. It suited the Miss Steeles perfectly; they could begin their visit just before the dinner, and Lucy, who had long wished to observe the Dashwoods up close, was overjoyed at receiving Mrs. Dashwood’s invitation.

For Elinor, the news was distressing. If Edward were to attend, as his mother surely would, then she would see him again—after everything—surrounded by Lucy. The thought filled her with dread. Yet her fears were eased not by reason, but by Lucy’s kindness. When Lucy confessed Edward would not be at the dinner, and even suggested he stayed away because of his deep devotion to her, Elinor felt a strange relief. She almost believed it.

The day arrived—Tuesday, the day of reckoning. As they ascended the stairs together, Lucy clutched Elinor’s arm. “Pity me, dear Miss Dashwood!” she whispered. “There is no one here but you who truly understands how I feel. I can hardly stand. In a moment, I shall see the woman whose approval will make or break my happiness—my future mother!”

Elinor could have offered comfort by pointing out that the woman might just as easily be Miss Morton’s mother. But instead, she said sincerely, “I do pity you.” Lucy stared, astonished. She had expected envy, not sympathy—though she herself was trembling with nerves.

Mrs. Ferrars was small and thin, standing upright with rigid formality. Her face was serious, even sour, her complexion pale and sallow. Her features were plain, devoid of beauty or expression—yet a constant frown had carved lines of pride and ill temper into her brow. She spoke little, her words few and measured, as if carefully rationed. And when she did speak, none of them were directed to Elinor, whom she regarded with unmistakable disdain.

But Elinor was no longer hurt by this. A few months ago, such treatment would have wounded her deeply. Now, it meant nothing. She watched with quiet amusement as Mrs. Ferrars and Lady Middleton lavished attention on Lucy—on *her*, the girl whose claim to Edward was known only to Elinor. The contrast was painful, yet revealing. The older women smiled and praised Lucy as if she were a jewel, while Elinor sat unnoticed, ignored, yet untouched by sorrow.

And as she observed the scene, she despised them all—the insincerity, the manipulation, the petty vanity. Lucy exulted in her favor; Miss Steele, content in her own way, only wanted someone to tease about Dr. Davies. The dinner itself was grand—lavish servants, fine china, and every detail designed to show off wealth and status. Despite the Dashwoods’ recent financial strains, no trace of poverty showed, except perhaps in conversation. John Dashwood had little to say worth hearing, and Fanny even less. But then, most of the guests suffered from similar shortcomings—lacking wit, elegance, spirit, or good temper.

After dinner, the ladies retired to the drawing room. The men had carried the conversation with talk of politics, land enclosures, and horse training—but once they left, the topic narrowed sharply: the height of Harry Dashwood versus Lady Middleton’s younger son, William. Both boys were roughly the same age, but only Harry was present. So the debate became pure speculation, repeated endlessly by all.

The mothers politely claimed the other’s child was taller. The grandmothers, more openly biased, defended their own descendants with fierce conviction. Lucy, eager to please both sides, insisted both boys were remarkably tall and couldn’t imagine any difference at all. Miss Steele, with even greater finesse, praised each in turn, depending on who was listening.

Elinor, having once sided with William, offended both Mrs. Ferrars and Fanny. She did not repeat her opinion. Marianne, when asked, refused to give one—“I’ve never thought about it,” she said plainly—offending everyone further.

Earlier, Elinor had painted a pair of screens for her sister-in-law, which were now mounted and displayed in the drawing room. As the gentlemen entered, John Dashwood noticed them and, with exaggerated politeness, handed them to Colonel Brandon. “These are done by my eldest sister,” he said. “You, as a man of taste, will surely appreciate them. I don’t know if you’ve seen her work before, but she’s generally considered an excellent painter.”

Colonel Brandon, though modest about his own judgment, admired them warmly—as he would anything created by Elinor. The others, naturally curious, passed the screens around. Mrs. Ferrars, unaware of their origin, requested to see them. After Lady Middleton praised them, Fanny presented them to her mother, tactfully noting, “These were painted by Miss Dashwood.”

“Hum,” Mrs. Ferrars replied. “Very pretty.” And without looking at them again, she handed them back.

Fanny flushed slightly, perhaps thinking her mother had been too dismissive. “They are very pretty, ma’am—are they not?” she added quickly.

Then, perhaps fearing she’d been too kind, she continued, “Do you not think they resemble Miss Morton’s style? She paints most delightfully! Her last landscape—how beautifully it was done!”

“Beautifully indeed,” Mrs. Ferrars agreed. “But *she* does everything well.”

Marianne could not bear it. Already annoyed by Mrs. Ferrars’s coldness, she was enraged by this comparison—this false praise, offered at Elinor’s expense. Without hesitation, she stepped forward. “This is admiration of a very particular kind!” she exclaimed. “What is Miss Morton to us? Who knows her? Who cares? It is Elinor we think of—Elinor we speak of!”

She took the screens from Fanny’s hands and held them close, admiring them as they deserved to be admired.

Mrs. Ferrars turned sharp and angry. “Miss Morton is Lord Morton’s daughter,” she snapped.

Fanny looked furious. John Dashwood paled, horrified by his sister’s boldness.

Elinor was more distressed by Marianne’s outburst than by the insult that provoked it. But Colonel Brandon’s eyes, fixed on Marianne, revealed only tenderness—admiration for the love and loyalty that could not endure seeing her sister slighted.

Marianne did not stop. The cold arrogance of Mrs. Ferrars’s behavior—her indifference to Elinor—spoke of future pain, of struggles Elinor might face. Overcome by emotion, Marianne moved to her sister’s side, wrapped an arm around her neck, pressed her cheek to hers, and whispered fiercely, “Dear, dear Elinor—don’t mind them. Don’t let them make you unhappy.”

She could say no more. Her voice broke, and hiding her face on Elinor’s shoulder, she wept.

Everyone turned. Colonel Brandon rose instinctively. Mrs. Jennings offered her salts with a knowing “Ah! Poor dear!” Sir John, incensed, moved beside Lucy Steele and whispered a full account of the emotional scene.

In minutes, Marianne recovered enough to sit quietly among the others. But the evening’s mood remained heavy—her spirits still shaken by what had passed.

Later, John Dashwood leaned toward Colonel Brandon, speaking softly. “Poor Marianne,” he murmured. “She doesn’t have Elinor’s strength. She’s very sensitive, very nervous. You wouldn’t think it, but she was once remarkably beautiful—just as lovely as Elinor. Now… well, you can see it’s all gone.”

Elinor’s curiosity to meet Mrs. Ferrars was now fully satisfied. The encounter had revealed everything she needed to know—indeed, more than she wished to know. In Mrs. Ferrars’s demeanor, she saw not only pride and coldness, but a narrow-mindedness and prejudice so fixed that Elinor could now understand the obstacles Edward had faced in their engagement. She realized how much harder it would have been for him to marry her had he not already been bound to Lucy. And yet, even with that knowledge, she found herself grateful—for her own sake—that fate had spared her from such a connection. It was not joy she felt at Edward’s entanglement, but relief. At least, she told herself, if Lucy had been kinder, gentler, more deserving, then she ought to have rejoiced.

She could not help wondering, though, how Lucy could be so easily flattered by mere civility. How could her vanity blind her so completely? Mrs. Ferrars had treated her with politeness—not affection, not favor—but merely because she was not Elinor. Yet Lucy interpreted this as a sign of preference, as if the attention were meant solely for her. Elinor had seen it in her eyes the moment they met—and the next morning, when Lady Middleton dropped her off in Berkeley Street, Lucy confirmed it plainly enough. She came alone, hoping to find Elinor unaccompanied, and she did. A message from Mrs. Palmer soon sent Mrs. Jennings away, leaving them alone.

“My dear friend,” Lucy exclaimed as soon as they were by themselves, “I’ve come to tell you about my happiness! Wasn’t it wonderful—Mrs. Ferrars’s kindness yesterday? So warm, so gracious! You know how I feared meeting her, but the moment I was introduced, she seemed to take an instant liking to me. Don’t you see it? You saw it all—didn’t you feel it too?”

“She was certainly very civil,” Elinor replied carefully.

“Civil?” Lucy laughed. “Did you see nothing beyond that? I saw something far more—kindness meant only for me! No hauteur, no coldness—just sweetness. And your sister? She was just as lovely, just as welcoming. They’re both such charming women—so delightful! I can’t believe you never speak of how pleasant Mrs. Dashwood is.”

Elinor said nothing. There was no answer she could give without betraying her true feelings—or her growing sorrow.

“Are you ill, Miss Dashwood?” Lucy asked suddenly, leaning forward. “You look pale. You’re not speaking—surely you’re not unwell?”

“I’ve never felt better,” Elinor replied, forcing a smile.

“I’m glad,” Lucy said, sincere in her concern. “I’d hate to think you unwell—you’ve been my greatest comfort. Heaven knows what I’d do without your friendship.” She paused, then added, “And I know you care for me. Next to Edward’s love, it’s the greatest comfort I have. Poor Edward! But now there’s one good thing—we’ll be able to see each other often. Lady Middleton adores Mrs. Dashwood, so we’ll be in Harley Street frequently. And Edward spends half his time with his sister. Besides, Mrs. Ferrars and your sister both said they’d be delighted to see me again—more than once! Such kind, generous women! If you ever tell your sister what I think of her, you must praise her as highly as possible.”

Elinor gave no promise. She would not encourage Lucy to believe she would reveal anything.

“I would have known at once if Mrs. Ferrars disliked me,” Lucy continued. “If she had given me only a formal bow, said nothing, and never looked at me again—I’d have known it instantly. I couldn’t have borne it. When she dislikes someone, it’s absolute. But she didn’t—she liked me. I know she did.”

Before Elinor could respond, the door opened. The servant announced Mr. Ferrars, and Edward stepped in.

The moment was awkward—unbearably so. Each of them felt it in their faces, in their silence. They were all three together, alone, with no one else to ease the tension. The very situation they had all hoped to avoid had come to pass.

Lucy, composed and demure, made no move to speak. She allowed her emotions to show only in her eyes—soft, tender, full of quiet triumph. She offered Edward a brief greeting and said no more.

But Elinor had more to do. For Edward’s sake, for her own dignity, she forced herself to act with grace. After a moment’s pause, she welcomed him with a calmness that surprised even herself. Her voice was steady, her manner almost natural. She told him she was pleased to see him, and expressed regret that she had missed him when he visited before. She refused to let Lucy’s presence or her own pain stop her from showing the kindness due to a friend—almost a brother.

Edward, though still uneasy, sat down. His embarrassment was deeper than theirs, for he carried the weight of guilt and longing. He had no indifference like Lucy’s, nor the composure Elinor managed with effort.

Lucy remained silent. She would not contribute to the conversation. Every word spoken came from Elinor—about their mother’s health, their arrival in town, their plans. Edward barely asked a question, as if afraid to open the floodgates of feeling.

Then, Elinor made a bold decision. Pretending to fetch Marianne, she left the room. She lingered on the landing, her heart pounding, her resolve firm. She stayed long enough to give Edward and Lucy a few moments alone—then returned.

Marianne burst into the drawing-room immediately. Her joy was immediate and intense. She rushed to Edward, taking his hand, her voice bright with emotion.

“Dear Edward!” she cried. “This is a moment of great happiness—almost enough to make up for everything!”

Edward tried to return her warmth, but under Lucy’s watchful gaze, he could not say all he felt.

They sat again. Silence fell, broken only by Marianne’s quiet glances—first at Edward, then at Elinor—her eyes full of tenderness and sorrow that their reunion should be marred by Lucy’s presence.

Edward broke the silence first. “You look different, Miss Marianne,” he said gently. “I hope London agrees with you.”

“Oh, don’t think of me!” she replied, her voice trembling. “Don’t worry about my health. Elinor is well—you see that. That’s enough for us both.”

Her words were meant to comfort, but they only deepened the unease. Lucy glanced up, her expression cool.

“Do you like London?” Edward asked, eager to change the subject.

“Not at all,” Marianne answered. “I expected pleasure, but found none. Only seeing you, Edward, has brought me any comfort. Thank Heaven, you are still the same.”

She paused. No one spoke.

Then she added, “We must ask Edward to accompany us back to Barton. We’ll likely leave in a week or two. I trust he won’t mind taking charge of us.”

Edward muttered something incoherent—no one knew what.

Marianne smiled. “I see you’re nervous. But don’t worry—there’s much I want to tell you, but not now.”

She paused, then said, “Why weren’t you at Harley Street yesterday? Why didn’t you come?”

“I was engaged elsewhere.”

“Engaged? But what could be more important than meeting friends?”

“Perhaps,” Lucy interrupted quickly, “you think young men never keep engagements unless they want to. Small ones as well as big.”

Elinor frowned. But Marianne seemed untouched.

“No,” she said calmly, “I believe Edward kept his promise because he couldn’t break it. He has the most delicate conscience in the world—the most scrupulous about every commitment, no matter how small. He fears giving pain more than anyone I’ve ever known. He cannot be selfish. Edward, it’s true. And I will say it. What! Are you never to hear yourself praised? Then you are no friend of mine. Those who accept my love must also accept my praise.”

Her words were meant to honor Edward—but they stung both Lucy and Elinor. Edward rose abruptly.

“Going so soon?” Marianne asked, drawing him aside. “My dear Edward, this must not be.”

She whispered, “Lucy won’t stay long. You’ll be free soon.”

But Edward insisted on leaving. Lucy, who would have stayed longer, departed shortly after.

“What brings her here so often?” Marianne asked once they were alone. “Couldn’t she see we wanted her gone? How tiresome for Edward!”

“But why not?” Elinor replied. “She’s known him longest. It’s natural he should wish to see her as much as us.”

Marianne looked at her steadily.

“You know, Elinor,” she said, “I cannot bear this kind of talk. If you expect me to contradict you, I won’t. I won’t be tricked into denying what I know isn’t true. I cannot pretend to believe things I don’t.”

And with that, she walked out.

Elinor did not follow. She could not explain. Her promise to Lucy bound her. She could not reveal the truth, even if it meant watching Marianne suffer from a mistaken belief.

All she could hope for was that Edward would not be forced to endure such painful meetings again—and that, in time, the truth might find its way to light, without causing greater harm.

Within days of the gathering at Mrs. Jennings’s, the newspapers announced that Mrs. Thomas Palmer had safely delivered a son and heir—a piece of news that brought joy to all who already knew it, though none more so than Mrs. Jennings herself. This event, though modest in its own right, reshaped the daily rhythms of those around her. With Charlotte now confined to bed and recovering, Mrs. Jennings devoted her mornings and evenings entirely to her care, rising early and returning only late, her presence a constant fixture at the Palmer home. As a result, Elinor and Marianne found themselves obliged—by the kind but insistent wishes of the Middletons—to spend each full day in Conduit Street, under Lady Middleton’s roof.

Though they would have preferred the quiet comfort of Mrs. Jennings’s house, especially during the morning hours, they yielded without protest. But their presence there was neither welcomed nor appreciated as sincerely as it seemed to be. Lady Middleton, despite her outward politeness, harbored no real affection for them. She found their silence unflattering, their preference for books suspiciously critical, and their lack of admiration for her children deeply offensive. To her, their reserve spoke not of dignity, but of coldness; their love of reading, of hidden satire. They were, in her eyes, an awkward intrusion—dampening her idle moments and disrupting Lucy Steele’s carefully cultivated flattery.

Lucy, too, viewed the Dashwoods with unease. Their mere presence threatened her place in the household, cutting into the attention she craved. While she could not help but admire their grace and refinement, she resented them for sharing the warmth of Lady Middleton’s regard. She longed for just one moment of confessional intimacy—a full account of Marianne’s heartbreak with Mr. Willoughby—and believed such a revelation would make her forgiveness complete. Yet Elinor remained silent, indifferent to her pleas, while Marianne responded with scornful detachment. Even a lighter gesture—such as a shared laugh about the Doctor—might have softened Lucy’s resentment, but the Dashwoods offered nothing. When Sir John dined away from home, Lucy might go an entire day without hearing a single teasing word on the subject, except those she gave herself.

None of this troubled Mrs. Jennings in the least. In her cheerful ignorance, she saw only the benefit of the girls being together. Each evening, she would return from Charlotte’s side, radiant with pride, and greet Elinor and Marianne with joyful exclamation: “How delightful to escape that stupid old woman for another day!” She moved between Sir John’s house and her own, always bubbling with news—minute by minute updates on the baby’s health, his weight, his cries, and even the exact shade of his eyes. Only one thing disturbed her: Mr. Palmer’s stubborn refusal to see any distinction in his newborn son. Though she could plainly detect likenesses to both sides of the family, he insisted all babies looked alike. He refused to believe the child was anything but ordinary—let alone the finest infant in England. Her complaints echoed daily, yet he remained unmoved.

Meanwhile, Mrs. John Dashwood faced a different sort of misfortune. On the very day her sisters and Mrs. Jennings called upon her in Harley Street, another acquaintance dropped in unexpectedly. It was a chance encounter, seemingly harmless. But the visitor, carried away by imagination, mistook the Miss Dashwoods for permanent guests in the house. The error spread quickly, and within days, invitations arrived—not only for John and Fanny, but for Elinor and Marianne as well—to a small musical soirée at her home.

The consequence was immediate and unwelcome. Mrs. Dashwood was forced not only to send her carriage to collect the sisters, but also to endure the discomfort of appearing attentive to them. Worse still, she feared they might expect to be invited again. While she retained the power to refuse future invitations, the very idea of being judged for her actions stung her pride. When people choose a course they know is wrong, they resent being held to a higher standard.

Marianne, meanwhile, had grown so accustomed to daily engagements that going out had become automatic. She dressed without thought or enthusiasm, her appearance as unconcerned as if she were merely stepping into a room. Miss Steele, however, watched every detail with rapt attention. She catalogued every stitch, asked the price of each item, guessed the number of gowns Marianne owned, and hoped to discover how much her laundry cost weekly. Her scrutiny ended not with silence, but with a compliment: “Upon my word, you look vastly smart! I dare say you’ll make a great many conquests.” To Marianne, this was the ultimate impertinence—after being dissected like a specimen, she was told she’d charm men. Such words sent her quietly toward the carriage, which waited five minutes after arriving—an arrival that frustrated Mrs. Dashwood, who had hoped for a delay.

The evening itself passed uneventfully. The musical party was typical: some guests truly enjoyed the music, others did not; the performers considered themselves the finest private musicians in England. Elinor, uninterested in music and unashamed of it, allowed her gaze to wander freely. Among a group of young men, she spotted the same man who had once lectured them on toothpick cases at Gray’s. He soon turned his attention to her, speaking easily with her brother. Just as Elinor resolved to ask John for his name, he approached her himself. John introduced him as Mr. Robert Ferrars.

He greeted her with polished civility, bowing with a flourish that confirmed everything Lucy had said: he was exactly the kind of man who prided himself on appearances. Elinor felt a pang of regret—for if her affection for Edward had depended less on his character and more on the merit of his family, Robert’s arrogance might have shattered her hopes entirely. But instead of letting it harden her heart, she observed the contrast between the brothers. Robert explained it himself, speaking candidly of Edward’s shyness. “It’s not that he lacks sense,” he said, “but he was educated privately. I, on the other hand, went to Westminster. That makes all the difference. My mother often mourns it, but I tell her, ‘You must accept it. You chose to send Edward to Mr. Pratt’s instead of to school. If you’d done otherwise, he’d be quite different.’” He said this with genuine conviction, and Elinor refrained from arguing—though she could not forget the loneliness of Edward’s childhood.

Robert then remarked, “I believe you live in Devonshire—in a cottage near Dawlish?” Elinor corrected him gently, and he expressed surprise that anyone could live in Devonshire without living near Dawlish. But when she described their home, he warmed instantly. “Ah, a cottage! I adore cottages. There’s such comfort, such elegance. If I had money, I’d buy land near London and build one myself. I’d drive down whenever I pleased, gather friends, and be happy.” He recalled advising Lord Courtland, who had shown him three architectural plans. “‘My dear Courtland,’ I said, throwing them into the fire, ‘don’t build a mansion. Build a cottage.’ And I think he’ll do it.”

Elinor nodded politely. She didn’t feel the need to argue. His opinions were not worth challenging.

John Dashwood, equally unimpressed by music, found himself thinking during the evening. The mistaken belief that his sisters were staying with Mrs. Dashwood had sparked a new idea. Why not invite them properly? While Mrs. Jennings was occupied, it would be a simple kindness—one that might fulfill his father’s dying wish. The expense was negligible, the inconvenience slight. It was a duty, he decided, to ease his conscience.

Fanny, however, recoiled at the suggestion. “But they’re Lady Middleton’s visitors,” she protested. “They spend every day with her. How can we take them away?”
“Lady Middleton has no right to claim them forever,” John replied gently. “They’ve already spent a week with her. A few more days with us would be perfectly reasonable.”
Fanny hesitated. Then, with sudden resolve, she said, “I’d gladly invite them—if I could. But I’ve already promised the Miss Steeles. They’re good girls. Their uncle helped Edward. We should show our gratitude. Besides, they may not be in town next year. I’m sure you’ll like them—you already do. And Harry adores them.”

John, convinced, accepted. He would invite his sisters another year. But privately, he suspected that year would never come—because Elinor would likely be married to Colonel Brandon, and Marianne would be visiting them as a guest.

Fanny, relieved and triumphant, wrote immediately to Lucy, inviting her and her sister to stay in Harley Street as soon as Lady Middleton could spare them. The note was a triumph for Lucy. It felt as though Mrs. Dashwood herself was working on her behalf—nurturing her hopes, advancing her cause. To be near Edward, to be welcomed into his family, was her greatest desire. The invitation was a dream fulfilled. She declared the visit to Lady Middleton would end in two days’ time—though it had previously had no set limit.

When Elinor saw the letter, her own feelings shifted. Such favor, so swiftly granted, suggested Lucy’s goodwill stemmed from something deeper than mere spite. Perhaps, with time and effort, it could grow into something powerful. Lucy had already won over Lady Middleton with her flattery and touched Mrs. John Dashwood with her charm. If she could win them, perhaps she could win more.

The Miss Steeles moved into Harley Street. Sir John, visiting frequently, returned with glowing reports. Mrs. Dashwood, he said, had never been so pleased with any young women in her life. She had given each a needlebook made by an emigrant, called Lucy by her first name, and lamented that she might never part with them.

And so, the second volume came to a close.

After a fortnight of careful nursing, Mrs. Palmer had recovered sufficiently for her mother to resume her usual routine, no longer needing to dedicate every moment to her care. With only occasional visits to check on her daughter’s well-being, Mrs. Jennings returned to her own home and habits—her cheerful presence once again filling Berkeley Street with warmth and lively conversation. The Dashwood sisters welcomed the return of familiar rhythms, finding comfort in the quiet stability of their daily lives.

It was on the third or fourth morning after this renewed sense of normalcy that Mrs. Jennings burst into the drawing-room, her face flushed with urgency and excitement. Elinor, seated alone by the window, looked up as her friend approached with the air of one bearing a secret too immense to contain.

“Lord! my dear Miss Dashwood,” she exclaimed, “have you heard the news?”

“No, ma’am,” Elinor replied calmly. “What is it?”

“Something so astonishing!” Mrs. Jennings leaned forward, lowering her voice as if afraid even the walls might betray the tale. “When I arrived at Mr. Palmer’s, Charlotte was in such a state about the baby—crying, fretting, covered in little red spots. She was convinced it was seriously ill. But I took one look and said, ‘Lord! my dear, it’s nothing but the red gum.’ Nurse agreed. Still, Charlotte wouldn’t be placated, so Mr. Donavan was summoned—and luckily, he’d just come from Harley Street, so he came right over. As soon as he saw the child, he confirmed what we’d said: just the red gum. Charlotte relaxed immediately.”

Mrs. Jennings paused, gathering her breath before delivering the real news.

“But then—just as he was leaving—I suddenly thought to ask him if there was any other news. And do you know? He smirked, glanced around, and gave me this grave look. Then, in a whisper, he said, ‘For fear any unpleasant report reaches the young ladies under your care regarding their sister’s indisposition, I think it best to say that I believe there is no great cause for alarm; I hope Mrs. Dashwood will do very well.’”

Elinor’s heart skipped a beat. “Fanny ill?”

“That’s exactly what I said!” Mrs. Jennings clapped her hands. “‘Lord!’ I cried, ‘is Mrs. Dashwood unwell?’ And then—it all poured out. It seems that Edward Ferrars—the very young man I used to tease you about (though now, thank goodness, I’m glad nothing ever came of it)—has been secretly engaged to my cousin Lucy Steele for over a year!”

She leaned back, eyes wide. “Can you imagine it? Not a soul knew—except poor Nancy, who couldn’t keep her mouth shut. They were both so careful, afraid of Mrs. Ferrars, and neither your brother nor sister had the faintest idea until today. And then—poor Nancy, always meaning well but not much of a thinker—she blurted it all out. She thought, ‘They adore Lucy; they’ll have no objections,’ and off she went to tell Fanny, who was sitting quietly at her carpet-work, completely unaware of what was coming. Just five minutes earlier, she’d been telling your brother she hoped to match Edward with some nobleman’s daughter—can you believe it?”

Elinor felt her breath catch. She could only imagine the devastation.

“She fell into violent hysterics,” Mrs. Jennings continued, “screams so loud they reached your brother’s ears as he sat downstairs writing to his steward. He rushed upstairs, and chaos broke loose. By then, Lucy had already arrived, blissfully ignorant of the storm brewing. Poor thing! I feel so sorry for her. Your sister scolded like a fury, drove Lucy into a fainting fit, and Nancy fell to her knees, sobbing. Your brother paced the room, helpless. Mrs. Dashwood declared they must leave immediately, and he had to beg on his knees just to let them stay long enough to pack. Then *she* collapsed again—hysterics, again—and he sent for Mr. Donavan. When he arrived, the house was in ruins. The carriage waited at the door, and they were stepping inside when he came. Lucy, he says, could barely walk. Nancy was nearly as bad.”

Mrs. Jennings shook her head. “I can’t stand your sister. I truly hope this marriage goes through despite her. Think of poor Edward—how shattered he’ll be when he hears! To have his love treated so cruelly! They say he’s deeply fond of her—no wonder. I wouldn’t be surprised if he flies into a rage. Mr. Donavan agrees. And the best part? He’s gone straight back to Harley Street, ready to attend Mrs. Ferrars when she’s told. She was summoned as soon as the cousins left. Your sister was certain *she* would fall apart too. Well, maybe she will—but I don’t care. I’ve no patience for people making such a fuss over money and status. Why shouldn’t Edward marry Lucy? Mrs. Ferrars can afford to provide for her son. Lucy may have next to nothing, but she knows how to make the most of what she has. If Mrs. Ferrars gives him five hundred a year, Lucy will live as well as anyone with eight. They could settle in a cottage like yours—or slightly larger—with two maids and two men. And I might even help them find a housemaid—Betty’s sister is looking for work, and she’d suit perfectly.”

Mrs. Jennings paused, catching her breath. Elinor, having collected herself, responded with measured calm, offering thoughtful observations without betraying her inner turmoil. She was relieved—not to be suspected of lingering feelings for Edward, nor to have Marianne present to witness her composure. For the first time in months, she could speak freely, with dignity and fairness.

She found herself torn between pity for Edward and disdain for Lucy, though she forced herself to acknowledge the latter’s merit. Yet she could not bring herself to admire her. Her thoughts turned instead to the inevitable outcome—Edward and Lucy united, against all odds.

But she knew Marianne would not hear such news lightly. It was her duty to prepare her sister, to reveal the truth gently, to shield her from further pain. She would have to break the news, knowing it would shatter Marianne’s hopes and stir deep resentment.

The task was painful. She was about to remove what she believed was Marianne’s greatest comfort—her belief in Edward’s devotion—and replace it with a harsh reality that would likely destroy her regard for him forever. Worse still, she feared Marianne would see parallels between their situations, reliving her own heartbreak all over again.

Yet Elinor resolved to act with honesty and grace. She did not wish to dwell on her own sorrow, nor to appear weak. Instead, she spoke plainly, simply, her voice steady despite the emotion beneath. She described the engagement, its length, and the circumstances—without melodrama, without despair.

Marianne listened in silence at first, then burst into tears. Her grief was immediate and overwhelming. Elinor, ever the anchor, offered reassurance—calm words, firm assurances of Edward’s character, defending him against blame except for imprudence. But Marianne refused to accept it.

“Edward is just like Willoughby!” she cried. “And you—*you* loved him truly. How could you bear it?”

Elinor met her gaze steadily. “I did love him. But I loved others more than myself. I kept silent because I owed it to Lucy, to our family, to you—to spare you all from suffering I could not ease.”

Marianne stared, stunned. “Four months? You’ve known for four months? While I was sick, while I accused you of being happy—*you* carried this burden?”

“I could not tell you,” Elinor said softly. “It would have destroyed you. And I could not betray my promise to Lucy. I had to pretend indifference, while my heart broke. I endured her triumph, her repeated joy in my misfortune. I knew Edward was lost to me—yet I found no fault in him. Nothing proved him unworthy. Nothing showed he cared less for me. I suffered the consequences of love without its rewards. And all this, while I was already grieving for you.”

Marianne fell silent, her anger melting into shame. “Oh, Elinor,” she whispered, “I have been cruel. You’ve been my only comfort, and I’ve repaid you with bitterness. I cannot forgive myself.”

Tears streamed down her cheeks as she embraced her sister. In that moment, Elinor felt a rare peace. She asked Marianne to promise never to speak ill of Lucy, to meet her with kindness, and even to greet Edward with the same warmth as before—if fate brought them together.

Marianne agreed, her voice trembling but sincere.

The next morning, their brother arrived, his face grave. He sat down heavily and began, “You’ve heard, I suppose, about the terrible discovery made yesterday?”

They nodded, unable to speak.

“Your sister has suffered terribly,” he continued. “And Mrs. Ferrars—oh, it’s been a scene of unbearable distress. But I hope we’ll weather it. Poor Fanny—she was in hysterics all day. But Donavan says there’s no serious danger. Her constitution is strong, and her spirit unshakable. She says she’ll never trust anyone again—after such betrayal, such ingratitude! She had invited these girls out of kindness, thinking them harmless, pleasant companions. We had wanted to invite you and Marianne, but she insisted on hosting them. And now—this reward! ‘I wish,’ she said, ‘with all my heart, we had asked your sisters instead.’”

He paused, expecting gratitude, which came swiftly.

“And Mrs. Ferrars?” Elinor asked.

“Her reaction was worse,” John said. “When Fanny told her, she was devastated.
