She’d been planning a grand future for Edward—had imagined him marrying Miss Morton, with a fortune of thirty thousand pounds. She never dreamed he’d been secretly engaged to someone else. ‘There,’ she said, ‘I thought I was safe.’ She was in agony. We discussed what to do, and she decided to send for Edward.”

He hesitated. “He came. But I regret to say… nothing worked. All of Mrs. Ferrars’s arguments—about duty, about wealth, about his future—fell on deaf ears. She offered the Norfolk estate, clear of taxes, worth a thousand a year. Then twelve hundred. She promised him his two thousand pounds, but warned him she would never see him again. She vowed to block his career if he chose this path. Yet Edward stood firm. He refused to break his word.”

Marianne gasped. “Gracious God—could he really be so stubborn?”

“He was,” John said. “He said little, but everything he said was resolute. Nothing would make him give up his engagement.”

Mrs. Jennings, unable to hold back, interrupted. “Then he’s acted like a man of honour! I beg your pardon, Mr. Dashwood, but if he hadn’t, I’d have called him a coward. Lucy is my cousin, and she’s one of the kindest girls alive—deserves a good husband.”

John blinked, surprised, but replied with polite restraint. “I wouldn’t speak ill of any relation of yours, madam. Miss Steele is undoubtedly a fine young woman. But in this case, the match is impossible. A secret engagement with a man under her uncle’s care—especially one whose mother is so wealthy—seems rather extraordinary. Still, I don’t mean to criticize anyone you care for. We all wish her happiness. Mrs. Ferrars has acted with dignity and generosity. Edward has chosen his path, and I fear it will be a difficult one.”

Marianne sighed, her eyes distant.

“Well, sir,” Mrs. Jennings asked, “how did it end?”

“With a complete rupture,” John said. “Edward is disowned. He left yesterday. Where he is now, or whether he’s still in town, I don’t know. We cannot inquire.”

“Poor young man,” Mrs. Jennings murmured. “What will become of him?”

“What indeed?” John echoed. “It’s heartbreaking. Born to such prosperity—now reduced to living on the interest of two thousand pounds. And consider—within three months, he could have had two thousand five hundred a year. I can’t imagine a more miserable fate. We all feel for him. But there’s nothing we can do.”

“I’d welcome him at my house,” Mrs. Jennings declared. “Bed and board—anyone would be welcome. He shouldn’t be scraping by in lodgings.”

Elinor smiled faintly, touched by the gesture—even if delivered with Mrs. Jennings’s usual bluntness.

“If he’d only done what his friends wished,” John said, “he’d be in his rightful place, wanting for nothing. But now—there’s nothing anyone can do. And there’s one more blow—hearing that Mrs. Ferrars intends to settle the estate on Robert immediately, rather than wait for Edward’s conditions. I left her this morning with her lawyer, finalizing the details.”

Mrs. Jennings scoffed. “That’s her revenge. Everyone has their way. But I wouldn’t punish one son just because another made me angry.”

Marianne rose, pacing slowly. “How galling,” John continued, “for a man to see his younger brother inherit what should have been his own. Poor Edward. I truly feel for him.”

Mrs. Jennings was effusive in her admiration of Edward’s behavior, but only Elinor and Marianne truly grasped the depth of his sacrifice. They alone understood how little temptation he had faced to stray from duty, and how meager the comfort he could find—beyond the quiet pride of doing what was right—in losing both fortune and friends. Elinor took pride in his steadfastness; Marianne, though once so quick to judge, now softened toward him, moved by pity for the pain he endured. Their bond, once strained by silence and suspicion, was now mended, yet neither sister found it easy to speak of what had passed when they were alone.

Elinor avoided the subject deliberately—fearing that Marianne’s fervent assurances of Edward’s enduring love might only deepen her own lingering hope, a feeling she wished to suppress. For Marianne, conversation on the matter brought only sorrow. She could not help comparing her own impulsive heart to Elinor’s measured restraint, and each time, she felt the sting of regret more keenly. She saw clearly how much better her sister had handled their shared trial, but instead of inspiring resolve, this awareness only deepened her sense of failure. She mourned not for what might be, but for what she had not done—her courage spent long ago, leaving behind only guilt and despair.

For several days, no new news reached them from Harley Street or Bartlett’s Buildings. Yet Mrs. Jennings, ever eager to extend her kindness, had made up her mind to visit her cousins as soon as possible. Only an unusual influx of guests had delayed her journey. On the third day after the truth came to light, a fine March Sunday drew many to Kensington Gardens. Mrs. Jennings and Elinor went together, but Marianne stayed behind—still afraid of encountering the Willoughbys, whose presence stirred old wounds she had not yet healed.

Soon after entering the gardens, they met a close friend of Mrs. Jennings’s, who joined them readily. Her lively chatter absorbed Mrs. Jennings’s attention, giving Elinor a rare moment of peace to reflect. She saw neither Edward nor the Willoughbys, and for a while, nothing stirred her thoughts. Then, to her surprise, Miss Steele approached them with a bright smile.

“I’m so delighted to see you!” she declared, linking arms with Elinor. “I’ve been longing to tell you something.” Lowering her voice, she added, “I suppose Mrs. Jennings has heard all about it. Is she angry?”

“Not at all,” Elinor replied gently. “I don’t believe she is.”

“Oh, thank goodness! And Lady Middleton—she isn’t cross, is she?”

“I can’t imagine why she would be.”

Miss Steele breathed a sigh of relief. “I’ve had such a dreadful time! Lucy was furious—vowed she’d never make me another bonnet, or do anything for me again. But now she’s calmed down, and we’re as close as ever. Look—she made this bow for my hat last night and tucked in the feather herself. You’ll laugh at me, I know. But why shouldn’t I wear pink ribbons? It’s the Doctor’s favorite color, after all. Honestly, I didn’t even realize he preferred it until he mentioned it. My cousins have been driving me mad—sometimes I don’t know where to look!”

She drifted into a tangent, and Elinor, sensing the need to steer the conversation back, said quietly, “Well then, Miss Dashwood—people may say what they like about Mr. Ferrars declaring he wouldn’t marry Lucy, but it’s simply not true. It’s shameful that such rumors should spread.”

“I’ve never heard a word like it,” Elinor assured her.

“Oh, but it *was* said—by more than one person. Miss Godby told Miss Sparks that no sensible person could expect Edward to give up Miss Morton, with her thirty thousand pounds, for Lucy, who has nothing. And Miss Sparks told me. Besides, my cousin Richard said himself that when it came down to it, he feared Edward would break off the engagement. When Edward didn’t come near us for three whole days, I was beside myself. I think Lucy gave up all hope—after Wednesday, we saw nothing of him until Saturday. Once she thought of writing to him, but then her spirits lifted. But this morning, just as we returned from church, he arrived. That’s when everything came out. He’d been summoned to Harley Street, spoken to by his mother and family, and before them all, he declared he loved only Lucy, and would marry no one else. The talk upset him terribly, and as soon as he left, he rode into the country, staying at an inn Thursday and Friday to collect himself. After thinking it through, he realized that now he had no fortune, no prospects—only two thousand pounds, and no hope of more—he couldn’t ask Lucy to wait. If he entered the clergy, he’d likely get only a curacy. How could they live on that? He couldn’t bear the idea of her suffering. So he begged her—if she had any wish to end it—to do so at once, and let him fend for himself. I heard every word. And he said it all for *her* sake, not his own. I swear he never hinted at being tired of her, or wanting to marry Miss Morton. Not a word.”

“But Lucy wouldn’t listen,” Miss Steele continued. “She told him immediately—oh, with all the sweet talk and lovey-dovey nonsense you know—she had no desire to break off. She said she could live on very little, and however small his income, she’d be happy to share it all. And so he was overjoyed. They talked about what to do next, and decided he should enter the clergy right away. They’ll wait to marry until he secures a living. But then—I couldn’t hear anymore, because my cousin called from below: Mrs. Richardson had arrived in her coach and wanted one of us to join her. I ran upstairs, put on a pair of silk stockings, and hurried off with them.”

“I don’t understand,” Elinor said, startled. “You mean you interrupted them? You were all in the same room?”

“Oh, heavens no!” Miss Steele laughed. “Do you really think people confess their feelings when others are around? No indeed. They were shut up in the drawing-room, and I only heard bits by standing at the door.”

“You listened at the door?” Elinor exclaimed. “And now you’ve repeated it to me? I wish I’d known—because I certainly wouldn’t have allowed you to tell me details of a conversation you had no right to overhear. How could you treat your sister so unfairly?”

“Oh, dear,” Miss Steele giggled. “It’s nothing. I just stood outside and listened. And Lucy would have done the same for me—remember Martha Sharpe and I used to hide in closets to spy on each other? That’s just how things are.”

Elinor tried to change the subject, but Miss Steele quickly returned to Edward. “He talks of going to Oxford soon,” she said. “But now he’s lodging in Pall Mall. What a horrid woman his mother is, isn’t she? And your brother and sister weren’t very kind either. Still, I won’t say anything against them to you—they did send us home in their carriage, which was more than I expected. And I was terrified your sister would ask for the huswifes she gave us a few days ago. But nothing was said, and I made sure to keep mine hidden.”

Edward, she explained, had business at Oxford and must go there for a time. Afterward, he hoped to find a bishop willing to ordain him. “I wonder what curacy he’ll get!” she giggled. “I know exactly what my cousins will say when they hear—‘Why don’t you write to the Doctor and get Edward the new living?’ But I’d never do such a thing. ‘La!’ I’ll say, ‘How could you think of such a thing? Me write to the Doctor?’”

“Well,” Elinor said dryly, “at least you’re prepared for the worst.”

Miss Steele was about to respond when her party approached. “Oh, here come the Richardsons! I’ve so much more to tell you, but I can’t stay. They’re very respectable people—Mr. Richardson makes a fortune, and they keep their own coach. I don’t have time to speak to Mrs. Jennings, but please tell her I’m delighted she’s not angry with us, and that Lady Middleton feels the same. If you and your sister ever leave town and Mrs. Jennings needs company, we’d be honored to stay with her as long as she likes. I suppose Lady Middleton won’t invite us again this season. Goodbye—I’m sorry Miss Marianne wasn’t here. Do remember me kindly to her. La! You’re not wearing your spotted muslin—are you afraid it’ll tear?”

That was her final concern. She hurried off to greet Mrs. Richardson, leaving Elinor alone with a heart full of reflection. She had learned nothing new—only confirmation of what she already suspected. Edward’s marriage to Lucy remained certain, though the timing hung entirely on his securing a living—a goal that seemed distant indeed. Everything rested on a future uncertain, just as Elinor had foreseen.

As they returned to the carriage, Mrs. Jennings demanded updates. But Elinor, unwilling to spread gossip gained so unfairly, limited her account to what Lucy would likely want known—the continuation of their engagement and their plans to wait for a living. This prompted Mrs. Jennings to remark, “Wait for a living? We all know how that ends. They’ll wait a year, find nothing, and settle for a fifty-pound curacy, plus interest on his two thousand pounds and whatever Mr. Steele and Mr. Pratt can spare. Then they’ll have a child every year! Lord help them—how poor they’ll be! I must see what I can do to help furnish their house. Two maids and two men? No, no—they’ll need a strong girl who can do everything. Betty’s sister would never suit them now.”

The next morning, Elinor received a letter from Lucy, delivered by the two-penny post:

“My dear Miss Dashwood, I hope you’ll forgive me for writing to you, but I know your kindness toward me will make you pleased to hear how well Edward and I are, after all our trials. Thank God, though we’ve suffered greatly, we are both well and as happy as we shall always be in each other’s love. We’ve faced great hardships and cruel persecution, but we’re grateful for the friends we’ve kept—yourself among them. Your kindness will always be remembered by me and by Edward, whom I’ve told of it.

I’m sure you’ll be glad to hear that I spent two lovely hours with Edward yesterday. He refused to part with me, though I urged him to for prudence’s sake. I insisted he let me go, believing it my duty—but he would not hear of it. He said he cared nothing for his mother’s anger as long as he had my love. Our prospects aren’t bright, to be sure, but we’ll wait and hope. He’ll be ordained soon, and if you ever have the chance to recommend him to anyone with a living to offer, I’m certain you won’t forget us. Dear Mrs. Jennings too—I trust she’ll speak for us to Sir John or Mr. Palmer, or any friend who might help.

Poor Anne was wrong to act as she did, but she meant well, so I say nothing. I hope Mrs. Jennings won’t mind if she comes to call—what a kindness it would be! My cousins would be proud to meet her.

My paper reminds me to conclude. Please convey my deepest gratitude and respect to Mrs. Jennings, Sir John, Lady Middleton, and the dear children when you see them. And send my love to Miss Marianne.

I am, &c.”

Elinor read it once, then handed it to Mrs. Jennings, knowing it was meant for her eyes. Mrs. Jennings read aloud with warm approval.

“Very well indeed! How nicely she writes! Oh, yes—that was quite proper of her to let him go if he wanted to. Just like Lucy. Poor soul! I wish I could get him a living, with all my heart. She calls me ‘dear Mrs. Jennings’—see? She’s a good-hearted girl. Very well, indeed. That sentence is beautifully turned. Yes, yes—I’ll certainly go to see her. How thoughtful she is, remembering everyone! Thank you, my dear, for showing it to me. It’s the prettiest letter I’ve ever seen—and does Lucy’s heart and head great credit.”

The Miss Dashwoods had now been in town for more than two months, and Marianne’s longing to return home grew stronger with each passing day. She sighed for the open air, the peace of the countryside, and the quiet simplicity of Barton—where she believed true comfort might still be found. Elinor, though more composed, shared her sister’s desire to leave, though she was careful not to rush into plans that could strain their strength or cause discomfort. She understood the burden of such a journey, especially on one so unwell as Marianne, who refused to acknowledge any such limitations. Still, Elinor began to seriously consider how best to make their departure possible. She had already spoken to Mrs. Jennings about their wish to go, and the kind hostess responded with heartfelt resistance, pleading every reason to keep them longer.

But then came a proposal that, though it would delay their return by a few weeks, seemed far more suitable than any other option. The Palmers were planning to move to Cleveland at the end of March for the Easter holidays, and Charlotte Palmer had extended a warm invitation to both Miss Dashwoods and Mrs. Jennings to join them. While this arrangement might have seemed too familiar for Elinor’s delicate sense of propriety, Mr. Palmer himself spoke with such genuine courtesy and kindness—especially since his behavior toward them had improved greatly since learning of his sister-in-law’s unhappiness—that Elinor felt no hesitation in accepting.

When she told Marianne of her decision, however, her sister reacted with immediate distress. “Cleveland!” she exclaimed, her voice trembling. “No, I cannot go to Cleveland.”
“You forget,” Elinor said gently, “that its location is not near—”
“But it is in Somersetshire,” Marianne interrupted, her eyes glistening. “I cannot go into Somersetshire. There—where I once hoped to go… No, Elinor, you cannot expect me to set foot there.”

Elinor did not argue against the sentiment; instead, she sought to redirect Marianne’s thoughts. She reminded her sister that going to Cleveland would allow them to return to Barton sooner than they otherwise could—within just over three weeks. From Cleveland, only a few miles from Bristol, the journey back to Barton would take but a single long day. Their mother’s servant could meet them there, and they need not stay longer than a week. With such practical advantages, and knowing how deeply Marianne loved their mother, Elinor trusted that affection would outweigh sorrow.

Mrs. Jennings, far from wanting to part with her guests, pressed them to return with her from Cleveland. Elinor appreciated the gesture, but it did not change her mind. With their mother’s approval secured, all arrangements were made as thoroughly as possible. And slowly, Marianne began to find comfort in counting down the hours until they could finally leave London behind.

“Ah, Colonel,” Mrs. Jennings remarked when he first called after their departure was settled, “I do not know what you and I shall do without the Miss Dashwoods. They’re determined to go home with the Palmers, and I shan’t know what to do when I come back! Lord! We’ll sit and stare at each other like two bored cats.”

She may have meant this as a playful exaggeration—but perhaps also as a subtle hint. For soon after, when Elinor moved to the window to measure a print she intended to copy for a friend, the Colonel followed her there, his expression thoughtful, and they conversed quietly for several minutes. Mrs. Jennings, pretending not to listen, had changed her seat to sit beside the piano where Marianne played, yet she could not help noticing Elinor’s flushed face and agitated manner. Her attention was fully absorbed by what the Colonel said, and her copying paused entirely.

Then, as Marianne transitioned between pieces, a few words drifted clearly to Mrs. Jennings: “I am afraid it cannot take place very soon.”
Startled, she nearly cried out—then stopped herself, muttering under her breath, “This is strange! Surely he need not wait to grow older?”

Yet despite the apparent delay, Elinor showed no sign of disappointment. When they parted, Mrs. Jennings distinctly heard her say, in a voice full of sincerity, “I shall always think myself very much obliged to you.”
Delighted by the warmth of her gratitude, Mrs. Jennings marveled that the Colonel could leave so calmly, without even acknowledging it. She had never imagined him so indifferent a suitor.

But what truly passed between them was far different from what she suspected.

“I have heard,” the Colonel said, with deep compassion, “of the injustice your friend Mr. Ferrars has suffered at the hands of his family. If I understand correctly, he has been cast aside for standing firm in his engagement to a most worthy young woman. Is this true?”
Elinor confirmed it.

“The cruelty of it,” he replied, “the sheer imprudence of tearing apart two people so long devoted to one another, is heartbreaking. Mrs. Ferrars does not know what she may be driving her son to. I’ve seen Mr. Ferrars a few times in Harley Street, and I find him admirable—a man of quiet dignity and good character. Though one cannot become intimately acquainted with him quickly, I’ve seen enough to wish him well—for his own sake, and because he is dear to you. I understand he intends to enter the clergy. Would you do me the favor of telling him that the living of Delaford, recently vacated as I learned today, is his if he chooses to accept it? Of course, given his present circumstances, it may seem a poor offering. But I hope it may still be of some use. It is a small rectory—the late incumbent earned no more than two hundred pounds a year. Though it could be improved, I fear not enough to provide a comfortable income. Still, I would take great pleasure in giving it to him. Please convey my offer to him.”

Elinor was stunned—her astonishment equal to what she might have felt had he proposed marriage. A position she had thought utterly beyond Edward’s reach was now offered to him, and she—of all people—was chosen to deliver it. Her emotion was profound, though not born of romantic hope. It was gratitude, respect, and admiration for the Colonel’s generosity and kindness. She thanked him sincerely, praising Edward’s integrity and devotion, and promised to carry the message if he truly wished her to.

Yet she could not help adding, “No one could perform this task better than yourself.”
And indeed, she would have preferred to spare Edward the burden of receiving a favor through her. But the Colonel, equally sensitive to delicacy, declined the role himself, insisting she should act as his messenger. So she agreed, grateful to be able to serve.

Edward, she believed, was still in town—and fortunately, she had learned his address from Miss Steele. She would inform him of the offer before the day ended.

Afterward, the Colonel spoke of his own happiness at having such a respectable neighbor, and then, with gentle regret, mentioned that his house was small and modest.
Elinor, seeing the concern, reassured him: “The size of the house matters little. It will suit their family and means perfectly.”

The Colonel was surprised—this response revealed that she already saw Edward’s marriage as inevitable. He had assumed the living was too insignificant to support a wife. “It can barely sustain a bachelor,” he said. “It cannot enable him to marry. My influence ends here. But if fortune ever allows me to assist him further, I would do so gladly—though I must confess, I see no way forward now. What I offer today seems almost nothing, since it brings him so little closer to the true joy he seeks. His marriage remains distant—at least, I fear it cannot happen soon.”

That was the sentence Mrs. Jennings had misheard, thinking it a refusal of love. But now, understanding the truth of their conversation, Elinor’s gratitude appears not less sincere, nor less rightly expressed—whether the gift was a hand in marriage or a future in life.

“Well, Miss Dashwood,” Mrs. Jennings said, her eyes twinkling with knowing amusement as soon as the gentleman had left the room, “I won’t ask you what Colonel Brandon has been saying to you—though, by my word, I _did_ try to stay out of earshot, I couldn’t help catching enough to understand the whole affair. And I assure you, I’ve never been more delighted in all my life! I wish you joy from the bottom of my heart.”

“Thank you, ma’am,” Elinor replied, her cheeks warm and her voice soft with emotion. “It truly is a great joy to me—and I feel so deeply the kindness of Colonel Brandon. There are few men who would act with such generosity. Few hearts so full of compassion! I must confess, I was utterly astonished.”

“Oh, nonsense, my dear!” Mrs. Jennings laughed. “You’re far too modest. I wasn’t surprised at all—I’ve thought for weeks now that this was bound to happen.”

“You judged rightly by his character,” Elinor said gently, “but surely you couldn’t have foreseen that the moment would come so quickly?”

“Opportunity?” Mrs. Jennings repeated, waving her hand airily. “Why, when a man’s mind is made up, opportunity finds him—don’t you worry about that. Well, my dear, I wish you joy again and again. If there’s ever been a pair meant for each other, I think I’ll soon know where to find them.”

“You mean to go to Delaford after them, I suppose?” Elinor asked, a faint smile touching her lips.

“Aye, indeed I do,” Mrs. Jennings declared. “And as for the house being in poor repair—well, I don’t see what he’s complaining about. It’s as fine a place as any I’ve seen!”

“He mentioned it was in need of some attention,” Elinor said.

“And whose fault is that?” Mrs. Jennings retorted sharply. “Why doesn’t he fix it? Who else should?”

Their conversation was interrupted by the servant announcing the carriage was ready. Mrs. Jennings immediately rose, bustling toward the door.

“Well, my dear,” she said, pausing only briefly, “I must be off before I’ve half finished my talk. But we’ll have it all over tonight—we’ll be quite alone then. I won’t ask you to come with me; I can tell your mind’s too full to care for company. Besides, you’ll want to tell Marianne everything.”

Marianne had already left the room.

“I shall certainly tell Marianne,” Elinor replied, “but I won’t mention it to anyone else just yet.”

“Oh, very well,” Mrs. Jennings said, visibly disappointed. “Then you wouldn’t have me tell Lucy? I’m thinking of going as far as Holborn today.”

“No, ma'am,” Elinor answered firmly, “not even Lucy, if you please. A day’s delay won’t matter much. And until I’ve written to Mr. Ferrars, I think it ought not to be spoken of to anyone. I’ll write to him straight away—it’s important no time is lost. He has much to attend to regarding his ordination.”

Mrs. Jennings stared, puzzled. Why such haste to inform Mr. Ferrars? She pondered for a moment, then her face lit up with sudden understanding.

“Oh, ho!” she exclaimed. “I see! Mr. Ferrars is to be the one! Well, all the better for him. Of course he must be ordained in time. I’m glad things are moving along so nicely between you. But, my dear—isn’t this rather odd? Shouldn’t the Colonel write himself? Surely he’s the one best suited.”

Elinor didn’t fully grasp the beginning of her remark, nor did she feel inclined to question it. She simply responded to the end.

“Colonel Brandon is so sensitive,” she explained, “that he’d rather someone else deliver the news to Mr. Ferrars than speak of it himself.”

“And so you’re the one who has to do it,” Mrs. Jennings observed, shaking her head. “Well, that’s an unusual kind of delicacy! But I won’t keep you—seeing you’re preparing to write. You know your own affairs best. So good-bye, my dear. I haven’t heard such happy news since Charlotte gave birth.”

And off she went—but returned almost instantly.

“I just thought of Betty’s sister,” she said. “She’d make a splendid mistress for her. Though whether she’d suit as a lady’s maid, I can’t say. She’s excellent at housework and needlework. You’ll consider it later, I suppose?”

“Certainly, ma'am,” Elinor replied, barely listening, her thoughts already turning to the letter she must write. How to begin? What to say? The situation between her and Edward made even the simplest words difficult. She feared both saying too much and too little, and sat frozen, pen poised, until Edward himself entered the room.

He had met Mrs. Jennings at the door on his way to leave a farewell card. She, after apologizing for not returning herself, had insisted he come in, saying Miss Dashwood was upstairs and wished to speak with him on urgent business.

Elinor had just told herself that, however hard it might be to write, at least it would be easier than speaking aloud—when he appeared, forcing her into the most daunting task of all. Her surprise and embarrassment were overwhelming. She hadn’t seen him since his engagement became known—since he knew she was aware of it. And now, with the weight of what she had been thinking, and what she had to tell him, she felt utterly unsteady.

Edward, too, seemed troubled. They sat down together, both silent, both awkward, both caught in a quiet tension. He could not remember if he had apologized for intruding upon her first—but to be safe, he offered a formal one as soon as he could speak.

“Mrs. Jennings told me you wanted to speak with me,” he said. “At least, I understood her to say so—or I certainly wouldn’t have come in like this. Though I’d have been very sorry to leave London without seeing you and your sister—especially since it may be some time before I meet you again. I’m leaving for Oxford tomorrow.”

“You wouldn’t have gone,” Elinor said, gathering herself and determined to get through it quickly, “without our best wishes—even if we couldn’t give them in person. Mrs. Jennings was right. I have something important to tell you—one I was about to write. I’ve been entrusted with a pleasant duty.” Her voice trembled slightly. “Colonel Brandon—just here ten minutes ago—asked me to tell you that, knowing you intend to take holy orders, he’s pleased to offer you the living at Delaford, which has just become vacant. He regrets it isn’t more substantial, but he hopes it will serve you well.”

She paused, breathless. “Allow me to congratulate you on having such a noble and discerning friend—and to share his hope that the living—about two hundred pounds a year—were more generous, so it might support you fully, not merely provide temporary comfort. In short, so it might fulfill all your hopes for happiness.”

Edward sat stunned. He could not speak. His face showed every trace of astonishment, disbelief, and gratitude—all at once. Yet all he managed was, “Colonel Brandon?”

“Yes,” Elinor said, gaining strength as the worst passed. “Colonel Brandon means it as a mark of his sympathy for what you’ve endured—for the unjust treatment by your family, which has placed you in such distress—a feeling I’m sure Marianne, myself, and all your friends share. And also as a sign of his high regard for your character, and his approval of your conduct in this matter.”

“Colonel Brandon offering _me_ a living!” Edward whispered. “Is it possible?”

“The cruelty of your own family has made you doubt kindness anywhere,” Elinor said gently.

“No,” he replied, suddenly aware, “not kindness from _you_. I cannot forget that to you—to your goodness—I owe everything. I feel it—I would express it if I could—but as you know, I am no speaker.”

“You’re mistaken,” Elinor said firmly. “You owe it entirely—almost entirely—to your own merit and Colonel Brandon’s recognition of it. I had no part in it. I didn’t even know the living was vacant until I learned of his plan. Nor had it ever occurred to me that he might hold such power. As a friend of mine, perhaps he takes greater pleasure in giving it to you—but I swear, you owe nothing to my influence.”

She hesitated, truth compelling her to admit a small role, yet unwilling to appear as the giver of his fortune. That hesitation likely confirmed in Edward’s mind a suspicion already forming.

For a moment, he sat deep in thought. Then, as if with effort, he said, “Colonel Brandon seems a man of great worth. I’ve always heard him praised, and I know your brother holds him in high esteem. He’s undoubtedly sensible—and perfectly gentlemanly in manner.”

“Indeed,” Elinor said, “I believe you’ll find him all that and more. And since you’ll be such close neighbours—the parsonage is nearly beside the mansion house—it’s especially important that he _should_ be all these things.”

Edward said nothing. But when she turned her head, he gave her a look—serious, intense, shadowed with unease—as though he wished the distance between their homes were far greater.

“Colonel Brandon lodges in St. James Street,” he said shortly, rising.

Elinor told him the address.

“I must go at once,” he said, “to thank him—thank him for something you won’t let me thank _you_ for. To tell him he has made me—very, very happy.”

Elinor made no attempt to stop him. They parted with her offering earnest wishes for his future happiness, and him attempting—though failing—to return the sentiment with equal warmth.

As the door closed behind him, Elinor whispered to herself, “When I see him again, I shall see him married to Lucy.”

And with that comforting thought, she sat down to reflect—on the past, on Edward’s words, on his feelings, and on her own, which she found deeply troubling.

When Mrs. Jennings returned, her mind was so consumed by the secret she carried that she couldn’t wait to share it. As soon as Elinor appeared, she burst out:

“Well, my dear! I sent you up the young man—didn’t I do right? And I suppose you had no trouble convincing him—he didn’t resist your proposal, did he?”

“No, ma'am,” Elinor said quietly. “That was unlikely.”

“And how soon will he be ready?” Mrs. Jennings pressed. “Because everything depends on that.”

“Honestly,” Elinor replied, “I know little about such matters. I can’t guess the time or preparation needed. But I suppose two or three months will complete his ordination.”

“Two or three months!” Mrs. Jennings gasped. “Lord, my dear, how calmly you speak! Can the Colonel wait that long? Lord bless me—I’d be driven mad! And though I’m glad to help poor Mr. Ferrars, I don’t see why we should wait months for him. Surely someone else could be found—someone already ordained!”

“My dear ma'am,” Elinor said, “what are you thinking? Colonel Brandon’s only goal is to help Mr. Ferrars.”

“Lord bless you!” Mrs. Jennings cried. “Do you really think the Colonel marries you just to give ten guineas to Mr. Ferrars?”

The pretense ended. A mutual understanding sparked, bringing laughter and relief. Neither lost any joy—Mrs. Jennings simply exchanged one delight for another, still holding fast to her original hope.

“Aye, aye,” she said, once the shock passed, “the parsonage is small, and likely in need of repair. But to hear a man apologize for a house that has five sitting rooms downstairs—and I’m told the housekeeper says it could hold fifteen beds! And to _you_, who’ve lived in Barton cottage! It’s ridiculous. But, my dear, we must urge the Colonel to fix it up—make it comfortable before Lucy moves in.”

“But Colonel Brandon doesn’t seem to think the living is enough to marry on,” Elinor said.

“The Colonel’s a fool,” Mrs. Jennings declared. “Just because he has two thousand a year, he thinks no one else can live on less. Take my word for it—if I’m alive, I’ll be visiting Delaford Parsonage before Michaelmas. And I shan’t go unless Lucy is there.”

Elinor agreed wholeheartedly. She saw no reason they would wait for anything more.

Edward, radiant with joy after delivering his heartfelt thanks to Colonel Brandon, hastened to Lucy with news of their impending union. His elation was so profound that when Mrs. Jennings called upon her the following day, brimming with congratulations, Lucy could truthfully declare she had never seen him so exuberant in all her life. Her own spirits soared just as high—her happiness was complete—and she embraced Mrs. Jennings’s hopeful prediction that they would soon be settled together at Delaford Parsonage before Michaelmas. Far from hesitating to acknowledge Elinor’s role in their good fortune, Lucy spoke of her friendship with both Edward and herself with deep gratitude. She freely admitted their debt to Miss Dashwood, and declared with conviction that no effort on Elinor’s part—past or future—would ever astonish her, for she believed Elinor capable of sacrificing everything for those she truly cherished. As for Colonel Brandon, Lucy not only held him in reverent admiration but also earnestly desired he be honored in every worldly way: his tithes should reach their full measure, and she quietly resolved to make ample use of his estate—his servants, his carriage, his cows, and even his poultry—once they were established at Delaford.

It had been over a week since John Dashwood had visited them in Berkeley Street, and since then, no further mention had been made of his wife’s illness beyond a single casual inquiry. This silence prompted Elinor to feel it was time she paid Mrs. Dashwood a visit—a duty that neither her inclinations nor her companions’ encouragement supported. Marianne refused outright to accompany her and went so far as to urge her sister not to go at all. Mrs. Jennings, though always ready to lend her carriage, detested Mrs. John Dashwood so deeply that even her curiosity about how the woman looked after the recent revelation, or her desire to affront her by showing support for Edward, could not overcome her aversion to being in her presence again. Thus, Elinor set out alone, burdened by reluctance, to face a woman whose company neither of her friends found tolerable.

Mrs. Dashwood was absent, but before the carriage could turn away, Mr. Dashwood unexpectedly emerged from the house. He greeted Elinor warmly, saying he had just been about to call on them himself. Assuring her that Fanny would be delighted to see her, he invited her inside. They ascended together to the drawing-room, which was empty.

“Fanny is likely in her own room,” he said. “I’ll fetch her shortly—she’ll have no objection to seeing you. Indeed, none at all. Especially now—though I won’t say more—but you and Marianne were always favorites. Why won’t Marianne come?”

Elinor offered a polite excuse.

“I’m glad to see you alone,” he replied, “for I have something important to discuss. That living of Colonel Brandon’s—can it be true? Has he really given it to Edward? I heard yesterday and came straight here to confirm it.”

“It is perfectly true,” Elinor replied. “Colonel Brandon has bestowed the living of Delaford upon Edward.”

“Really?” he exclaimed. “Well, this is astonishing! No family tie, no connection between them, and yet at a time when livings are so valuable—what was the income?”

“About two hundred pounds a year.”

“Very well. And if the previous incumbent had been elderly and frail, someone might have secured the presentation for perhaps fourteen hundred pounds. So why didn’t he settle that earlier? Now it’s too late to sell, but a man of Colonel Brandon’s judgment—how could he be so careless in such a matter? It shows there’s inconsistency in nearly every human heart. Still, I suppose the truth is—Edward is only holding it until the real buyer comes of age. Yes, yes—that must be it.”

Elinor firmly corrected him, explaining that she had personally delivered the offer from Colonel Brandon to Edward and thus knew the terms precisely. Reluctantly, he yielded to her account.

“This is truly astonishing,” he said, once he’d heard her explanation. “What could have motivated Colonel Brandon?”

“A simple one: to help Mr. Ferrars.”

“Well, well—whatever Colonel Brandon may be, Edward is certainly fortunate. But please, do not mention this to Fanny. Though I’ve told her already, and she bears it remarkably well, she’d prefer it not discussed.”

Elinor struggled to suppress a remark about how Fanny ought to welcome any wealth that enriched her brother without diminishing her own or her child’s comfort.

“Mrs. Ferrars,” he added, lowering his voice as if revealing a secret of great consequence, “knows nothing of it yet. And I believe it’s best to keep it hidden from her as long as possible. When the marriage happens, she’ll inevitably learn the truth.”

“But why such caution?” Elinor asked. “Even if Mrs. Ferrars takes no pleasure in knowing Edward can live comfortably—something entirely improbable—why would she care now? After her actions, she has cast him off forever. She has made everyone under her influence reject him too. Surely, once severed, she cannot possibly feel sorrow or joy for him. She cannot be affected by anything that happens to him. Would she truly abandon the comfort of a child while still carrying the burdens of motherhood?”

“Ah, Elinor,” John said gently, “your logic is sound, but it stems from ignorance of human nature. When Edward marries, rest assured, his mother will feel as deeply as if she had never disowned him. Therefore, every detail that might hasten that dreadful event must remain concealed from her. Mrs. Ferrars can never forget that Edward is her son.”

“You surprise me,” Elinor said. “I thought it must have slipped her memory by now.”

“You misjudge her greatly. Mrs. Ferrars is among the most devoted mothers in the world.”

Elinor remained silent.

“We think now,” Mr. Dashwood continued after a pause, “of Robert marrying Miss Morton.”

Elinor smiled faintly at the solemnity of his tone and replied calmly, “I suppose the lady has no choice in the matter.”

“Choice? What do you mean?”

“I only meant,” she said, “that from your manner, it seems Miss Morton would be equally content whether she married Edward or Robert.”

“Certainly, there’s no difference. Robert will now be considered the eldest son in every practical sense. And besides, both young men are agreeable enough—I wouldn’t say one is clearly superior to the other.”

Elinor said nothing more. John fell quiet too, then resumed:

“One thing, my dear sister,” he said, taking her hand and speaking in hushed tones, “I can assure you of—indeed, I shall tell you, because I know it will please you. I have strong reason to believe—no, I have it from the most reliable source—though I never heard Mrs. Ferrars say it herself, her daughter did, and I heard it from her—that despite any objections to a certain connection—you understand what I mean—it would have been far preferable to her than this. It would not have caused her half the distress this does. I was delighted to hear Mrs. Ferrars view it so. A very comforting thought for us all. ‘It would have been infinitely less painful,’ she said, ‘and I would gladly accept nothing worse.’ But of course, that’s all moot now—impossible to consider. Yet I wanted to share it with you, knowing how much it would delight you. Not that you have cause to regret, my dear Elinor. There’s no doubt you’ll do extremely well—perhaps even better, all things considered. Has Colonel Brandon been visiting lately?”

Elinor had heard enough—not to swell her pride, but to unsettle her mind. She was relieved when Robert Ferrars entered the room, sparing her from further reply or exposure to her brother’s revelations.

After a brief exchange, John remembered that Fanny had not yet been informed of Elinor’s presence and left to find her. Elinor was left alone with Robert, whose cheerful indifference and self-satisfied air—born of a life of indulgence and the unjust favoritism of his mother—only confirmed her low opinion of his character and sincerity.

They had barely been alone two minutes when Robert began asking about Edward. He, too, had heard of the living and was eager to know more. Elinor repeated what she had told John, but Robert reacted quite differently. He burst into uncontrollable laughter.

The idea of Edward becoming a clergyman, residing in a modest parsonage, struck him as absurd. At the thought of Edward reading prayers in a white surplice or announcing marriages between ordinary folk like John Smith and Mary Brown, he could imagine nothing funnier.

Elinor sat still, her expression unchanging, watching him with eyes that conveyed nothing but disdain. It was a look well placed—silent, but powerful—relieving her own emotions without betraying them to him.

Robert eventually sobered, not through her rebuke, but by his own conscience.

“We can treat it as a joke,” he said, regaining composure after his forced laughter subsided. “But seriously, it’s a tragedy. Poor Edward—he’s ruined for life. I’m genuinely sorry for him. I know him to be a kind-hearted fellow—perhaps the most well-meaning man alive. Don’t judge him by our limited acquaintance, Miss Dashwood. Poor Edward! His manners aren’t refined, I admit. But we’re not all born with charm or grace. To see him among strangers—pitiful, truly. But I swear, he has the finest heart in England. I was utterly shocked when I learned the truth. My mother told me first. I felt compelled to act. I immediately said to her, ‘My dear madam, I don’t know what you intend, but as for me—if Edward marries that girl, I’ll never speak to him again.’ That was exactly what I said. I was horrified. Poor Edward—he’s destroyed himself, cut himself off from decent society forever. But I’m not surprised. Given his upbringing, it was inevitable. My poor mother was beside herself.”

“Have you met the lady?”

“Yes—once, when she stayed here. I dropped in briefly and saw enough. Just an awkward country girl, lacking elegance or beauty. Exactly the sort who might capture poor Edward’s heart. I offered to speak to him myself, to dissuade him, but it was too late. I hadn’t known until after the rift. I couldn’t interfere then. But if I’d been informed earlier, I believe I could have prevented it. I would have told him plainly: ‘My dear fellow, consider what you’re doing. You’re making a disgraceful match—your whole family disapproves.’ I truly believe something could have been done. But now it’s too late. He’ll starve, you know. Absolutely starve.”

He had just concluded this grim assessment when Mrs. John Dashwood entered the room, ending the conversation.

Though she never mentioned the matter outside her own household, Elinor could see its effect in the slight flush on her face and the strained warmth of her greeting. She even expressed regret that Elinor and her sister would leave town so soon, as she had hoped to see more of them—an effort her husband, who followed her in and lingered adoringly at her side, seemed to interpret as especially tender and graceful.

CHAPTER XLII. One final visit to Harley Street, where Elinor received her brother’s warm wishes for their journey to Barton without expense, and news that Colonel Brandon would soon follow them to Cleveland, marked the end of the sisters’ time in town with their brother. A polite but distant invitation from Fanny to call at Norland—though it was unlikely they ever would—alongside John’s more heartfelt promise to visit Elinor at Delaford, hinted at future connections. It amused Elinor to notice how everyone seemed eager to send her to Delaford—a place she now found less appealing than ever. Not only did her brother and Mrs. Jennings see it as her future home, but even Lucy had pressed her to come when they parted.

Early in April, shortly after sunrise, the two parties set out from Hanover Square and Berkeley Street, meeting by arrangement along the road. To accommodate Charlotte and her child, their journey would take over two days, while Mr. Palmer and Colonel Brandon traveled faster and were expected to join them at Cleveland shortly after their arrival.

Marianne, though long eager to leave London, felt a deep sorrow as she said farewell to the house where she had once known such hope and trust in Willoughby—feelings now gone forever. She could not bear the thought of him still there, moving forward with new plans and engagements, unaware or indifferent to her pain. Tears came easily as she left behind the place that held so much of her past.

Elinor, on the other hand, welcomed the departure with quiet relief. She had no lingering ties to leave behind, no one whose absence would trouble her heart. She was glad to escape the constant presence of Lucy, grateful that Marianne had been able to leave without encountering Willoughby again since his marriage, and hopeful that a few months of peace at Barton might help restore her sister’s spirits and bring her own mind greater calm.

Their journey passed smoothly. On the second day, they entered Somerset—once a cherished place in Marianne’s thoughts, now tinged with melancholy. By midday on the third, they arrived at Cleveland.

Cleveland was a large, modern residence nestled on a gentle slope. Though it lacked a park, its gardens were well-kept and spacious, featuring open shrubberies, shaded walks, and winding gravel paths leading to the front. The lawn was dotted with trees, and the house stood sheltered beneath firs, mountain ashes, and acacias, with tall Lombardy poplars forming a natural screen around the service buildings.

Marianne stepped into the house with a heart full of emotion—knowing she was just eighty miles from Barton and not far from Combe Magna. Within minutes, she slipped away, wandering through the blossoming shrubberies to reach a distant hilltop. From the Grecian temple atop it, she gazed across the countryside, letting her eyes rest on the farthest ridges, imagining she could see Combe Magna beyond the horizon. In these moments of quiet sorrow, she found comfort in tears, yet also joy in being at Cleveland. Returning by a different path, she savored the freedom of country life—the ability to wander alone, undisturbed, through beautiful landscapes—and resolved to spend every day exploring the grounds in solitude.

She returned just in time to join the others as they began a tour of the estate. The morning passed pleasantly: strolling through the kitchen garden, admiring the flowering walls, listening to the gardener’s complaints about frost damage; browsing the greenhouse, where Charlotte laughed at the loss of her favorite plants; and visiting the poultry yard, where the antics of hens abandoning nests or falling prey to foxes provided light amusement.

The weather remained fair until after dinner, when steady rain began to fall. Marianne, who had planned evening walks, was disappointed—especially as she had hoped for another twilight stroll to the temple. But even she recognized that heavy rain was no fit weather for walking.

With only a small group, the hours passed quietly. Mrs. Palmer occupied herself with her child, Mrs. Jennings with her needlework, and conversation turned to friends left behind, Lady Middleton’s schedule, and speculation about whether Mr. Palmer and Colonel Brandon would reach Reading that night. Elinor joined in politely, while Marianne quickly found her way to the library—her usual refuge—and selected a book.

Mrs. Palmer, though lacking in refinement, made everyone feel welcome with her cheerful, open manner. Her warmth more than compensated for her occasional lapses in grace. Her kindness, softened by her gentle appearance, was winning; her quirks, though noticeable, were never pretentious. Elinor could forgive nearly all of her faults—except her laugh, which often seemed too loud and sudden.

The next day, the two gentlemen arrived late for dinner, bringing fresh energy and lively conversation to the gathering. After a long morning of rain, their arrival lifted the mood greatly.

Elinor had seen little of Mr. Palmer before and noticed differences in how he treated her and her sister. Now, in his own home, she found him courteous and proper toward guests, though occasionally brusque with his wife and mother-in-law. He was agreeable enough when not absorbed in his sense of superiority—particularly over Mrs. Jennings and Charlotte. His habits were typical of his age and class: particular about food, irregular in routine, fond of his child despite pretending otherwise, and spending mornings at billiards instead of business. Yet, despite these flaws, Elinor found herself liking him more than expected—though she was relieved not to like him too much. His self-indulgence and pride reminded her of Edward’s opposite qualities: generosity, simplicity, and modesty. She found herself drawing comfort from the memory of his quiet strength.

Colonel Brandon brought news of Edward Ferrars, having recently visited Dorsetshire. Speaking frankly and kindly, he shared details about the parsonage at Delaford—its shortcomings and his plans to improve it. He treated Elinor as both a trusted friend and a valued confidante. His evident pleasure at seeing her again after only ten days, his eagerness to talk, and his respect for her opinions, made Mrs. Jennings’s belief in his affection seem reasonable. Yet Elinor, who had always believed Marianne to be his true favorite, saw no sign of such feelings. She observed him closely—not just his words, but his expressions. When Marianne showed early signs of illness—headache, sore throat, and a cold beginning to take hold—his silent concern, visible in his eyes, revealed a depth of care that escaped Mrs. Jennings entirely. Elinor understood what those looks meant: the tender worry of someone deeply attached.

Two lovely evening walks, on the third and fourth nights, led Marianne deeper into the grounds—beyond the formal paths, into wilder areas where trees were older and grass longer. Despite the dampness, she lingered too long, sitting in wet shoes and stockings. These indulgences, combined with her lack of caution, led to a severe cold. At first she dismissed it, but soon the symptoms worsened—fever, fatigue, cough, and sore throat—forcing her to acknowledge the illness. Remedies were offered from every side, but she refused most, insisting that rest alone would heal her. Only with great effort did Elinor persuade her to try a few simple remedies before retiring for the night.

The morning dawned as usual, and Marianne rose with the same quiet routine she had followed for so many days. To every question, she replied with a calm voice that she was feeling better—though her words rang hollow against the truth of her condition. She tried to prove her recovery by resuming her customary tasks: reading, walking slowly about the room, or sitting by the fire. But her efforts were feeble. Her hands trembled, her eyes flickered with fatigue, and the book she held remained unread. Most of the day passed in silence, spent either curled on the sofa, too weak to move, or huddled near the hearth, shivering despite the warmth. When evening came, she retired early, more exhausted than before, her strength ebbing with each passing hour.

Colonel Brandon watched her with growing concern. Elinor, though tending her sister tirelessly—forcing medicines upon her at night, watching over her through long hours of wakefulness—remained outwardly composed. Like Marianne, she placed her trust in rest, believing sleep would restore what illness had taken. Yet neither anticipated the storm that followed.

That night was restless and feverish. Marianne tossed beneath the sheets, her breathing uneven, her skin hot to the touch. The next morning, she attempted to rise again, but could not sustain herself. After a brief struggle, she returned willingly to bed, drained and defeated. It was then that Elinor, no longer able to delay, agreed with Mrs. Jennings’s urgent suggestion: they must send for Mr. Harris, the apothecary from the Palmers’ household.

He arrived promptly, examined Marianne with care, and offered reassurance—she would recover within days, he said. But his words carried a shadow: he spoke of a “putrid tendency,” and let slip the word “infection.” Instantly, Mrs. Palmer, already anxious for her infant, grew alarmed. Mrs. Jennings, who had long suspected worse than Elinor dared admit, now looked grave and solemn. She echoed Charlotte’s fears, insisting that she must leave immediately with her child. Mr. Palmer, though dismissive at first, found himself overwhelmed by his wife’s distress and yielded. Their departure was decided—within an hour, they were gone, bound for a relative’s home just beyond Bath, where Mr. Palmer promised to join them soon, and where Mrs. Palmer begged her mother to come as well.

Mrs. Jennings, however, refused to abandon her post. With a heart full of kindness, she declared she would not leave Cleveland while Marianne remained unwell. She would be the substitute mother, she vowed, and stood ready to assist Elinor in every way. And indeed, she proved invaluable—attentive, tireless, and often wiser in matters of care than even Elinor herself.

Marianne, weakened by her sickness, felt the weight of her illness more keenly than ever. She could no longer pretend tomorrow would bring healing. And when she thought of what tomorrow might have brought—had she been well—her sorrow deepened. That day, they were meant to begin their journey home, surprising their mother in Barton, escorted all the way by Mrs. Jennings’s servant. Now, that plan lay shattered. All she spoke of was the delay, lamenting it with quiet despair. Elinor did her best to comfort her, urging her to believe, as she herself still did, that the illness would pass quickly.

But the next day brought no improvement. Marianne remained unchanged—no better, yet not clearly worse. The household continued to shrink. Mr. Palmer, reluctant to leave both out of compassion and pride, was finally persuaded by Colonel Brandon to keep his promise and follow his wife. As he prepared to depart, Brandon himself began to speak of leaving too—though only after great inner struggle.

Yet Mrs. Jennings stepped in with gentle authority. To send the Colonel away while Marianne lay ill, she insisted, would be cruel. He was needed—by her, by Miss Dashwood, by all. She joked lightly about needing him for piquet in the evenings, while Marianne rested above. But her tone was firm, her appeal sincere. And so, though Brandon had intended to go, he stayed—his heart relieved, his duty fulfilled.

Marianne knew nothing of these arrangements. She saw no sign of Mrs. Palmer, nor did she ask. The absence caused her no surprise, nor any sorrow. Two days passed, and her condition remained stubbornly the same. Mr. Harris visited daily, speaking confidently of a swift recovery. Elinor shared his optimism. But others were less hopeful. Mrs. Jennings had long believed Marianne would not survive. Brandon, though he tried to reason against such thoughts, found himself listening to her fears, unable to shake them. Alone in the house, the hours stretched into melancholy reflection. He could not escape the dread that he would never see Marianne again.

Then, on the third morning, a glimmer of hope appeared. Mr. Harris arrived and declared a marked improvement—her pulse stronger, her symptoms lighter. Elinor rejoiced, her spirits lifted. She smiled as she wrote to their mother, downplaying the delay, confident they would soon be on their way home.

But the day ended differently. By evening, Marianne worsened. She grew heavier, more restless, her discomfort deeper than before. Still, Elin­or clung to hope, attributing the change to exhaustion from sitting up earlier. She gave her cordials, watched her closely, and saw her fall into a fitful sleep—peaceful enough, she thought, to offer relief.

She resolved to stay awake, to watch her sister through the night. Mrs. Jennings, unaware of the shift, retired early. The maid, one of the main nurses, was elsewhere. Elinor was alone with Marianne.

At first, the sleep seemed steady. But gradually, it became agitated. Marianne shifted constantly, murmured incoherent sounds. Then, startled by a sudden noise, she sat bolt upright, eyes wide with feverish panic.

“Is Mama coming?” she gasped.

Elinor, though terrified, kept her voice steady. “Not yet,” she answered, helping her back to bed. “But she will be here soon. It’s a long way from Barton.”

“But she must not go round by London,” Marianne cried, breathless. “I shall never see her if she goes by London.”

Elinor realized then—she was not herself. She checked her pulse. It was lower, faster than before. Fear seized her. She sent for Mr. Harris at once—and dispatched a messenger to Barton for their mother.

Her mind turned to Colonel Brandon. She needed advice, someone to help. She hurried to the drawing-room, knowing he would be there.

He listened without flinching. He could not ease her fear—but he could act. Without hesitation, he offered to ride to Barton himself, to fetch Mrs. Dashwood.

Elinor hesitated only a moment—then accepted. She thanked him, her voice trembling with gratitude. While he sent his servant for horses and a message to the apothecary, she penned a few lines to her mother.

And how precious it was to know that Colonel Brandon would be the one to bring her—strong, thoughtful, kind. A companion whose presence could soften the blow, whose wisdom would guide her, whose friendship might soothe her grief.

Brandon moved swiftly—arranging everything with calm precision. He calculated the time of his return, left no detail undone. The horses arrived sooner than expected. With a solemn glance, he pressed Elinor’s hand, whispered something too low to hear, and climbed into the carriage.

It was nearly midnight when he departed. Elinor returned to Marianne’s room, waiting for the apothecary, guarding her sister through the dark hours.

It was a night of agony. Marianne suffered in delirium, shifting between fevered dreams and painful silence. Elinor watched, helpless, her own heart breaking with each passing minute. She would not call Mrs. Jennings—she feared her fears would only deepen the burden.

And yet, Marianne’s mind drifted often to her mother. Each mention of her name pierced Elinor’s soul. She reproached herself—why had she waited? Why had she not acted sooner? She imagined her mother arriving too late, too late to see her daughter conscious, too late to hold her one last time.

Just as she considered sending for another doctor, Mr. Harris arrived—late, but at last. His assessment brought some comfort. Though the illness had worsened unexpectedly, he denied it was life-threatening. He spoke of new treatments, new remedies, and offered confidence. He promised to return in a few hours.

When morning came, Mrs. Jennings learned of the night’s events. She grieved deeply—her fears now confirmed. She could not pretend hope. The thought of losing such a young, beautiful girl filled her with sorrow. And she mourned not only for Marianne, but for Elinor, and for the mother who might soon face the worst.

Mr. Harris returned punctually—but his hopes were dashed. The medicine had failed. The fever raged on. Marianne was quieter now, but far from herself—lost in a heavy stupor.

Elinor, seeing the depth of his worry, urged him to seek another opinion. But he refused—he had one more treatment to try, and he believed in it. His words sounded encouraging, but they did not reach her heart.

She sat in silence, her thoughts drifting—between her sister’s suffering, her mother’s journey, and the terrible possibility of loss. Only when she noticed a faint change in Marianne’s pulse did she dare to hope.

She watched, waited, tested again and again. At last, she could not suppress her joy. She whispered her discovery to Mrs. Jennings, who reluctantly admitted a temporary improvement—but warned against expectation.

But hope had already taken root. And though Elinor told herself not to believe, she could not help it. She leaned over her sister, watching—waiting—for signs of life returning.

Half an hour passed. Then more. Her breath was easier. Her skin cooled. Her lips lost their blue tinge. And once, she looked at Elinor—not with fever, but with clear, though weary, eyes.

Anxiety and joy warred within her—until Mr. Harris arrived at four o’clock. His words were pure triumph: Marianne was recovering—better than he had hoped. Out of danger. Fully restored.

Mrs. Jennings allowed herself to smile. She welcomed the news with open arms. But Elinor remained still. Her joy was deep, silent, overwhelming. She did not laugh, did not cry aloud. Her heart swelled with gratitude—for life, for love, for her sister’s return.

She stayed by Marianne’s side, watching every breath, soothing every whisper, ensuring no fear lingered. The possibility of relapse crossed her mind—but when she saw her sister sink into a calm, steady sleep at six, she silenced every doubt.

Now, the hours counted down. Colonel Brandon should return soon. By ten, her mother would arrive—relieved, perhaps, from the torment of the journey.

At seven, she left Marianne sleeping peacefully and joined Mrs. Jennings for tea. She had eaten little—too much fear, then too much hope. This meal was a welcome comfort.

Mrs. Jennings urged her to rest, to let her take over. But Elinor felt no weariness. She could not leave her sister’s side.

So Mrs. Jennings went upstairs briefly to check, then returned to her room to write letters and sleep.

Outside, the night was fierce. Wind howled, rain lashed the windows. But Elinor paid it no mind. Marianne slept soundly. And the travelers—soon, they would find their reward.

At eight, the clock struck. And though Elinor knew it was too early, she heard it—the rumble of wheels, the clatter of hooves. She rushed to the window, opened the shutter.

There, lit by flickering lamps, was a carriage drawn by four horses. Her mother—fear-driven, desperate—had arrived.

She could not remain calm. She thought of her mother’s terror, her dread, her anguish. And of what she must say.

She hurried downstairs, her heart pounding. The bustle in the vestibule confirmed it—they were inside.

She ran to the drawing-room.

And there, standing alone in the dim light, was Willoughby.

Elinor stepped back, her face pale with shock at the sight of him. Her first instinct was to flee—to turn and leave the room without a word. Her hand had already reached for the door latch when he rushed forward, his voice sharp with urgency rather than pleading.

“Miss Dashwood,” he said, “just half an hour—ten minutes—I beg you, stay.”

“No, sir,” she replied firmly, refusing to be swayed. “I shall not stay. Your business cannot possibly concern me. Perhaps the servants forgot to tell you that Mr. Palmer is not at home.”

“Even if they’d told me,” he burst out, “that Mr. Palmer and every soul in his family were damned to hell, I wouldn’t have turned away. My business is with you—and only you.”

“With *me*?” she echoed, stunned. “Well, sir—be quick—and if you can—less passionate.”

“Sit down,” he insisted, “and I’ll be both.”

She hesitated. The thought of Colonel Brandon arriving unannounced crossed her mind. But she had promised to listen, and now curiosity wove itself with duty. After a brief moment of resolve, she decided silence and speed were best. She walked quietly to the table and sat down. He took the chair opposite, and for half a minute neither spoke.

“Please be quick, sir,” Elinor urged, impatient. “I have no time to spare.”

He sat motionless, deep in thought, as if he hadn’t heard her.

“Your sister,” he said abruptly, “is out of danger. I heard from the servant. God be praised!—But is it true? Is it really true?”

Elinor remained silent.

He pressed on, more urgently: “For heaven’s sake, tell me—is she out of danger or not?”

“We hope so,” she murmured.

He rose and paced the room.

“If I’d known this half an hour ago—but since I’m here,” he said, returning to his seat with forced cheerfulness, “what does it matter? For once, Miss Dashwood—it may well be the last time—let us be cheerful together. I’m in a fine mood for laughter. Tell me honestly,” he added, his cheeks flushing slightly, “do you think me more of a rogue or a fool?”

Elinor stared at him, bewildered. She began to suspect he was drunk—the oddness of his visit, his manner, seemed explicable only by liquor. With that idea, she stood up at once.

“Mr. Willoughby,” she said, “I advise you to return to Combe immediately. I cannot stay any longer. Whatever your purpose, it will be better served tomorrow, when you’ve had time to think.”

“I understand you,” he replied, smiling with quiet composure. “Yes, I am very drunk. A pint of porter with my cold beef at Marlborough was enough to unhinge me.”

“At Marlborough!” she exclaimed, utterly confused.

“Yes,” he said. “I left London this morning at eight o’clock. The only ten minutes I’ve spent outside my chaise since then were for a meal at Marlborough.”

His steady gaze and clear expression convinced her that, whatever folly had brought him to Cleveland, it wasn’t drunkenness. After a pause, she said, “Mr. Willoughby, you ought to know—*I* do—that after everything that has passed, your coming here like this, forcing yourself upon my notice, demands a most serious explanation. What is your purpose?”

“I mean,” he said, with quiet intensity, “if I can, to make you hate me just a little less than you do now. I mean to offer some kind of explanation, some kind of apology—for what I’ve done. To open my heart completely, and show you that though I’ve always been a fool, I’ve never been entirely a villain. And perhaps, through that, to earn something close to forgiveness—from your sister.”

“Is this truly why you’ve come?”

“Upon my soul, it is,” he answered, his tone so sincere it stirred memories of the man she once knew. Despite herself, she found his honesty almost believable.

“If that is all,” she said, “then you may be satisfied already. Marianne *has* forgiven you—she has long forgiven you.”

“Has she?” he asked, his voice filled with sudden fervor. “Then she forgave me too soon. But she shall forgive me again—on better grounds. Now, will you hear me?”

Elinor nodded.

“I don’t know,” he began, after a pause heavy with expectation, “how you’ve interpreted my behavior toward your sister—or what dark motive you’ve imagined me capable of. You may not think more kindly of me after this—but it’s worth trying. You shall hear everything. When I first became acquainted with your family, I had no other intention than to pass my time pleasantly while I stayed in Devonshire—more pleasantly than ever before. Your sister’s beauty, her charm, could not fail to please me. And her kindness toward me, from the very beginning—how strange it seems now, when I reflect on how much she gave, and how little I returned! At first, I confess, it was only vanity that responded. I cared nothing for her happiness, thinking only of my own amusement. I indulged feelings I’d long grown used to indulging, doing everything I could to win her affection—without ever meaning to return it.”

At this, Elinor looked at him with scornful contempt and interrupted:

“It’s hardly worth continuing, Mr. Willoughby. Such a beginning cannot lead to anything worthwhile. Please—don’t make me suffer further by hearing more.”

“I insist,” he said, “you hear the whole truth.” He paused, then continued. “My fortune was never large. I’d always lived beyond my means, surrounded by people wealthier than myself. Every year since I came of age—even before—had added to my debts. Though Mrs. Smith’s death was meant to free me, it was uncertain and possibly years off. So for some time, I’d intended to restore my fortunes through marriage to a woman of means. Therefore, attaching myself to your sister was never a possibility. And yet—with selfishness, cruelty, and meanness beyond reproach—I pursued her affections, never intending to give them back. But one thing must be said: even in that state of selfish vanity, I didn’t know the full harm I was causing—because I didn’t *then* know what it meant to love. But have I ever truly loved? It may well be doubted. For if I had, could I have sacrificed my own feelings to pride and greed? Could I have sacrificed hers? Yet I did. To avoid a poverty that her love would have made bearable, I chose wealth—and lost everything that could have made life meaningful.”

“So,” Elinor said, her voice softening slightly, “you once believed you were attached to her?”

“To resist such beauty, such tenderness? Is there any man alive who could have done it? Yes—I found myself, gradually, sincerely fond of her. The happiest hours of my life were those I spent with her when I believed my intentions were honorable, my feelings pure. Even then, though I’d fully resolved to declare my love, I foolishly delayed—afraid to commit while my circumstances were so tangled. I won’t argue now—nor will I let you dwell on the absurdity of hesitating when honor already bound me. But the result proves I was a cunning fool, carefully preparing a way to make myself despised and miserable forever. Finally, I made up my mind. I intended, as soon as I could speak with her alone, to justify my attentions and openly declare my love. But—just before that chance came—a disaster struck. A terrible discovery was made.”

He hesitated, looking down.

“Mrs. Smith somehow learned—perhaps through a distant relation who wished to remove me from her favor—of a past connection, a relationship—but I needn’t explain further,” he added, glancing at her with flushed cheeks and searching eyes. “You’ve likely heard the whole story already.”

“I have,” Elinor replied, blushing herself, her heart hardening anew against pity. “I’ve heard it all. And how you expect to excuse any part of your guilt in that dreadful affair… I confess, I cannot comprehend.”

“Remember,” he said quickly, “from whom you heard it. Could it have been fair? I admit her situation and character deserved respect. I don’t seek to justify myself—but I can’t let you believe I have no defense. Just because she was wronged doesn’t mean she was blameless. And just because I was reckless doesn’t mean she was perfect. Her passion, her lack of judgment—though I don’t defend myself—her love for me deserved better treatment. I often recall, with deep shame, the tenderness she once inspired in me. I wish—I truly wish—she had never felt it. But I harmed more than her. I harmed someone whose affection for me—may I say it?—was nearly as strong as hers. Whose mind—oh, how infinitely superior!”

“But your indifference toward that poor girl,” Elinor said sharply, “your callous neglect—no weakness of understanding excuses that. You must have known she was reduced to destitution while you enjoyed yourself in Devonshire, chasing new pleasures, always happy, always gay.”

“But I didn’t know it,” he protested warmly. “I didn’t remember failing to give her my address. Common sense should have told her how to find me.”

“And what did Mrs. Smith say?”

“She accused me outright. My confusion was unbearable. Her purity, her strict morals, her ignorance of the world—everything worked against me. I couldn’t deny the truth, and every attempt to soften it failed. She’d already suspected my general immorality and was dissatisfied with the little attention I’d given her during this visit. In short, it ended in a complete rupture. One thing might have saved me—when she offered, in her moral zeal, to forgive the past if I married Eliza. That I could not do—and I was formally cast out of her favor and her home.”

“The night after,” he continued, “the night I was to leave the next day, I spent in agony, deciding my future. The struggle was great—but it ended too soon. My love for Marianne, my certainty of her love for me—it was all outweighed by my fear of poverty, by false notions of wealth that I’d inherited and society had reinforced. I believed I could secure my current wife if I chose to propose. I convinced myself that nothing else remained but prudence. But before I left Devonshire, a painful scene awaited me—I was supposed to dine with you that day. An apology was needed. But whether to write it or deliver it in person was a long debate. To see Marianne would be agonizing—I even doubted I could see her again and keep my resolution. Yet I underestimated my own courage. I went. I saw her. I saw her suffering—and I left her suffering, leaving her hoping never to see me again.”

“Why did you come, Mr. Willoughby?” Elinor asked, her voice full of reproach. “A note would have sufficed. Why was it necessary to appear?”

“It was necessary to my pride,” he said. “I couldn’t bear to leave the country in a way that might make you or others suspect what had happened between Mrs. Smith and me. So I decided to call at the cottage on my way to Honiton. But seeing your sister—seeing her—was truly dreadful. And worse, I found her alone. You were all gone—I don’t know where. I’d left her only the evening before, so certain, so firm in my resolve to do right! A few hours were to bind us forever. I remember how joyful I felt walking from the cottage to Allenham—pleased with myself, delighted with everyone! But in this final meeting, I approached her burdened with guilt that nearly stripped me of the ability to pretend. Her sorrow, her disappointment, her deep regret when I told her I had to leave so suddenly—I shall never forget it—combined with her trust, her faith in me! Oh God—what a heartless rascal I was!”

They sat in silence for a moment. Then Elinor spoke.

“Did you tell her you’d return soon?”

“I don’t know what I said,” he replied, impatient. “Less than I owed her, certainly—and likely more than I could promise. I can’t bear to think of it. Then came your dear mother—her kindness, her confidence—torturing me further. Thank Heaven, it *did* torture me. I was miserable. Miss Dashwood, you cannot imagine how comforting it is to look back on my own misery. I hold such a grudge against myself for the stupid, shameful folly of my heart that all my past suffering now feels like triumph.”

He paused.

“Well, sir,” Elinor said, though she pitied him, growing restless for his departure, “is that all?”

“All?” he said. “No—have you forgotten what happened in town? That infamous letter? Did she show it to you?”

“Yes,” she said. “I read every note.”

“When her first letter reached me—immediately, since I was in London the entire time—my feelings…” He stopped. “In plain words—my feelings were excruciating. Every line, every word—were, in the old metaphor she’d disapprove of, daggers to my heart. To learn Marianne was in town—was a thunderbolt. Thunderbolts and daggers!—What a rebuke she’d give me! Her taste, her opinions—I know them better than my own, and they are dearer.”

Elinor’s heart, which had shifted many times during this strange conversation, softened again. Yet she felt compelled to stop him from slipping into sentimental regrets.

“This is not right, Mr. Willoughby,” she said gently. “You are married. Speak only what you feel is necessary for me to hear.”

“Marianne’s letter,” he said, “assured me she still loved me as deeply as ever—despite weeks apart, still constant, still trusting. That awakened all my remorse. I say awakened, because time and London, business and distraction, had dulled it. I’d begun to grow hardened, convincing myself I was indifferent, pretending she too must have moved on. I dismissed our past attachment as trivial, shrugging it off, silencing every pang with the secret thought: ‘I’ll be glad to hear she’s happily married.’ But her letter showed me the truth. I realized she was infinitely dearer to me than any other woman—and that I was betraying her horribly. But by then, everything was settled with Miss Grey. Retreat was impossible. All I could do was avoid you both. I sent no reply to Marianne, hoping to cut off contact. For a time, I even refused to call in Berkeley Street. But finally, I judged it wiser to appear casually cool. So I waited until you were all safely out of the house, then left my name.”

“You watched us leave?”

“Yes. You’d be surprised how often I watched you, how close I came to meeting you. I entered shops just to avoid seeing your carriage pass. Living in Bond Street, I saw you nearly every day. Only constant vigilance kept us apart. I avoided the Middletons and anyone else who might connect us. But I accidentally met Sir John on his first day in town, the day after I’d called on Mrs. Jennings. He invited me to a party—dancing at his house that evening. Had he not mentioned you and your sister would be there, I wouldn’t have dared go near him. The next morning, another short note arrived—still tender, open, trusting—everything that made my actions abhorrent. I couldn’t answer it. I tried—but couldn’t form a single sentence. But I thought of her—every moment. If you can pity me, Miss Dashwood, pity me then. With my mind and heart full of your sister, I was forced to play the happy lover to another woman! Those weeks were worse than any torment. Eventually, as you know, you were thrust upon me. And what a spectacle I made! What an evening of agony!
