Marianne—beautiful as an angel—calling me Willoughby with such a tone! Oh God—holding out her hand, asking for an explanation, her eyes pleading so desperately! And Sophia, jealous as the devil, watching from the other side—well, it doesn’t matter now. It’s over. That was the last time I saw her—the last image I carry. She was white as death. Horrible—but today, when I thought of her dying, it was a comfort to know exactly how she appeared to those who saw her last. She was always before me—on my journey, in that same look, that same pallor.”

They sat in silence again, each lost in thought. Then Willoughby broke it.

“Well, let me go. Your sister is certainly better, surely out of danger?”

“We are assured of it.”

“And your mother—so devoted to Marianne?”

“But the letter, Mr. Willoughby—your own letter—have you nothing to say about it?”

“Yes—yes, especially that. Your sister wrote again the next morning—you saw what she said. I was breakfasting at the Ellisons when her letter, among others, was delivered from my lodgings. It caught Sophia’s eye before mine. Its size, the paper, the handwriting—immediately aroused her suspicion. She’d heard rumors of my attachment to a young lady in Devonshire, and the events of the previous evening had revealed who she was. Jealousy flared. With playful pretense—so charming when one loves her—she opened the letter and read it. She was punished for her boldness. She read what made her miserable. I could have borne her misery—but her rage, her malice—It had to be appeased. And so—what do you think of my wife’s style of writing? Delicate? Tender? Truly feminine?”

“Your wife?” Elinor said. “The letter was in your hand.”

“Yes—but I merely copied sentences I was ashamed to sign. The original was hers—her thoughts, her grace. But what could I do? We were engaged, everything arranged, the date nearly set. In truth—honest truth—her money was essential. In my position, anything was permissible to prevent a break. And in the end, what did it matter to Marianne or her friends how I phrased it? The goal was clear. I had to declare myself a scoundrel—whether with dignity or defiance mattered little. ‘I am ruined forever in their eyes,’ I thought. ‘Shut out from their company. They already think me unprincipled; this letter will only make them think me a blackguard.’” He paused, then added, “So I copied her words, and parted with the last traces of Marianne. Her three letters—unluckily, they were all in my pocketbook, or I might have denied them, kept them forever—I had to surrender them. I couldn’t even kiss them. And the lock of hair—always carried with me—now searched for by her with cruel sweetness—dear lock—every memento torn from me.”

Elinor sat motionless for a long while after Willoughby had gone, even after the sound of his carriage had faded into silence. Her mind was overwhelmed—by sorrow, by confusion, by thoughts that tangled and clashed within her. The man she had despised only moments before now stirred a strange compassion in her heart. Despite all his faults, despite the pain he had caused, she found herself mourning his loss—not because of what he deserved, but because of what he had once meant to Marianne. She admitted to herself, with quiet honesty, that her feelings toward him were shaped not by reason, but by memory: by his charm, his warmth, the way he had once lit up Marianne’s world. And though she knew it was wrong to still care, she could not yet let go.

At last, she returned to Marianne’s room. Her sister was just waking, her face calm and soft from deep sleep. Elinor’s chest tightened. So much had happened—the visit, the fear, the hope—her emotions swirled too fiercely for rest. Yet she kept her face composed, afraid to show how deeply she felt. But her caution lasted only a short time. Within half an hour, another carriage rolled up to the house.

She rushed downstairs, driven by a need to spare her mother every second of dread. Just as the door opened, she caught her mother stepping in—pale, trembling, nearly fainting. Mrs. Dashwood had been convinced, as they approached the house, that Marianne was dead. She had no words, no breath to speak. But Elinor did not wait. In one swift moment, she told her—Marianne was alive, recovering, safe. The relief burst forth like sunlight through storm clouds. Mrs. Dashwood clung to her daughter, sobbing with joy, her arms wrapping around Elinor again and again. Between them stood Colonel Brandon, silent, steady, his presence a quiet strength. She turned to him, her eyes full of gratitude and shared joy, pressing his hand as if to say, *You helped bring this back to me.*

When Mrs. Dashwood regained her composure, her first wish was to see Marianne. In minutes, she was at her bedside, weeping softly as she looked upon her child—now more precious than ever, after the terror of losing her. Elinor watched, her heart swelling with happiness, though she worried that the emotion might disturb Marianne’s fragile peace. But her mother, strong in love and duty, held herself in check. Marianne, weak but content, needed only quiet and rest—and she gave it willingly, knowing her mother was near.

Mrs. Dashwood insisted on staying awake all night, watching over her daughter. Elinor, reluctantly obeying her plea, finally went to bed. But sleep refused to come. The long hours of anxiety, the endless thoughts of Willoughby—“poor Willoughby,” she now allowed herself to whisper—kept her mind restless. She wished she had heard his story before judging him so harshly. She blamed herself, then forgave herself, over and over. And yet, the thought of telling Marianne what he had said filled her with dread. What would it do to her sister? Could she ever truly be happy again? For a fleeting moment, Elinor almost hoped Willoughby would never marry—only to stop herself, remembering Colonel Brandon, and the quiet dignity of his love. She pulled back from such a thought. No—she wished nothing but health and happiness for Mrs. Willoughby.

The shock of Colonel Brandon’s sudden arrival at Barton had been softened by Mrs. Dashwood’s own terror. Her fears for Marianne had already pushed her to plan a journey to Cleveland, without waiting for news. The Careys were expected any minute to take Margaret away—her mother unwilling to risk infection. But now, all was changed.

Each day, Marianne grew stronger. Her recovery brought radiant joy to Mrs. Dashwood, whose spirits shone with unshakable happiness. She declared herself, again and again, the happiest woman alive. Elinor listened, touched—but sometimes wondered if her mother ever remembered Edward. But Mrs. Dashwood, swept up in her own joy, had forgotten everything else. Marianne had been saved from death—a danger, she now saw, partly of her own making, for encouraging her daughter’s hopeless attachment to Willoughby. And now, there was more: a new kind of joy, one Elinor had not foreseen.

As soon as they were alone, Mrs. Dashwood leaned forward, her eyes sparkling. “My dear Elinor—you don’t know all my happiness yet. Colonel Brandon loves Marianne. He told me himself.”

Elinor listened, her heart fluttering between surprise and tenderness. She said nothing, but her attention was complete.

“You’re never like me, dear,” Mrs. Dashwood continued, “or I’d marvel at your calmness. If I had asked for one thing above all for our family, it would have been for Colonel Brandon to marry one of you. And I believe Marianne will be the happiest of the two.”

Elinor wanted to ask why—why her mother thought so, when their ages, temperaments, lives were so different. But she knew better. Her mother’s imagination ran wild when love was involved. So instead of questioning, she smiled gently.

“He spoke to me yesterday, as we traveled,” Mrs. Dashwood went on. “It came out naturally, without planning. I could talk of nothing but Marianne—my poor child! He couldn’t hide his pain. I saw it matched my own. Perhaps he thought friendship wouldn’t justify such feeling… or perhaps he didn’t think at all. But he confessed—his love for Marianne has been constant, deep, true. Ever since the first moment he saw her.”

Elinor listened carefully. She knew her mother’s words were colored by emotion—by longing, by hope. She heard not just facts, but dreams dressed as truth.

“His love,” Mrs. Dashwood continued, “is far beyond anything Willoughby ever felt. It is warmer, truer, steadier. And it endured through knowing how deeply Marianne loved that boy—without selfishness, without hope. Can you imagine such nobility? Such honesty? No one can mistake Colonel Brandon’s heart.”

“Colonel Brandon’s character,” Elinor said quietly, “is already well known. He is respected by all who know him.”

“I know it,” Mrs. Dashwood replied seriously. “And after what we’ve seen, I’d never encourage such affection unless I believed in him. But his coming for me—so quickly, so kindly—proves him one of the finest men I’ve ever met.”

“And yet,” Elinor said, “his worth isn’t built on one act. He’s known and admired by Mrs. Jennings, the Middletons. Even I, though I’ve known him only recently, hold him in high regard. If Marianne can find happiness with him, I’ll welcome it as warmly as you do. But what did you say to him? Did you give him hope?”

“Oh, my love,” Mrs. Dashwood sighed, “I couldn’t speak of hope then. Marianne might have died. But he didn’t ask for encouragement. It was a confession—not a request. Still, later, when I could speak, I told him—if she lived, my greatest joy would be to see them married. Since we’ve returned, I’ve repeated it. I’ve given him every comfort I can. A little time,” she said firmly, “and all will be well. Marianne’s heart cannot stay fixed on such a man forever. Colonel Brandon’s merits will win it.”

“But from his expression,” Elinor said gently, “he doesn’t seem as certain.”

“No,” Mrs. Dashwood admitted. “He thinks Marianne’s feelings are too deeply rooted to change quickly. And even if her heart were free, he doubts himself—worries about the difference in age, in temperament. But he’s mistaken. His age is an advantage—it means stability, wisdom. And his nature—gentle, sincere, thoughtful—is exactly what Marianne needs. And his manner—though not as dazzling as Willoughby’s—he has a quiet charm. I never liked the look in Willoughby’s eyes sometimes. Do you remember?”

Elinor could not recall it—but her mother didn’t wait. “And the Colonel’s manners—so natural, so kind—are more suited to Marianne than Willoughby’s flashy ways. I’m certain that even if Willoughby had been good, Marianne would never have been truly happy with him. But with Colonel Brandon—she will be.”

She paused. Elinor did not agree fully, but she said nothing. Her mother’s joy was too great to challenge.

“At Delaford,” Mrs. Dashwood added, “she’ll be close to me—even if I stay here. And surely, in such a village, there must be a small cottage nearby—just right for us.”

Poor Elinor. Another plan to move to Delaford. But her spirit remained firm. She said nothing.

“And his fortune!” Mrs. Dashwood went on, smiling. “At my age, everyone cares about that. Though I don’t know the exact sum, I’m sure it’s substantial.”

They were interrupted by a third person entering the room. Elinor slipped away, alone at last. She sat in silence, her thoughts swirling. She wished for Colonel Brandon’s happiness. She wanted Marianne to find peace. But as she wished it, a quiet ache stirred within her—for Willoughby, for what might have been, for the past that could never be undone.

Marianne’s illness, though severe in its effects, had not lasted long enough to delay her recovery. Strengthened by youth, natural resilience, and the comforting presence of her mother, she improved steadily. Within four days of Mrs. Dashwood’s arrival, Marianne was well enough to move into Mrs. Palmer’s dressing-room—a change that brought her a sense of renewed strength and quiet joy. It was there, at her own request, that she invited Colonel Brandon to visit her. She longed to express her heartfelt gratitude for his having brought her mother to her side when she needed her most.

Colonel Brandon entered the room with a visible tremor in his step. His eyes, meeting hers, softened with emotion as he took her pale hand—so fragile now, yet still full of life. Elinor, watching closely, sensed that his feelings went beyond mere kindness or affection for Marianne. There was something deeper, something stirred within him by the sight of her weakened form—her hollow cheeks, her faint complexion, the way she leaned back in her chair, breathless yet serene. The resemblance between Marianne and Eliza, once noted but now intensified by illness, seemed to unlock memories long buried. The pain in his face told of past sorrows, of griefs he had carried silently through years.

Mrs. Dashwood observed the scene with a different eye. To her, the Colonel’s emotion appeared no more than a natural response to seeing a beloved friend so changed. But in Marianne’s words—soft, sincere, tinged with something beyond simple thanks—she believed she saw the first flicker of something more profound. A feeling, perhaps, that had been quietly growing beneath the surface.

As Marianne continued to gain strength—each day bringing new signs of vitality—the idea of returning to Barton began to take shape. Mrs. Dashwood, moved by both her own longing and her daughters’ quiet hopes, began to speak of leaving Cleveland. Their departure would depend on her decision: Mrs. Jennings could not leave until they did, and Colonel Brandon, urged by their shared requests, soon found himself bound to remain as well. At their joint invitation, he offered his carriage for the journey home, so that Marianne might travel comfortably. In return, he accepted an invitation from Mrs. Dashwood and Mrs. Jennings to visit them at Barton in a few weeks—an offer made with genuine warmth and pleasure.

The day of departure arrived. Marianne took her farewells with deep emotion. Her parting from Mrs. Jennings was especially touching—full of sincerity, respect, and regret for past neglects. Then, turning to Colonel Brandon, she bid him goodbye with the warmth of true friendship, her voice steady, her smile gentle. He helped her into the carriage, making sure she was settled, insisting she take half the space, as if afraid she might tire. Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor followed, and the others were left behind—Mrs. Jennings to her chaise, where she would pass the time with gossip and tears; Colonel Brandon, alone, to begin his journey back to Delaford.

The journey home took two days. Marianne endured it without strain, supported by her family’s constant care. Every comfort was given, every need anticipated. And in their attentiveness, each found reward—not only in her physical ease, but in the calmness that had returned to her spirit.

To Elinor, this calm was the most precious gift. She had watched her sister suffer for weeks—silent anguish, heartbreak too deep for words, a sorrow she could neither share nor soothe. Now, seeing Marianne composed, thoughtful, even cheerful at times, filled her with quiet joy. She believed this peace came not from forgetting, but from reflection—genuine, painful, but healing reflection.

As they neared Barton, however, the old emotions stirred again. The fields, the trees, the familiar paths—all brought memories. Marianne grew quiet, her gaze fixed out the window, her face turned away. When Elinor saw her weeping as she stepped from the carriage, she felt only tenderness. Such grief was natural, and in its restraint, noble.

Inside the cottage, Marianne looked around with a firmness in her eyes. She seemed determined to reclaim her life, to face every object, every memory, without flinching. She spoke little, but each word aimed at cheerfulness. Though a sigh sometimes escaped her, it was always followed by a smile.

That evening, she tried the piano. She sat before it, fingers hovering over the keys—but then paused. The music she picked up was an opera Willoughby had bought for her, one that held their favorite duets. On the cover, in his handwriting, was her name. She shook her head, set it aside, and closed the instrument. “I’ll practice much,” she said firmly, “from now on.”

The next morning, the signs of recovery were stronger still. Marianne spoke with more energy, her thoughts clearer. She spoke of Margaret’s return, of the joy of being reunited with her family, of walks, music, reading—of a future filled with purpose.

“I will rise at six,” she declared. “From then till dinner, I’ll divide my time between music and books. I’ve made a plan. I’ll study seriously. Our library is too familiar—I’ll go to the Park for better works. And I’ll borrow others from Colonel Brandon. Six hours a day—within a year, I’ll learn what I now feel I lack.”

Elinor admired the resolve. It was the same passionate spirit that once led Marianne into reckless indulgence, now directed toward self-improvement. Yet her smile faded when she remembered the letter from Willoughby—still unspoken. She feared telling Marianne the truth might shatter this fragile peace.

So she delayed. But the moment came sooner than she hoped.

After several days at home, the weather finally warmed. One soft, golden morning, Marianne, leaning on Elinor’s arm, was allowed to walk in the lane before the house. They moved slowly, cautiously, but as they passed the hill behind the cottage, Marianne stopped.

“There,” she said calmly, pointing. “On that mound—I fell. And there I first saw Willoughby.”

Her voice dropped, then rose again. “I’m glad I can look at it without pain. Shall we ever talk about him, Elinor? Or would it be wrong? I *can* talk now—I hope I can do so properly.”

Elinor encouraged her gently.

“Regret,” Marianne said, “is over. As far as he is concerned. I won’t tell you how I felt—I’ll tell you how I feel now. If I could believe he wasn’t always pretending… if I could think he wasn’t always deceiving me… above all, if I could know he wasn’t *that* wicked—after what happened to that poor girl—”

She stopped.

“If you could be certain of that,” Elinor said softly, “you’d find peace?”

“Yes. My peace depends on it. Not only because it’s terrible to suspect someone who meant so much to me of such things—but what would I become myself? How could I bear knowing I was so careless, so blind?”

“How, then,” Elinor asked, “would you explain his behavior?”

“I would suppose him… fickle. Very, very fickle.”

They walked in silence for a while.

“I don’t wish him too much good,” Marianne said at last, “when I pray his reflections aren’t worse than mine. He’ll suffer enough.”

“Do you compare your actions to his?” Elinor asked.

“No. I compare mine to what they should have been. To yours.”

“Our situations were different.”

“They were more alike than our conduct shows. Don’t let your kindness hide what your judgment sees. My illness made me reflect. Before I could speak, I was already thinking. I looked back—and saw nothing but foolishness, selfishness. I hurt myself. I hurt others. My own feelings caused my suffering. My weakness nearly killed me. I knew my illness was self-made. I neglected my health, even when I knew it was wrong. Had I died, it would have been suicide. I didn’t realize the danger until it was gone. And yet—I wonder at surviving. I wonder that my desire to live—to make amends—to give time to God and to you all—didn’t kill me right then.”

She paused.

“To die like that—what misery I would have left you, my nurse, my sister, my dearest friend! You who saw my bitterness, my complaints! How would I live in your memory? My mother—how could you console her? I cannot describe my shame. Everywhere I looked, I saw duty ignored, fault indulged. I repaid Mrs. Jennings’s kindness with contempt. I was rude to the Middletons, the Palmers, even strangers. I hardened my heart against their goodness. I gave John and Fanny less than they deserved. But you—you above all—were wronged by me. Only I knew your pain. Yet did it move me? No. Your example stood before me—but I did not follow. Was I kinder to you? Did I help with the duties you bore alone? No. Even when I knew you were unhappy, I avoided effort. I thought only of my own sorrow. I forgot you—my sister—whose love I claimed to value above all.”

Her voice broke. Elinor reached out, holding her hand tightly.

“You are so good,” Marianne whispered. “But I must prove it. I have a plan. If I can keep it—my feelings will be ruled, my temper improved. I will no longer harm others or torment myself. I will live for my family. You, Mother, Margaret—we shall be all the world to me. From you, I will draw my joy. I will not leave home unless necessity calls. And if I do go out, it will be to show I am humbled, changed—able to be polite, patient, generous.”

She paused.

“As for Willoughby—no, I cannot say I will forget him. That is impossible. His memory cannot be erased. But it will be governed. By reason. By faith. By work.”

She lowered her voice. “If only I knew his heart… everything would be easier.”

Elinor, who had been weighing whether to tell the truth, felt the moment had come. She began carefully, gently. She explained Willoughby’s apology—his reasons, his remorse—without exaggeration, without sentimentality. She spoke plainly, honestly, but softened his claims of lingering affection.

Marianne said nothing. She trembled. Her eyes stayed on the ground. Her lips turned white. Questions surged in her mind—but none came out. She listened, breathless, gripping her sister’s hand without knowing it. Tears streamed down her face.

Elinor, fearing fatigue, guided her home. Along the way, she spoke only of Willoughby—of what he had said, how he had looked, the tone of his voice—carefully detailing every moment, knowing Marianne needed to hear it all.

When they reached the cottage, Marianne kissed her sister’s cheek, whispered through her tears, “Tell Mama,” and slowly climbed the stairs.

Elinor did not follow. She respected her sister’s need for solitude. But her mind raced—what would Marianne say? Would she speak again? Would she understand? With quiet determination, she walked into the parlour, ready to fulfill her promise—and to wait, if necessary, for the next chapter of her sister’s healing.

Mrs. Dashwood listened with deep emotion as the truth about Willoughby was revealed. She was relieved to learn that some of the accusations against him were not entirely true—she felt sorrow for the man who had once been her daughter’s beloved, and she hoped he might find peace in the future. But no amount of explanation could undo the past. The trust between Marianne and Willoughby was gone, shattered beyond repair. His actions—especially toward Eliza Williams—had left wounds too deep to heal. No words could erase what Marianne had suffered, nor could they restore the purity of his character in her eyes. And so, despite any sympathy she might feel, Mrs. Dashwood knew that Willoughby could never regain Marianne’s love or threaten Colonel Brandon’s place in her heart.

Had Mrs. Dashwood heard Willoughby’s story directly from him—had she seen the pain in his face, felt the weight of his remorse—her compassion might have been stronger. But Elinor, though moved by the tale, had chosen not to dramatize it. Her own judgment, shaped by time and reflection, had cooled the passion of earlier belief. She wanted only to speak plainly, without embellishment, to give an honest account of what had happened—not to stir emotions that might mislead.

That evening, when the three women sat together, Marianne spoke of Willoughby again—though it was clear she struggled to do so. Her hands trembled, her voice faltered, and a flush crept across her cheeks. Yet she pressed on.

“I wish to assure you both,” she said, “that I understand everything—as fully as you could desire.”

Mrs. Dashwood reached out instinctively to comfort her, but Elinor silently stopped her with a glance. She wanted to hear her sister’s thoughts unfiltered, untouched by maternal tenderness.

Marianne continued slowly, “It is a relief—to know what Elinor told me this morning. I’ve heard exactly what I needed to hear.” For a moment, she paused, as if overwhelmed. Then, steadier than before, she added, “I am now perfectly satisfied. I want nothing to change. I could never have been happy with him—after knowing what I now know. I would have lacked confidence, respect… nothing could have erased that.”

“Oh, I know it!” Mrs. Dashwood exclaimed. “Happy with a man whose conduct has brought such pain? A man who harmed one of our dearest friends—a noble, gentle soul like Colonel Brandon? No, my dear Marianne—your tender heart would never have found joy in such a union. Your conscience would have known every wrong he committed, just as it should.”

Marianne sighed softly. “I wish for no change.”

Elinor stepped in gently. “You see the matter clearly—just as reason and integrity must lead one to see it. And I believe you can see, as I do, that marrying him would have brought endless troubles. His affection, even if real at first, was far from certain. And his lifestyle—he spends freely, even admitting it himself. Self-denial is foreign to him. With your innocence and a small income, such a marriage would have meant constant hardship. You would have tried to live frugally, to manage wisely—but how much could one woman do to stop ruin already set in motion?”

Her lips quivered. “Selfish?” she whispered, almost in disbelief.

“His entire behavior,” Elinor said quietly, “has been driven by selfishness. It began when he toyed with your affections. Then, when he truly loved you, he delayed telling you. And finally, he left Barton without a word—because it suited him. His own comfort, his own ease—those were always his priorities.”

“It is true,” Marianne murmured. “My happiness was never his concern.”

“And now,” Elinor continued, “he regrets what he’s done. But why? Because it hasn’t made him happy. He’s free of financial worries now, and he finds himself alone—yet he blames only the temper of his wife, not the choices that led him here. But does that mean he would have been happy with you? No. He would have faced poverty, constant strain. He’d have had a kind wife—no complaints there—but he’d have lived in want. And soon enough, he’d realize that a secure home, a good fortune, matters more to lasting contentment than even the most gentle disposition.”

“I believe it,” Marianne said. “And I have nothing to regret—only my own foolishness.”

“Rather say your mother’s imprudence,” Mrs. Dashwood replied gently. “She bears the blame.”

But Marianne would not let her go further. Elinor, seeing that both women recognized their own mistakes, chose not to dwell on the past. Instead, she returned to her original thought: “One truth stands out above all—the root of Willoughby’s downfall was his first sin: his treatment of Eliza Williams. That act poisoned everything after. Every mistake, every regret, stems from that beginning.”

Marianne nodded, deeply moved. And Mrs. Dashwood, stirred by the insight, turned to speak warmly of Colonel Brandon—his kindness, his patience, his quiet dignity. But Marianne seemed distant, lost in her own thoughts.

In the days that followed, Elinor noticed that Marianne did not improve as expected. Though she still tried to appear cheerful, her strength waned. Yet her spirit remained firm, and Elinor trusted that time would bring healing.

Margaret returned, and the family was reunited once more, settling back into the quiet rhythm of life at the cottage. Their studies were not yet as intense as before, but they planned to renew them soon.

But Elinor’s mind wandered often to Edward. Since leaving London, she had heard nothing of him—no word of his plans, no clue to where he might be. Only one letter from her brother mentioned him, briefly: “We know nothing of our unfortunate Edward, and can make no enquiries on so prohibited a subject, but conclude him to be still at Oxford.” That was all.

Then, one morning, the family’s servant returned from Exeter. After answering his mistress’s questions, he offered a startling piece of news:

“I suppose you know, ma'am, that Mr. Ferrars is married.”

Marianne gasped, her eyes flying to Elinor’s face. She saw her sister turn pale—and then collapsed into hysterics.

Mrs. Dashwood, who had glanced over at the same moment, saw the depth of Elinor’s distress. She was torn between the two daughters—one broken with grief, the other consumed by silent agony.

The servant, sensing trouble, called a maid. Together, they helped Marianne to another room. By then, she was slightly calmer. Mrs. Dashwood stayed with her, leaving Elinor to question Thomas.

“Who told you Mr. Ferrars was married?” she asked.

“I saw him myself this morning in Exeter—with his lady, Miss Steele,” he replied. “They were stopping at the New London Inn. I was going there with a message from Sally at the Park to her brother, who works as a post-boy. As I passed, I looked up and saw her—Miss Steele, I mean. I took off my hat, and she recognized me. She called out, asked after you, especially Miss Marianne, and said Mr. Ferrars and she sent their best wishes. They’re sorry they couldn’t come to visit, but they’re in a hurry to go farther down. They’ll return soon, and then they’ll definitely call.”

“Did she say she was married?” Elinor asked.

“Yes, ma'am. She smiled and said she’d changed her name since last being here. Always a pleasant girl, very polite. So I wished her joy.”

“Was Mr. Ferrars in the carriage?”

“Yes, ma'am. I saw him leaning back, but he didn’t look up. He never was much for talking.”

“Was anyone else with them?”

“No, ma'am—just the two.”

“Where did they come from?”

“They came straight from town,” Thomas said. “Mrs. Ferrars told me.”

“And are they going westward?”

“Yes, ma'am—but not staying long. They’ll be back soon, and then they’ll surely visit.”

Mrs. Dashwood looked at Elinor. But Elinor knew better than to hope. She recognized Lucy’s voice in every detail—the careful wording, the false cheer. She was confident Edward would never come near them. In a low voice, she told her mother, “They’re likely going to Mr. Pratt’s near Plymouth.”

Thomas had finished speaking. Elinor looked as if she wanted more.

“Did you see them leave?” she asked.

“No, ma'am. The horses were coming out, but I had to hurry back—I didn’t want to be late.”

“Did Mrs. Ferrars look well?”

“Yes, ma'am. She said she was fine. And to my mind, she’s always been a handsome young lady. She seemed very happy.”

Mrs. Dashwood had no more questions. Thomas was dismissed, and the table cleared.

Marianne had already sent word she wouldn’t eat. Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor had no appetite either. Margaret, watching them, felt fortunate that, despite all the recent trials, she had never been forced to skip dinner before.

After dessert and wine were arranged, the two older sisters sat in silence, each lost in thought. Mrs. Dashwood dared not speak. She realized now that she had misunderstood Elinor’s feelings. She had believed her daughter’s attachment to Edward was weaker—because Elinor had kept her pain hidden, guarded by duty and compassion for Marianne. Now she understood: Elinor had suffered deeply, quietly, with greater courage than she had ever imagined.

And Mrs. Dashwood felt a pang of guilt. She had focused so completely on Marianne’s sorrow that she had neglected her other daughter—whose heart had been breaking just as much, though in silence.

Elinor now understood the true weight of certainty—how different it was from mere expectation, however firmly one might believe in an outcome. She had always allowed herself a quiet hope, even as she told herself it was impossible, that Edward would never marry Lucy. Perhaps he would change his mind. Perhaps friends would intervene. Maybe some better opportunity for her to secure a future would arise—something that would spare them all this pain. But now, the truth was undeniable: Edward was married. And with that knowledge came a sharp pang of regret for the secret wish she’d nurtured, a flattery of her own heart that only made the blow more painful.

She was startled at first by the news that they had married so soon—before Edward could be ordained, before he could take possession of the living. But then she saw how natural it must have seemed to Lucy, who had long been driven by caution and haste, determined to claim him before time slipped away. They had wed in London, and were already on their way to her uncle’s house. How Edward must have felt, riding within four miles of Barton, seeing the familiar face of her mother’s servant, hearing Lucy’s message—she could not imagine. Soon enough, they would settle at Delaford. Delaford—the place where so much of her past lingered, where memories intertwined with longing. She longed to know it, yet dreaded being near it. In her mind’s eye, she saw them already in the parsonage: Lucy, brisk and clever, managing every detail with careful frugality, pretending to be carefree while secretly saving every penny; charming Colonel Brandon, winning over Mrs. Jennings, courting favor wherever she could. And Edward—what did she see in him? Nothing clear. No joy, no sorrow—only a blankness that hurt her more than any emotion might have. She turned away, unable to bear the thought of him.

She hoped someone in London would write soon, to confirm the news and tell more. But day after day passed without word. Though she knew no one was necessarily at fault, she found herself blaming each absent friend—careless, indifferent, slow to act. “When do you write to Colonel Brandon, ma'am?” she asked one evening, her voice tinged with impatience.

“I wrote last week, my dear,” Mrs. Dashwood replied, “and I expect to see him rather than hear from him again. I urged him strongly to come here. He may walk in today—or tomorrow—or any day.”

That was something—a reason to wait, to anticipate. Colonel Brandon _must_ know what had happened. She had scarcely formed the thought when a rider appeared on horseback, drawing her gaze to the window. He stopped at the gate. A gentleman—tall, dark-haired—dismounted. It wasn’t Colonel Brandon. Not in stature or manner. Could it be—Edward? Her breath caught. She looked again. Yes—it was him.

She rose quickly, stepped back, and sat down. “He’s come from Mr. Pratt’s just to see us,” she whispered to herself. “I will be calm. I will be composed.”

But her family had seen too. She watched her mother and Marianne flush, exchange glances, whisper urgently. She longed to speak—to assure them that no coldness, no distance, should show in their welcome—but she couldn’t. She had no words. All she could do was sit still and let silence carry the moment.

They waited. The gravel path echoed with his footsteps. Then he stood in the hall, and next moment, he was before them. His face was pale, agitated—fearful of how he would be received, aware he deserved little kindness.

Mrs. Dashwood, guided by her love for Elinor and her desire to follow her lead, met him with a smile that strained to look warm. She offered her hand and said, “Welcome, Edward. We are so glad to see you.”

He blushed, stammered, and muttered something unintelligible. Elinor’s lips had moved with hers, but once the gesture was done, she wished she had touched him too. Too late. She sat back, forced a light tone, and began talking about the weather.

Marianne had withdrawn into the shadows, hiding her turmoil. Margaret, sensing only part of the truth, chose dignity—she sat far from him, silent, rigid.

When Elinor ran out of things to say about the season, an awkward silence settled over the room. Mrs. Dashwood broke it with a hesitant question: “How is Mrs. Ferrars?”

He answered quickly, “Very well, thank you.”

Another pause. Elinor gathered her courage. “Is Mrs. Ferrars at Longstaple?”

“At Longstaple?” he repeated, surprised. “No—my mother is in town.”

“I meant,” Elinor said, picking up her embroidery, “to ask about Mrs. _Edward_ Ferrars.”

She dared not lift her eyes. But both her mother and Marianne turned sharply toward him. He flushed, hesitated, looked uncertain. After a long silence, he said, “Perhaps you mean—my brother. You mean Mrs.—Mrs. _Robert_ Ferrars.”

“Mrs. Robert Ferrars!” exclaimed both women, stunned.

Even Elinor, though she remained silent, fixed her gaze on him—her eyes wide with astonishment.

He rose, walked to the window, picked up a pair of scissors, and began cutting the leather sheath to pieces as he spoke, his voice rushed and unsteady. “You may not know… you may not have heard… my brother is recently married—to Miss Lucy Steele. The youngest.”

The room fell into silence, broken only by the sound of the scissors tearing through leather. Everyone stared—except Elinor, who sat hunched over her work, trembling, overwhelmed.

“Yes,” he continued, “they were married last week. They’re now at Dawlish.”

Elinor could stay no longer. She rose, hurried from the room, and once the door closed behind her, she sank to her knees, overcome by tears—tears of relief, of joy, of release.

Edward, who had avoided looking at her until now, noticed her sudden departure. He saw her distress—or perhaps heard her sobs. From that moment, he fell into deep silence, lost in thought. No words, no questions, no affectionate greeting from Mrs. Dashwood could reach him. At last, without speaking, he left the room and walked toward the village, leaving the others bewildered, puzzled by a transformation so sudden and profound—one they could not begin to understand.

Edward’s freedom, though it seemed miraculous to everyone else, was now a fact—undeniable and complete. And what would become of that freedom? The answer was clear to all: after having endured the pain of one ill-advised engagement—entered into without his mother’s consent and lasting over four years—it was only natural that, once that bond was broken, he would rush into another. His purpose in coming to Barton was simple: to ask Elinor to marry him. Given that he had already been through such an ordeal before, one might have expected him to be more composed. Yet here he was, agitated, breathless, needing fresh air and reassurance as if he were making his first confession of love.

But soon enough, the moment came. He found the courage, seized the opportunity, spoke his heart, and received her answer. By the time they sat down to dinner at four o’clock—just three hours after his arrival—he had not only won Elinor’s hand but also secured her mother’s blessing. He was no longer just a hopeful lover; he was, in truth, one of the happiest men alive.

His joy was deeper than mere triumph. It wasn’t simply the joy of being loved in return—it was the relief of being freed from a past that had haunted him for years. He was released from an engagement with a woman he no longer cared for, from a promise that had brought him nothing but sorrow. Now, he stood before Elinor—not merely as a man who had found love, but as one who had escaped misery and stepped into peace.

And this transformation showed in everything he said. His voice was light, his manner open, his gratitude sincere. For the first time, he spoke freely to Elinor—not hiding his faults, not pretending to be perfect. He confessed his early attachment to Lucy with honesty and maturity.

“It was foolish,” he admitted, “a childish fancy born of ignorance and idleness. Had I been given some profession when I left Mr. Pratt’s care at eighteen, I believe I never would have fallen into it. At the time, I thought my feelings for Lucy were unshakable—but I had seen so little of the world, and so few women, that I couldn’t judge. She was kind, she was pretty—I thought so then—and I had no one else to turn to. I spent most of my time at Longstaple, where I felt welcome and comfortable. And so, gradually, I became attached. But looking back, I can see how easily it could have passed, had I had something else to occupy me.”

He paused, then added softly, “I don’t regret it entirely. It taught me much about myself—about how easily affection can be mistaken for love. But I do regret letting it go on so long.”

Elinor listened, her heart swelling with tenderness. The change in the Dashwood household was immense. Mrs. Dashwood, overwhelmed with joy, could barely contain herself. She praised Edward, adored Elinor, and struggled to balance her happiness with her desire not to embarrass them. Marianne, quieter, wept—not out of sadness, but because her own emotions were too deep to express. Her joy was real, but it carried the weight of loss, of unspoken comparisons. Only Elinor remained silent—though not from calmness, but from being utterly undone by her own happiness.

From the moment she learned Lucy was married to Robert, to the moment Edward stood before her, declaring his love, her mind had raced with hope, fear, and disbelief. But once it was done—once every doubt vanished, once she saw Edward free and devoted—she was overwhelmed. She could not breathe, could not think. Her heart was full, and it took hours before she could find any measure of stillness.

Edward stayed at the cottage for at least a week—longer than anyone expected. He had no intention of leaving before he had spoken every word he needed to say, relived every memory, and laid bare every dream. With lovers, silence is never final. Every topic must be revisited, every emotion re-expressed.

Their conversation turned quickly to Lucy’s marriage—the event that had changed everything. To Elinor, it was astonishing, almost absurd. How could Robert, whom she knew to be indifferent to Lucy’s beauty, fall so completely for her? How could he marry her when she was already engaged to his brother? And why did his family punish Edward for loving her, yet accept Robert’s choice?

She shook her head. “It makes no sense.”

Edward offered a theory. “Perhaps,” he said, “it began with flattery. Lucy may have played on Robert’s vanity, and Robert on her ambition. She might have hoped he could help me—then, when that failed, she looked elsewhere.”

Elinor recalled Robert’s words in Harley Street—how he believed he could have influenced Edward’s fate had he intervened sooner. “That,” she said, “was exactly like Robert. He may have thought that way from the start. And Lucy may have begun with the idea of gaining his support for you—only to realize later that she wanted him for herself.”

They both wondered how long it had gone on. Edward had been at Oxford, far removed from London life, relying only on Lucy’s letters. Until the very end, those letters were warm, regular, loving. There was no hint of betrayal—no warning.

Then came the letter from Lucy.

> *“Dear Sir,*
> *Being very sure I have long lost your affections, I have thought myself at liberty to bestow my own on another, and have no doubt of being as happy with him as I once used to think I might be with you. But I scorn to accept a hand while the heart was another’s. I sincerely wish you happy in your choice, and it shall not be my fault if we are not always good friends, as our near relationship now makes proper.*
> *I can safely say I owe you no ill-will, and am sure you will be too generous to do us any ill offices. Your brother has gained my affections entirely, and as we could not live without one another, we are just returned from the altar, and are now on our way to Dawlish for a few weeks—which place your dear brother has great curiosity to see. But I thought I would first trouble you with these few lines, and shall always remain,*
> *Your sincere well-wisher, friend, and sister,*
> *Lucy Ferrars.*
> *I have burnt all your letters, and will return your picture the first opportunity. Please to destroy my scrawls—but the ring with my hair you are very welcome to keep.”*

Elinor read it quietly, then handed it back.

“I won’t ask your opinion of it as writing,” Edward said. “I’d rather not have you seen any of her letters before. As a sister, it’s bad enough. As a wife—how I blushed over her pages! This is the only letter I’ve ever received from her that made up for its style.”

“And yet,” Elinor said gently, “you once believed her honest.”

“I did,” he admitted. “I thought her affection genuine. I didn’t know her true nature until now. She meant to deceive. She sent Thomas with that message to make me suffer—deliberately.”

Elinor nodded. “She had no regard for you. She used you. And she knew it.”

Edward sighed. “I kept believing she was kind-hearted. I told myself she was only ignorant, poorly educated. But now I see it clearly—her actions were selfish, calculated. She didn’t care for me at all.”

“Yet you never broke off the engagement,” Elinor said.

“I couldn’t,” he replied. “When my mother disowned me, I had no support. She insisted on standing by me—offering to share my fate. How could I think she was doing it for money or status? I believed it was love. And even now, I don’t understand why she would stay bound to a man she didn’t love, with only two thousand pounds to his name.”

“She may have hoped something better would come,” Elinor said. “Or she may have known that staying engaged gave her status. It was respectable. And if nothing better came along, marrying you was better than being alone.”

Edward nodded slowly. “Of course. It was natural. I should have seen it.”

Elinor smiled faintly. “You should have stayed away from Norland. You knew your heart wasn’t steady.”

“I thought,” he said, “that my engagement would protect me. That my faithfulness to Lucy would keep my feelings safe. I admired you—but I told myself it was only friendship. I didn’t compare you until I began to wonder… and then I couldn’t stop.”

“You were wrong,” Elinor said. “But I forgive you.”

He looked at her, eyes soft. “I’m glad.”

They talked of Colonel Brandon, whom Edward now wished to meet. He wanted to thank him properly—for the living of Delaford, which had changed everything. “I never thanked him right,” he said. “My thanks were cold, even grudging. He must think I’ve never forgiven him.”

“And now?” Elinor asked.

“Now I’m grateful. And I want to tell him so.”

He had heard little about Delaford—his knowledge of the estate came entirely from Elinor, who had listened attentively to Colonel Brandon’s descriptions. She knew the land, the tithes, the garden, the house—better than he did.

Only one matter remained unresolved: their future. They loved each other deeply, had the approval of their families, and understood each other perfectly. But they had no real income. Edward had two thousand pounds. Elinor had one. The living at Delaford provided thirty-five hundred pounds a year—but neither of them could count on Mrs. Dashwood’s help. Three hundred and fifty pounds a year wouldn’t sustain them comfortably.

Edward still hoped his mother might soften toward him. Perhaps she would restore his inheritance. But Elinor feared otherwise. Since Robert had married Lucy, and since Mrs. Ferrars had praised Edward’s choice of her as “only a lesser evil,” she suspected Robert’s offense would only benefit Fanny.

Four days after Edward’s arrival, Colonel Brandon arrived. Mrs. Dashwood was delighted—so much company in her small cottage was a rare treat. Edward, as the first guest, kept his room, while Brandon walked to the Park each night and returned in the morning, often arriving just in time to interrupt their quiet mornings.

Brandon, after three weeks at Delaford, was weary—his thoughts consumed by the age difference between himself and Marianne. But among friends, and under the warmth of their welcome, he began to recover. He knew nothing of Lucy’s marriage. When Mrs. Dashwood told him, he was astonished—but also pleased. He saw that his act of kindness had led to Elinor’s happiness.

The two men grew closer with each passing day. Their shared values, their similar temperaments, their mutual respect for each other’s sisters—these things drew them together naturally. A friendship formed quickly, rooted in sincerity and common ground.

Letters from town arrived, once filled with excitement for Elinor—now read with amusement. Mrs. Jennings wrote with outrage: “Lucy’s betrayal was monstrous! Poor Edward—broken-hearted at Oxford! I can’t bear to think of him. You must bring him to Barton and let Miss Marianne comfort him.”

Mr. Dashwood’s tone was solemn. “Mrs. Ferrars is devastated. Fanny suffers terribly. Robert’s crime is unforgivable—but Lucy’s is worse. Neither shall ever be mentioned again. If Mrs. Ferrars forgives Robert, his wife shall never be recognized.”

He added: “Edward has not written. Perhaps he fears offending. I shall write to Oxford, suggesting a letter of submission—perhaps addressed to Fanny, to be shown to her mother.”

Edward frowned. “A letter of submission? Beg my mother’s pardon for Robert’s ingratitude to her—and for her breach of honor to me? No. I cannot submit. I am not penitent. I am happy.”

“But you could ask forgiveness,” Elinor said gently. “You did offend. And you might express regret for entering into that first engagement.”

He agreed. “Yes. But not a letter of submission. I’d prefer to speak to Fanny in person.”

So it was decided: instead of writing, Edward would go to London and plead his case directly.

Marianne, now more thoughtful, remarked, “If John and Fanny truly try to reconcile you with your mother, perhaps they’re not so bad after all.”

After a brief visit, the two gentlemen left Barton together. They went first to Delaford—so Edward could see his future home, and assist Colonel Brandon in planning improvements. From there, he would travel to London, ready to face his family—not as a beggar, but as a man who had found love, freedom, and peace.

CHAPTER L. After a brief but firm resistance—just enough to preserve her reputation for propriety, yet not so strong as to appear unkind—Mrs. Ferrars allowed Edward back into her presence and declared him once more her son. Her family had been in constant flux lately. For many years, she had two sons; then, when Edward was deemed lost and disgraced, she lost one. Robert’s own downfall left her without any son for nearly two weeks. Now, with Edward restored, she had one again. Yet despite this reconciliation, Edward remained uneasy. He feared that revealing his engagement to Miss Dashwood might provoke another fatal turn in his mother’s temper—and perhaps hasten his end as swiftly as before. So he disclosed the news with careful hesitation, expecting outrage. But to his surprise, Mrs. Ferrars listened with an unnerving calm.

She did, at first, try to dissuade him—using every argument at her disposal. She reminded him of Miss Morton, whose rank and fortune far surpassed those of Elinor: a nobleman’s daughter with thirty thousand pounds, while Elinor was merely the daughter of a private gentleman with no more than three. But when she saw that Edward acknowledged the truth of her words yet remained resolute, she wisely decided—based on past experience—to yield. After a delay long enough to maintain her dignity and avoid any appearance of favoritism, she finally gave her consent to the marriage.

Next came the question of financial support. Here, it became clear that though Edward was now her only surviving son, he was still not her favorite. Robert had been assured of a thousand pounds a year; Edward, however, was expected to take holy orders for the sake of two hundred and fifty pounds at most. No additional sum was promised—beyond the ten thousand pounds already given with Fanny. Still, this amount was more than Edward and Elinor had hoped for, and they accepted it gratefully. Mrs. Ferrars herself seemed astonished by her own restraint, offering only half-hearted excuses for not giving more.

With their income secured, all that remained was to wait for the completion of the Parsonage at Delaford—where Colonel Brandon, eager to make the house suitable for Elinor, had taken upon himself to oversee extensive improvements. As usual, delays plagued the workmen, and promises were broken. But just as always, Elinor could not resist the temptation to marry before everything was perfectly ready. The wedding took place early in autumn at Barton church, under the eyes of friends and family.

The first month of their married life was spent at the Mansion-house, where they could personally supervise the finishing touches on their new home. They chose wallpapers, planned shrubberies, and even designed a sweeping driveway. Mrs. Jennings’s predictions—though tangled and haphazard—proved mostly true. By Michaelmas, she visited them at the Parsonage and declared, with genuine conviction, that she had never seen a happier couple.

Indeed, Edward and Elinor had little to desire. Their only wishes were for Colonel Brandon and Marianne to marry, and for better grazing land for their cows. They were welcomed by nearly all their relations and friends. Mrs. Ferrars came to inspect the happiness she had reluctantly permitted—though she treated them with only polite affection, never granting them real favor. That privilege belonged to Robert and Lucy, whose cunning and humility had won her over.

Lucy’s conduct throughout the affair was a masterclass in self-interest. At first, Robert had approached her simply to break off Edward’s engagement—believing it would be easy. But Lucy, instead of yielding, drew him deeper into her web. Each visit bred new doubts, each conversation required another meeting. Soon, they spoke less of Edward and more of Robert—whose charm and ambition captivated her completely. Before long, Robert had replaced his brother entirely in her affections.

Proud of his triumph, Robert married Lucy privately, defying his mother’s authority. They enjoyed several months of blissful seclusion at Dawlish, where Lucy reconnected with old acquaintances and Robert sketched grand plans for cottages. When they returned to town, Lucy urged Robert to beg forgiveness from Mrs. Ferrars—a simple act that worked wonders. At first, the pardon applied only to Robert. Lucy, having owed no duty to Mrs. Ferrars, remained unforgiven for weeks. But through persistent humility, constant apologies, and exaggerated gratitude, she gradually earned her attention—and eventually, her favor.

Soon, Lucy became indispensable to Mrs. Ferrars, as essential as Robert or Fanny. While Edward was never truly forgiven for once intending to marry her, and Elinor was regarded as an outsider despite her superior station, Lucy was openly cherished as a beloved child.

They settled in London, supported generously by Mrs. Ferrars, and lived in harmony with the Dashwoods—except for the simmering jealousy between Fanny and Lucy, and the occasional quarrels between Robert and his wife. Otherwise, peace reigned.

Edward’s fall from grace and Robert’s rise were mysterious to many. But the results justified the arrangement: Robert showed no signs of regret over his modest inheritance, nor did Edward seem discontented. On the contrary, Edward fulfilled his duties faithfully, loved his wife deeply, and lived with quiet joy. His contentment was evident in every aspect of his life.

Elinor’s marriage kept her closely tied to her family. Her mother and sisters spent more than half their time at Delaford. Mrs. Dashwood made frequent visits not only for pleasure, but also out of hope—her greatest wish being to unite Marianne with Colonel Brandon. It was now her dearest aim. Though she treasured her daughter’s company, she longed even more to see Marianne settled at the mansion-house. Edward and Elin: both felt the depth of Colonel Brandon’s grief and their own debt to him. And so, by mutual agreement, Marianne was to be his reward.

Surrounded by such affection, guided by such understanding, what could Marianne do? She was born for an extraordinary destiny. She was meant to discover the flaws in her own beliefs, to act against her deepest convictions, and to love someone else—not out of passion, but out of duty and compassion.

At nineteen, she found herself surrendering to new feelings, embracing new responsibilities, stepping into a new home. She became a wife, a mistress of a household, and a patroness of a village. Colonel Brandon, in turn, found in her the comfort he had long sought. His sorrows were eased by her regard; her presence brought light back into his life. And Marianne, in loving him fully, found her own fulfillment in making him happy.

Marianne never loved halfway. Her heart, once devoted entirely to Willoughby, now gave itself completely to Colonel Brandon.

Willoughby, upon hearing of her marriage, felt a pang—but soon after, he received the final blow: Mrs. Smith forgave him, citing his marriage to a woman of character as the reason. This revelation taught him that had he treated Marianne with honor, he might have had both happiness and wealth. His remorse was sincere. He envied Colonel Brandon, and grieved for Marianne. But he did not remain broken. He did not flee society or dwell in perpetual sorrow. He lived. He hunted, bred horses, raised dogs, and found joy in the pleasures of life. He remained fond of Marianne, always regarding her as the ideal woman—no other beauty ever measured up in his eyes.

Mrs. Dashwood, wise and practical, stayed at the cottage rather than move to Delaford. Fortunately for Sir John and Mrs. Jennings, Margaret had grown old enough to dance—and was now considered quite eligible for romance.

Between Barton and Delaford, there was a steady flow of affection and companionship—natural to close-knit families. Among the many blessings of Elinor and Marianne’s lives, let it not be forgotten that though they were sisters, living almost within sight of one another, they lived together in perfect harmony—without conflict between themselves, or discord between their husbands.
